---

## Post-Training Quantization - Weight-Only

These methods quantize only the weight tensors; activations remain in FP16/BF16. This avoids the challenge of activation outliers and is sufficient for memory-bound inference (most single-GPU LLM serving). The dominant category for open-weight model releases.

<a id="drop-by-drop"></a>
### Drop-by-Drop · W3/W4/W5 A16 (one checkpoint, bit-width selectable at inference) · PTQ W-only · 2026-06

<img src="../assets/diagrams/drop-by-drop.svg" width="640" alt="Drop-by-Drop diagram">
<p><em>Weights are grouped and encoded as a sum of vectors from an ordered stack of learned codebooks; Matryoshka-style supervision makes each codebook prefix a valid lower-bit reconstruction, so dropping trailing codebooks at inference selects a lower precision from one checkpoint.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights] --> B[Calibration data:\nyes]
    B --> C[Compute scale\nand zero-point]
    C --> D[Quantize weights\nto W3/W4/W5 A16 one checkpoint, bit-width selectable at inference]
    D --> E[Outlier handling:\nadditive multi-codebook repres]
    E --> F[Quantized model\nW3/W4/W5 A16 one checkpoint, bit-width selectable at inference]
```

> Drop-by-Drop trains one quantized copy of an LLM's weights that can be run at several different bit-widths without re-quantizing. Weights are grouped (8 at a time) and reconstructed as a sum of vectors pulled from an ordered stack of learned codebooks, where each codebook adds roughly one bit. During training the codebooks are supervised so that the first few alone already give a usable reconstruction, the way nesting works in Matryoshka representations, so you can keep or drop trailing codebooks at inference to trade quality for memory. This means a single stored checkpoint covers 3-, 4-, and 5-bit operating points, and you pick the precision at load time based on the hardware budget.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2606.12876](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.12876) |
| Precision | W3/W4/W5 A16 (one checkpoint, bit-width selectable at inference) |
| Granularity | per 8-weight group; M additive codebooks of 2^8 entries each, M in {3,4,5} giving 3/4/5 bits per weight |
| Calibration | 1024 calibration samples; AQLM-style codebook training (residual K-means init, beam search over codes + gradient descent on codebooks) |
| Symmetric? | n/a (codebook-based) |
| Outlier handling | additive multi-codebook representation absorbs all weight magnitudes (no separate outlier path) |
| Hardware target | GPU; targets heterogeneous deployment where a single checkpoint serves multiple bit-widths |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | Competitive WikiText-2/C4 perplexity and zero-shot accuracy (ARC-e/c, HellaSwag, Winogrande, PIQA) on Qwen-7B, LLaMA-8B, Gemma-2B, Mistral-7B; e.g. Qwen-7B ~72-74% mean zero-shot accuracy across the 3/4/5-codebook (3/4/5-bit) settings, with graceful degradation as codebooks are dropped |
| Builds on | [aqlm](#aqlm) |
| Related | [gptq](#gptq) · [quip-sharp](#quip-sharp) · [qtip](#qtip) |

**How it works:**

The method casts weight quantization as successive refinement: assuming weights are roughly Gaussian, an additive codebook code can be reconstructed with increasing fidelity as more codebooks (bits) are added under a weighted-MSE distortion tied to the LLM loss. Building on AQLM's additive-quantization pipeline, it injects Matryoshka-style supervision into the codebook-update objective so that ordered prefixes of the codebook set each minimize loss at their own bit-width. The result is nested, 'droppable' codebooks: at inference any prefix of length M yields a valid M-bit model, letting one trained checkpoint serve 3/4/5-bit precisions on demand.

---

<a id="liftquant"></a>
### LiftQuant · W2.4 A16 · PTQ W-only · 2026-06

<img src="../assets/diagrams/liftquant.svg" width="640" alt="LiftQuant diagram">
<p><em>LiftQuant: lift weights into a higher-dimensional 1-bit lattice, project back, and set average bit-width by the lift-to-original dimension ratio.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights] --> B[Calibration data:\nyes]
    B --> C[Compute scale\nand zero-point]
    C --> D[Quantize weights\nto W2.4 A16]
    D --> E[Outlier handling:\nlinear lifting/projection tran]
    E --> F[Quantized model\nW2.4 A16]
```

> LiftQuant is a post-training, weights-only quantization method that lets you pick almost any target average bit-width instead of being locked to whole numbers like 2, 3, or 4 bits. It represents each low-dimensional block of weights by projecting a simple 1-bit lattice down from a higher-dimensional 'lifted' space, so the bit budget is just the ratio between the lifted dimension and the original dimension and can be dialed in finely. A small calibration set drives an intra-block correction step that compensates for the projection error. This makes it possible to size a model exactly to a memory budget, for example squeezing a 70B model into 2.4 bits so it fits in a 24GB GPU.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICML 2026** |
| Paper | [2606.04050](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04050) |
| Code | [Heliulu/LiftQuant](https://github.com/Heliulu/LiftQuant) |
| Precision | W2.4 A16 |
| Granularity | per-group vector quantization with lifted 1-bit lattice codebook |
| Calibration | calibration set (e.g. RedPajama, ~4096 samples) for intra-block correction |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | linear lifting/projection transform plus intra-block error correction over the calibration set |
| Hardware target | GPU; decode via linear transforms and 1-bit uniform quantizers (e.g. fitting a 70B model into a 24GB GPU) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | 70B LLM compressed to 2.4 bits to fit a 24GB GPU, reported to surpass prior state-of-the-art 2-bit models on the same device; released checkpoints include Llama-2 and Qwen models at 2.5-bit and 3-bit |
| Builds on | [aqlm](#aqlm) · [quip-sharp](#quip-sharp) · [qtip](#qtip) |
| Related | [qtip](#qtip) · [aqlm](#aqlm) · [exl2](#exl2) · [quip-sharp](#quip-sharp) |

**How it works:**

Each group of original weights is approximated as the projection of points on a fixed 1-bit (binary) lattice that lives in a higher-dimensional lifted space; lifting then projecting back yields a structured but non-uniform codebook. Because the effective bit-width equals the ratio of the lifted dimension to the original dimension, varying that ratio gives quasi- continuous control over the average bits per weight rather than only integer levels. Decoding stays cheap since it only requires linear transformations and 1-bit uniform quantizers, and an intra-block correction pass over calibration data reduces the reconstruction error from projection.

---

<a id="harp"></a>
### HARP · W2/W3/W4 A16 · PTQ W-only · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/harp.svg" width="640" alt="HARP diagram">
<p><em>Learned butterfly block-orthogonal rotation (initialized from a randomized Hadamard transform) adapts the incoherence basis per layer for 2-4 bit weight-only PTQ.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights] --> B[Calibration data:\nyes]
    B --> C[Compute scale\nand zero-point]
    C --> D[Quantize weights\nto W2/W3/W4 A16]
    D --> E[Outlier handling:\nlearned structured butterfly ]
    E --> F[Quantized model\nW2/W3/W4 A16]
```

> HARP targets the rotation step used by incoherence-based weight-only quantizers. Methods like QuIP# and QTIP multiply weights and inputs by a fixed randomized Hadamard transform to smear out outliers before low-bit quantization, but that fixed transform cannot adjust to a particular layer, calibration distribution, or quantizer. HARP instead learns the orthogonal transform while keeping it cheap to apply: it builds each rotation from a chain of sparse butterfly-style block-orthogonal stages, can handle dimensions that are not powers of two through a mixed-radix schedule, and starts from the randomized Hadamard transform so it never does worse than the fixed baseline. The transform is fit on calibration data only (no end-to-end retraining) and the original full-precision computation is preserved exactly, so it slots into existing 2-4 bit pipelines and reportedly improves perplexity and zero-shot accuracy from 1B to 70B models while staying faster than FP16 at inference.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.29843](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29843) |
| Precision | W2/W3/W4 A16 |
| Granularity | per-group after learned two-sided rotation |
| Calibration | calibration set (rotation fitted on calibration data only) |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | learned structured (butterfly block-orthogonal) rotation that adapts the incoherence basis per layer/quantizer, initialized from a randomized Hadamard transform |
| Hardware target | GPU (structured sparse butterfly rotation kept fast; ~128 tok/s vs 61 tok/s FP16) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | Improves perplexity and zero-shot accuracy over fixed randomized Hadamard transforms across 2-4 bit settings on models from 1B to 70B parameters (exact numbers not given in abstract) |
| Builds on | [quip-sharp](#quip-sharp) · [qtip](#qtip) · [quip](#quip) |
| Related | [quarot](#quarot) · [spinquant](#spinquant) · [flatquant](#flatquant) · [aqlm](#aqlm) |

**How it works:**

HARP replaces the fixed randomized Hadamard transform (RHT) used in incoherence processing with a learnable two-sided structured orthogonal operator. Each rotation is parameterized as a product of sparse butterfly-like block-orthogonal stages, which keeps the cost near O(n log n) like a Hadamard transform while giving the transform enough free parameters to adapt the rotated basis to each layer, the calibration distribution, and the quantizer. Mixed- radix schedules let it operate on non-power-of-two dimensions, and initializing to the RHT (up to a fixed permutation) guarantees it starts at the fixed-Hadamard baseline and only improves from there. The processor is fit using only calibration data while exactly preserving the full-precision linear map, so quantized models keep their original outputs up to quantization error.

---

<a id="lfq"></a>
### LFQ · W4A16 / W3A16 · PTQ W-only · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/lfq.svg" width="640" alt="LFQ diagram">
<p><em>LFQ: standard MSE block reconstruction for early blocks, then logit-aligned (cross-entropy) joint optimization of the final block plus the LM head.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights] --> B[Calibration data:\nyes]
    B --> C[Compute scale\nand zero-point]
    C --> D[Quantize weights\nto W4A16 / W3A16]
    D --> E[Outlier handling:\nno explicit outlier mechanism;]
    E --> F[Quantized model\nW4A16 / W3A16]
```

> LFQ is an add-on for block-wise weight-only post-training quantization that targets the quality drop low-bit LLMs suffer on open-ended generation and long chain-of-thought tasks. The authors observe that standard block reconstruction has two blind spots: it never optimizes the final unembedding (LM head) layer, and it uses a mean-squared-error objective that does not directly preserve the model's output token distribution. LFQ fixes this by treating the last transformer block together with the LM head as one unit and tuning its quantized weights to match the full-precision logits via a cross-entropy objective instead of MSE. The change keeps perplexity and multiple-choice accuracy roughly unchanged while recovering accuracy on generation-heavy benchmarks.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICML 2026** |
| Paper | [2605.29756](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29756) |
| Precision | W4A16 / W3A16 |
| Granularity | per-channel (4-bit) and group-wise group size 128 (3-bit) |
| Calibration | C4 samples (~400-800 sequences of length 2048-4096), single-GPU |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | no explicit outlier mechanism; aligns output token distributions instead |
| Hardware target | GPU (weight-only low-bit inference) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct W4 per-channel: WikiText2 ppl FP 6.85 -> FlexRound+LFQ 7.21; IFEval FP 70.79 -> FlexRound+LFQ 71.35; MATH500 FP 74.2 -> FlexRound+LFQ 73.4 |
| Builds on | [omniquant](#omniquant) · [efficientqat](#efficientqat) |
| Related | [gptq](#gptq) · [autoround](#autoround) |

**How it works:**

For all transformer blocks except the last, LFQ keeps the usual per-block MSE reconstruction used by the chosen base method (FlexRound, OmniQuant, or Block-AP). For the final block it switches the objective: the quantized final block and the (also quantized) LM head are jointly optimized by minimizing the cross-entropy / KL divergence between the full-precision and quantized output token probability distributions over calibration sequences. This pulls the unembedding layer into the optimization, which block-wise PTQ normally omits, and aligns the soft output distribution rather than intermediate activations, which matters most for autoregressive generation where small per-token probability errors compound over long outputs.

---

<a id="windquant"></a>
### WINDQuant · W~2 (mixed, effective 1.96-2.02) A16 · PTQ W-only · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/windquant.svg" width="640" alt="WINDQuant diagram">
<p><em>WINDQuant: a PPO controller assigns per-column-chunk bit-widths under a global storage budget, giving sensitive chunks more bits and robust chunks fewer at ~2-bit average.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights] --> B[Calibration data:\nyes]
    B --> C[Compute scale\nand zero-point]
    C --> D[Quantize weights\nto Wapprox 2 mixed, effective 1.96-2.02 A16]
    D --> E[Outlier handling:\nRL controller assigns higher b]
    E --> F[Quantized model\nWapprox 2 mixed, effective 1.96-2.02 A16]
```

> WINDQuant frames ultra-low-bit weight quantization as a budget-constrained allocation problem and solves it with a reinforcement-learning controller. Instead of designing a new low-level quantization operator, it learns which bit-widths and quantization treatments to give to fine-grained column chunks of each weight matrix, subject to a single global storage budget. The controller is trained with PPO using activation-aware calibration, and each chosen chunk is fit with a lightweight per-unit quantizer. This lets precision vary inside a layer so that more sensitive chunks keep more bits while robust chunks are squeezed harder, reaching roughly 2-bit average storage on LLaMA models without retraining the network itself.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.26660](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26660) |
| Precision | W~2 (mixed, effective 1.96-2.02) A16 |
| Granularity | per-column-chunk mixed precision (chunk size G=256 weights) |
| Calibration | 64 Open-Platypus samples (sequence length 256), activation-aware |
| Symmetric? | mixed |
| Outlier handling | RL controller assigns higher bit-width and quantization treatment to sensitive column chunks |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | LLaMA-3.1-8B WikiText-2 perplexity 7.90 at 1.96-bit avg (vs 5.56 FP16); LLaMA-3-70B 5.10 at 2.02-bit avg (vs 2.53 FP16) |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) · [awq](#awq) |
| Related | [ramp](#ramp) · [gptq](#gptq) · [awq](#awq) · [omniquant](#omniquant) · [spinquant](#spinquant) · [aqlm](#aqlm) · [efficientqat](#efficientqat) · [llm-qat](#llm-qat) · [squeezellm](#squeezellm) · [spqr](#spqr) |

**How it works:**

A PPO policy treats bit-width and quantization-treatment selection for each column chunk (default chunk size G=256 weights) as a sequence of decisions made under a global effective- bit budget. State features capture weight statistics and activation-aware sensitivity from a small calibration set; the action assigns a per-chunk plan whose cost is tracked by explicit effective-bit accounting so the total stays within the target average bit-width. The reward combines an episode-end quality penalty, per-step budget shaping, and perplexity-ratio penalties, steering the controller toward allocations that minimize accuracy loss at the budget. After the plan is decided, each unit is fit with a lightweight local quantizer rather than retraining model weights, keeping optimization cost far below QAT.

---

<a id="qam-w"></a>
### QAM-W · W5.5A16 · PTQ W-only · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/qam-w.svg" width="640" alt="QAM-W diagram">
<p><em>QAM-W: row-normalize -> block-Hadamard rotate -> pair into 2D coordinates -> joint Lloyd-Max codebook quantization with activation-aware per-channel scaling.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights] --> B[Calibration data:\nyes]
    B --> C[Compute scale\nand zero-point]
    C --> D[Quantize weights\nto W5.5A16]
    D --> E[Outlier handling:\nrow L2-normalization and block]
    E --> F[Quantized model\nW5.5A16]
```

> QAM-W is a weight-only post-training quantization codec that treats pairs of weights as 2D points and codes them jointly instead of one scalar at a time. Each weight row is first L2-normalized (the 16-bit norm is stored as side information), then passed through a block- Hadamard rotation so the coordinates look like samples from a circular Gaussian. Adjacent coordinates are grouped into 2D pairs and quantized against a single Lloyd-Max codebook trained on that unit circular Gaussian, with an AWQ-style per-channel scale applied so that channels feeding large activations get finer resolution. At roughly 5.5 bits per weight the activation-aware variant stays within about 0.4% of BF16 WikiText-2 perplexity on five models from 1.1B to 13B parameters, reaching SmoothQuant W8A8 quality at around 32% fewer weight bits. The current implementation only reduces storage, dequantizing to BF16 before the matmul.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.26339](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26339) |
| Precision | W5.5A16 |
| Granularity | per-row normalization + block-Hadamard rotation, then 2D-pair codebook; per-channel activation-aware scaling |
| Calibration | up to 1024 unit-normalized rows per weight matrix for pair-scale (sigma_k) estimation; AWQ-style per-channel scale from layer-input RMS statistics |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | row L2-normalization and block-Hadamard rotation to Gaussianize/spread coordinates, plus AWQ-style activation-aware per-channel scaling |
| Hardware target | GPU storage-only (dequantizes to BF16 before matmul; no fused dequant-matmul kernel yet) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | within +-0.4% of BF16 WikiText-2 perplexity at ~5.5 bpw across five models; e.g. Llama-2-7B +0.29% (4.7664 vs 4.7527 BF16), Mistral-7B +0.1%, Qwen2.5-3B +0.3%, TinyLlama-1.1B +0.4% |
| Builds on | [awq](#awq) · [quip-sharp](#quip-sharp) · [quarot](#quarot) · [squeezellm](#squeezellm) |
| Related | [qtip](#qtip) · [aqlm](#aqlm) · [smoothquant](#smoothquant) · [quip](#quip) |

**How it works:**

Rather than scalar-quantizing each weight independently, QAM-W exploits 2D structure: per- row L2 normalization plus a block-Hadamard rotation reshapes weight distributions toward an isotropic Gaussian, after which consecutive coordinates are paired into 2D points and quantized with a shared Lloyd-Max codebook optimized for the unit circular Gaussian. A per- pair scale sigma_k (estimated from up to 1024 calibration rows) plus an AWQ-style per- channel activation-aware scale (exponent alpha=0.3, grid-tuned not learned) allocate codebook resolution to the channels that matter most for layer output. Joint 2D coding beats polar (amplitude x phase) coding by 2-15 perplexity points at equal bitrate. Bits-per-weight follows bpw = B/2 + 16/d_in, where B is bits per pair and 16 bits/row store the norm.

---

<a id="xfp"></a>
### XFP · W3-W4 (~3.4 effective bits) A16 · PTQ W-only · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/xfp.svg" width="640" alt="XFP diagram">
<p><em>XFP: split weights into sparse fp16 outliers plus sub-byte indices into a Lloyd-learned codebook, sized automatically to meet a per-channel cosine-similarity quality floor.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights] --> B[Calibration data:\nno]
    B --> C[Compute scale\nand zero-point]
    C --> D[Quantize weights\nto W3-W4 approx 3.4 effective bits A16]
    D --> E[Outlier handling:\nsparse fp16 outlier residual s]
    E --> F[Quantized model\nW3-W4 approx 3.4 effective bits A16]
```

> XFP is a weight-only quantizer that flips the usual setup: instead of picking a bit-width, the operator sets a target reconstruction-quality floor expressed as per-channel cosine similarity, and the method itself decides how big each layer's codebook should be, how many outliers to keep in full precision, and how to pack the result. Each weight matrix is split into a sparse fp16 residual that holds the largest outliers and a dense tensor of sub-byte indices into a per-group codebook learned by Lloyd iteration on the remaining bulk weights. Because no Hessian or calibration data is used, quantization runs without any forward passes over sample text. It is built mainly for Mixture-of-Experts models, using a stricter quality floor for attention and shared experts and a looser one for routed experts, and an outer loop adjusts those two thresholds until a model just fits a given memory budget while still producing usable output.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.14844](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14844) |
| Code | [tree/multiquant](https://github.com/flash7777/vllm/tree/multiquant) |
| Precision | W3-W4 (~3.4 effective bits) A16 |
| Granularity | per-channel learned codebooks (V2) or 32 shared codebooks per layer (V2a); per-group indices |
| Calibration | none - no Hessian and no calibration data; quality floors drive an iterative search |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | sparse fp16 outlier residual separated from the dense codebook-indexed bulk, with an auto-determined per-layer outlier budget |
| Hardware target | NVIDIA GPU (RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell workstation); unified decode kernel in a vLLM branch |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | 94.49% GSM8K strict-match on Qwen3.5-122B-A10B (V2, INT4-equivalent); 66.72% GSM8K on Qwen3.5-397B-A17B at ~3.4 effective bits |
| Builds on | [squeezellm](#squeezellm) · [spqr](#spqr) · [aqlm](#aqlm) |
| Related | [squeezellm](#squeezellm) · [spqr](#spqr) · [aqlm](#aqlm) · [marlin](#marlin) · [vllm-quant](#vllm-quant) |

**How it works:**

XFP decomposes each weight matrix into a sparse fp16 outlier residual plus a dense sub-byte index tensor that points into a learned per-group codebook. Codebook centroids are fit with about 20 rounds of Lloyd assignment-and-update on the outlier-cleaned bulk distribution, offered in a per-channel mode (V2) and a mode that shares a library of 32 codebooks per layer (V2a) behind a common decode kernel. The user specifies two per-channel cosine- similarity floors (strict for attention/shared experts, lazy for routed MoE experts) and the system automatically selects codebook size, outlier budget, and packing per layer to meet them. An outer H-Process iterates over the two thresholds to find the operating point where a model just fits the available hardware memory while still producing sensible output.

---

<a id="admm-q"></a>
### ADMM-Q · W2A16/W3A16/W4A16 (also composes to W4A8, W2A4KV4, W4A4KV4) · PTQ W-only · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/admm-q.svg" width="640" alt="ADMM-Q diagram">
<p><em>ADMM operator splitting: alternate continuous Hessian-weighted updates with projection onto the quantization grid, annealing the dual penalty until weights land on the grid.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights] --> B[Calibration data:\nyes]
    B --> C[Compute scale\nand zero-point]
    C --> D[Quantize weights\nto W2A16/W3A16/W4A16 also composes to W4A8, W2A4KV4, W4A4KV4]
    D --> E[Outlier handling:\nMSE grid-search range clipping]
    E --> F[Quantized model\nW2A16/W3A16/W4A16 also composes to W4A8, W2A4KV4, W4A4KV4]
```

> ADMM-Q replaces GPTQ's greedy column-by-column rounding with an operator-splitting solver that adjusts every weight in a layer at once to keep the layer's output close to the original. It alternates between a continuous update that lowers reconstruction error and a step that nudges weights toward the nearest allowed quantized values, gradually tightening the quantization constraint until the weights actually land on the grid. Extra tricks (a penalty schedule, preconditioning, and a final local-search pass that flips individual weights) make it run efficiently and converge reliably. Because it only changes how the weights are picked, it slots into existing pipelines and stacks with clipping, rotations, and activation scaling, with the biggest gains showing up at aggressive sub-4-bit settings.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.11222](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11222) |
| Precision | W2A16/W3A16/W4A16 (also composes to W4A8, W2A4KV4, W4A4KV4) |
| Granularity | per-channel or per-group, group size 128 tested |
| Calibration | layer-wise Hessian from calibration-sample input activations (unlabeled) |
| Symmetric? | mixed |
| Outlier handling | MSE grid-search range clipping (100 grid points, max shrink 0.8); composable with SmoothQuant per-channel scaling and SpinQuant rotations |
| Hardware target | GPU; drop-in weight quantizer for existing W4A16/W4A8/W4A4 inference pipelines |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | Qwen3-8B WikiText-2 ppl vs GPTQ: W3A16 10.06 vs 12.85; W4A8 (SmoothQuant) 8.68 vs 9.29; W2A4KV4 (SpinQuant) 19.42 vs 66.11. LLaMA-3-8B W4A4KV4 (SpinQuant) 7.26 vs GPTQ 7.34 |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) · [obs](#obs) |
| Related | [spqr](#spqr) · [quip](#quip) · [smoothquant](#smoothquant) · [spinquant](#spinquant) · [quarot](#quarot) · [awq](#awq) |

**How it works:**

ADMM-Q formulates layer-wise weight quantization as a constrained least-squares problem minimizing the Hessian-weighted reconstruction error ||W - Ŵ||_H subject to Ŵ lying on the quantization grid, then solves it with a combinatorial variant of the Alternating Direction Method of Multipliers. It splits the problem into a continuous primal variable and a discrete (quantized) copy linked by a dual variable: one block solves a Hessian-regularized continuous update, the other projects onto the discrete codebook, and the dual penalty is annealed on a schedule so the continuous solution is steadily pulled onto the grid with convergence guarantees. Preconditioning accelerates the continuous solves and a local-search post-processing step greedily flips individual quantized weights to escape poor local minima. Unlike GPTQ's one-pass greedy error feedback, all weights in the layer are optimized jointly and iteratively.

---

<a id="osaq"></a>
### OSAQ · W2/W3/W4 A16 · PTQ W-only · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/osaq.svg" width="640" alt="OSAQ diagram">
<p><em>OSAQ estimates the layer Hessian from calibration data, extracts its stable null space (directions of vanishing curvature), and adds a closed-form null-space combination to the weights to suppress outliers without changing the layer output; the transformed weights are then quantized (e.g. with GPTQ) at 2-4 bits with no inference overhead.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights] --> B[Calibration data:\nyes]
    B --> C[Compute scale\nand zero-point]
    C --> D[Quantize weights\nto W2/W3/W4 A16]
    D --> E[Outlier handling:\nadditive weight transform buil]
    E --> F[Quantized model\nW2/W3/W4 A16]
```

> OSAQ is a preprocessing step for weight-only LLM quantization that reshapes a layer's weights so they are easier to round to very low bit-widths. The authors notice that the layer's Hessian (which measures how much each weight direction affects the loss) is consistently low-rank across different inputs, meaning some directions barely matter. They build an additive correction out of those flat directions and add it to the weights to flatten out large outlier values, which leaves the layer's output essentially unchanged. Because the correction is computed in closed form and folded directly into the weights ahead of time, it adds no training cost and no inference cost, and it can be stacked in front of an existing quantizer like GPTQ.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICML 2026** |
| Paper | [2605.04738](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.04738) |
| Precision | W2/W3/W4 A16 |
| Granularity | per-channel and per-group (group size 128) |
| Calibration | 128 sequences x 2048 tokens, unlabeled (to estimate the Hessian) |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | additive weight transform built from the stable null space of the layer Hessian; outliers are absorbed into directions of vanishing curvature so task loss is unchanged |
| Hardware target | GPU; absorbed into weights offline so existing W4A16/W2A16 inference kernels apply with no added overhead |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | LLaMA2-13B W2A16 g128: WikiText-2 perplexity 28.1 (GPTQ) -> 13.4 (OSAQ+GPTQ), over 40% lower; tested up to LLaMA-3.1-405B |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) |
| Related | [quip](#quip) · [quarot](#quarot) · [spinquant](#spinquant) · [duquant](#duquant) · [owq](#owq) · [spqr](#spqr) |

**How it works:**

OSAQ observes that the layer-wise Hessian H exhibits low-rank consistency across calibration inputs, so a stable set of directions have vanishing curvature (the Hessian null space). It constructs an additive weight transform W' = W + delta, where delta is a linear combination of vectors spanning this null space, chosen to minimize the magnitude of weight outliers. Because delta lies in the null space, the change to the task loss is (to second order) zero, so accuracy is preserved while the weight distribution becomes flatter and easier to quantize. The optimal coefficients are obtained by a closed-form least-squares solution, and delta is absorbed into the weights offline, requiring no inter-layer transforms (unlike rotation methods) and adding no inference overhead; it is applied as a front-end to standard quantizers such as GPTQ.

<a id="slq"></a>
### SLQ · W3.3-W6.6 A16 (per-layer mixed; ~3.3-4.7 bits task-lossless, ~5-6.6 bits distribution-lossless) · PTQ W-only · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/slq.svg" width="640" alt="SLQ diagram">
<p><em>SLQ: per-layer Shapley sensitivity scoring feeds an ILP that allocates bitwidths {2..8} to meet a task- or distribution-lossless target under group-wise asymmetric quantization.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights] --> B[Calibration data:\nyes]
    B --> C[Compute scale\nand zero-point]
    C --> D[Quantize weights\nto W3.3-W6.6 A16 per-layer mixed; approx 3.3-4.7 bits task-lossless, approx 5-6.6 bits distribution-lossless]
    D --> E[Outlier handling:\nasymmetric zero-point quanti]
    E --> F[Quantized model\nW3.3-W6.6 A16 per-layer mixed; approx 3.3-4.7 bits task-lossless, approx 5-6.6 bits distribution-lossless]
```

> SLQ reframes LLM weight quantization around three concrete definitions of "lossless": task- lossless (zero-shot benchmark accuracy stays within natural sampling noise of the FP16 model), distribution-lossless (the quantized model's next-token distribution is practically interchangeable with the original), and a theoretical argument that asymmetric integer quantization injects less error than symmetric. To hit a chosen losslessness target at the fewest bits, it scores how much each layer is hurt by each candidate bitwidth and then solves an integer program that hands out bits per layer, so easy layers go low and sensitive ones stay high. The result is post-training, calibration-only (no fine-tuning), and reaches roughly 3.3 bits per parameter while preserving benchmark accuracy, with measured 1.7-3.6x throughput gains over FP16 using optimized mixed-bitwidth kernels.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.02404](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.02404) |
| Code | [IST-DASLab/SLQ](https://github.com/IST-DASLab/SLQ) |
| Precision | W3.3-W6.6 A16 (per-layer mixed; ~3.3-4.7 bits task-lossless, ~5-6.6 bits distribution-lossless) |
| Granularity | group-wise (group size 128), per-layer bitwidth from {2,3,4,5,6,7,8} |
| Calibration | 512 calibration samples; no gradient updates |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | asymmetric (zero-point) quantization plus GPTQ-style second-order error compensation; higher per-layer bitwidths allocated to sensitive layers |
| Hardware target | NVIDIA GPU (L40S); vLLM with Marlin-based mixed-bitwidth kernels for 4-8 bit weight-only inference |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | task-lossless at ~3.3 bits (e.g. Qwen3.5-27B at 3.30 bits, ~0.991 avg accuracy recovery vs FP16 across ARC-Challenge, GSM8K, GSM8K-CoT, IFEval, MMLU-CoT, MMLU-Pro); also evaluated on Qwen3-8B, Qwen3-32B, Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) · [owq](#owq) · [spqr](#spqr) |
| Related | [awq](#awq) · [squeezellm](#squeezellm) · [marlin](#marlin) · [autoround](#autoround) |

**How it works:**

SLQ performs weight-only post-training quantization with group-wise (g=128) asymmetric integer quantization and assigns a separate bitwidth to each layer drawn from {2,3,4,5,6,7,8}. Per-layer sensitivity is estimated with a multi-bitwidth Shapley-style attribution over calibration data, and an integer linear program then allocates bits across layers to minimize predicted degradation under a fidelity constraint. Two fidelity targets are defined: task-lossless (accuracy within sampling variance) and distribution-lossless, the latter measured by Expected Acceptance Rate (EAR), the average of sum_k min(p(k), q(k)) between original and quantized token distributions, which equals the maximum token-agreement probability under optimal coupling. A gamma-squared variance law motivates using asymmetric over symmetric quantization to reduce noise variance.

<a id="gsq"></a>
### GSQ · W2/W3 A16 · PTQ W-only · 2026-04

<img src="../assets/diagrams/gsq.svg" width="640" alt="GSQ diagram">
<p><em>GSQ: Gumbel-Softmax relaxation of discrete scalar-grid assignments, jointly learned with per-group scales, warm-started from GPTQ.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights] --> B[Calibration data:\nyes]
    B --> C[Compute scale\nand zero-point]
    C --> D[Quantize weights\nto W2/W3 A16]
    D --> E[Outlier handling:\nno dedicated outlier path; per]
    E --> F[Quantized model\nW2/W3 A16]
```

> GSQ is a weight-only, post-training quantizer that keeps weights on a plain symmetric scalar grid (so it runs on ordinary integer matmul kernels) but learns where each weight lands and how each group is scaled, instead of just rounding. Starting from a GPTQ solution, it treats the choice of grid point for each weight as a discrete decision and relaxes that decision with a Gumbel-Softmax so it can be tuned by gradient descent against calibration data, jointly with the per-group scales. The goal is to get close to the accuracy of much heavier vector- and trellis-quantized methods at 2 and 3 bits while staying simple to deploy. The authors show it holds up on Llama-3.1-8B/70B and even scales to a trillion-parameter Mixture-of-Experts model (Kimi-K2.5) where vector quantization is hard to apply.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2604.18556](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.18556) |
| Code | [IST-DASLab/GSQ](https://github.com/IST-DASLab/GSQ) |
| Precision | W2/W3 A16 |
| Granularity | group-wise, group size 128 (per-group scale) |
| Calibration | FineWeb-Edu calibration set (~4096 sequences of length 4096); warm-started from a GPTQ solution |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | no dedicated outlier path; per-group scales plus learned grid assignments absorb in-group dynamic range |
| Hardware target | GPU; standard symmetric scalar layers compatible with existing scalar inference kernels (e.g. Humming kernels on NVIDIA L40s, up to ~6.2x speedup over BF16 at 2-bit) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | On Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct at 2-bit, ~75.6% average zero-shot accuracy across ARC-C/ARC-E/HellaSwag/PIQA/WinoGrande, vs ~77.3% for QTIP (vector quant) and ~71.4% for EfficientQAT (scalar); closes most of the scalar-to-VQ gap at 2-3 bits |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) |
| Related | [qtip](#qtip) · [aqlm](#aqlm) · [quip-sharp](#quip-sharp) · [pv-tuning](#pv-tuning) · [efficientqat](#efficientqat) · [awq](#awq) |

**How it works:**

Each weight's assignment to one of the 2^b symmetric grid levels is modeled as a categorical variable; a Gumbel-Softmax relaxation makes these discrete assignments differentiable, so the per-coordinate level choices and the per-group scales are optimized jointly by gradient descent on a calibration objective. Optimization is warm-started from a GPTQ solution and a temperature schedule anneals the soft assignments toward hard (one-hot) grid choices by the end of training. Because the output is a standard symmetric per-group scalar quantization, no special vector-quantization codebooks or custom decode kernels are needed at inference. This lets GSQ approach the accuracy of QTIP/AQLM-style vector quantization at 2-3 bits while remaining a scalar format.

<a id="dash-q"></a>
### DASH-Q · W2A16 · PTQ W-only · 2026-04

<img src="../assets/diagrams/dash-q.svg" width="640" alt="DASH-Q diagram">
<p><em>DASH-Q discards off-diagonal Hessian terms, keeping only the diagonal curvature D = diag(h11, h22, ...) to turn second-order error compensation into independent per-column weighted least squares problems solved iteratively for stable 2-bit weight-only quantization.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights] --> B[Calibration data:\nyes]
    B --> C[Compute scale\nand zero-point]
    C --> D[Quantize weights\nto W2A16]
    D --> E[Outlier handling:\nDiagonal Hessian importance we]
    E --> F[Quantized model\nW2A16]
```

> DASH-Q is a weight-only post-training quantization method aimed at very low bit-widths (2 to 4 bits) when only a small amount of calibration data is available. Other curvature-based methods estimate a full Hessian to compensate quantization error across channels, but with limited calibration samples those cross-channel estimates become noisy and hurt accuracy at low bit-widths. DASH-Q keeps only the diagonal of the Hessian, treating each weight column independently, and solves an iterative weighted least squares problem so that columns carrying more signal power are preserved more carefully. The result is a quantizer that stays stable with few calibration samples and runs much faster than optimization-heavy alternatives, while improving accuracy in the 2-bit regime.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **EuroMLSys 2026** |
| Paper | [2604.13806](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.13806) |
| Precision | W2A16 |
| Granularity | Per-group, group size 32 (2-bit), 64 (3-bit), 128 (4-bit) |
| Calibration | 128 samples of sequence length 2048 from WikiText-2 training set |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | Diagonal Hessian importance weighting that prioritizes preservation of salient feature power; off-diagonal (cross-channel) curvature terms are discarded as noise rather than using a separate outlier path |
| Hardware target | GPU (general weight-only inference); no custom kernel introduced |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | At 2-bit weight-only on Llama-3.1-8B: 56.52% average zero-shot accuracy across 8 tasks (ARC-C/E, BoolQ, HellaSwag, OBQA, PIQA, SIQA, Winogrande), reported as +14.01% over the strongest baseline and +7.01% average improvement across five models (Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen3-14B, DeepSeek-MoE-16B, Phi-3.5-MoE, Mixtral-8x7B); up to 74.5x faster quantization than optimization-based PTQ baselines |
| Builds on | [obs](#obs) · [obq](#obq) · [gptq](#gptq) |
| Related | [awq](#awq) · [quip](#quip) · [owq](#owq) · [spqr](#spqr) · [squeezellm](#squeezellm) · [rtn](#rtn) · [quarot](#quarot) |

**How it works:**

The method replaces the full Hessian H used in OBS/OBC-style error compensation with its diagonal D = diag(h11, h22, ...), which decouples the coupled second-order optimization into independent per-column weighted least squares subproblems that have closed-form solutions. The diagonal weights act as importance scores that emphasize columns with high feature power, while discarding noise-prone off-diagonal dependencies that are poorly estimated from limited calibration data. Quantization uses asymmetric affine group-wise rounding (group sizes 32/64/128 for 2/3/4-bit), and the weighted least squares step is applied iteratively to refine the assignment without any retraining.

<a id="cae-rescomp"></a>
### CAE (ResComp) · W2/W3 A16 · PTQ W-only · 2026-04

<img src="../assets/diagrams/cae-rescomp.svg" width="640" alt="CAE (ResComp) diagram">
<p><em>Column-wise weight quantization with two residual-error sources fed into the inverse-Hessian update: the cross-layer output difference (as in GPTAQ) plus the new intra-layer compensation-aware error between compensated and original weights, both aligned to the original full-precision output.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights] --> B[Calibration data:\nyes]
    B --> C[Compute scale\nand zero-point]
    C --> D[Quantize weights\nto W2/W3 A16]
    D --> E[Outlier handling:\nsecond-order inverse Hessian]
    E --> F[Quantized model\nW2/W3 A16]
```

> This work re-examines how iterative weight-compensation quantizers like GPTQ and GPTAQ define the error they try to cancel. The authors point out that when these methods quantize a layer column by column, they line up the quantized output with the output of the already- compensated weights instead of the original full-precision weights, which is the wrong target. They reframe the objective to always match the true full-precision output, and show this introduces an extra error source they call the compensation-aware error: the gap between compensated and original weights inside the same layer. Folding this term into the column update (reusing GPTAQ's neuron-decomposition trick) costs only about 5 percent more quantization time and noticeably improves accuracy, with the biggest gains showing up at 3-bit and 2-bit weight-only settings.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICLR 2026** |
| Paper | [2604.07955](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.07955) |
| Code | [list0830/ResComp](https://github.com/list0830/ResComp) |
| Precision | W2/W3 A16 |
| Granularity | per-group symmetric, group size 128 |
| Calibration | 128 samples (C4 or WikiText-2), unlabeled |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | second-order (inverse Hessian) error compensation, augmented with a compensation-aware error term; pairs with QuaRot rotation for 2-bit |
| Hardware target | GPU; standard GPTQ/GPTAQ weight-only inference kernels |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | GPTQ+CAE cuts Llama2-7B 3-bit C4 perplexity from 13.60 to 8.34; GPTAQ+CAE reaches 8.19 (vs 8.40); 2-bit + QuaRot on Llama2-13B: WikiText-2 7.32 vs 7.50 for GPTAQ |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) · [quarot](#quarot) |
| Related | [obq](#obq) · [obs](#obs) · [spinquant](#spinquant) · [quip](#quip) |

**How it works:**

Existing compensation-based PTQ aligns each column's quantized output against the running compensated weights, not the original full-precision weights, which the authors argue is a sub-optimal calibration objective. They redefine the per-step objective to align the quantized output directly with the original full-precision output, exposing a second residual-error source beyond the usual cross-layer output difference: the discrepancy between compensated and original weights within the layer, named the compensation-aware error. Using GPTAQ's neuron-decomposition, this extra term is incorporated efficiently into the standard inverse-Hessian column update with minimal overhead. The enhancement is drop-in for both GPTQ and GPTAQ and is combined with QuaRot rotation for 2-bit settings.

<a id="oa-em"></a>
### OA-EM · W2A16 · PTQ W-only · 2026-04

<img src="../assets/diagrams/oa-em.svg" width="640" alt="OA-EM diagram">
<p><em>OA-EM: Hessian-weighted Mahalanobis EM initialisation of the additive codebook places 2-bit optimisation in a better basin than greedy sequential init.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights] --> B[Calibration data:\nyes]
    B --> C[Compute scale\nand zero-point]
    C --> D[Quantize weights\nto W2A16]
    D --> E[Outlier handling:\noutput-aware Hessian-weighted]
    E --> F[Quantized model\nW2A16]
```

> OA-EM tackles why additive (codebook) quantization tends to collapse at 2 bits per weight. The authors argue the real culprit is not the search or the finetuning but the starting point: the standard greedy, sequential way of laying down the first codebook drops the model into a poor region that later beam search and PV-tuning cannot climb out of. They introduce a representational ratio that relates how many weight groups must be encoded to how much capacity the codebooks actually have, and use it to reason about when initialisation matters most. Their fix replaces greedy placement with an EM procedure that assigns weight groups to codebook entries using a Hessian-weighted Mahalanobis distance, so the initialisation accounts for how much each weight affects the layer output. On small Llama and Qwen models this lowers 2-bit perplexity noticeably while keeping the same fast lookup-table inference as AQLM.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2604.08118](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08118) |
| Code | [kenno94-IK/aqlm-oaem](https://github.com/kenno94-IK/aqlm-oaem) |
| Precision | W2A16 |
| Granularity | per 128-weight group via additive codebook (K=256, M=2 codebooks) |
| Calibration | C4 calibration set (~1024 sequences) used to compute the output-aware Hessian for initialisation |
| Symmetric? | n/a |
| Outlier handling | output-aware (Hessian-weighted) codebook initialisation that steers optimisation into a better basin; magnitudes absorbed by additive multi-codebook representation |
| Hardware target | GPU (inherits AQLM's O(1) lookup-table dequantization kernels) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | Llama 3.2 3B at 2-bit: WikiText-2 ppl ~11.53 with OA-EM vs ~12.66 with greedy sequential initialisation |
| Builds on | [aqlm](#aqlm) · [pv-tuning](#pv-tuning) |
| Related | [quip-sharp](#quip-sharp) · [gptq](#gptq) · [squeezellm](#squeezellm) · [qtip](#qtip) |

**How it works:**

Additive quantization represents each weight group as a sum of M codebook entries; the quality of the final solution is largely fixed by the initial codebook. OA-EM frames initialisation as a clustering/assignment problem solved by Expectation-Maximization, where the distance used to assign a weight group to a codebook centroid is a Hessian-weighted Mahalanobis distance (output-aware), rather than plain Euclidean distance over raw weights. This biases the initial codebook toward minimizing the layer's output reconstruction error, placing the model in a better optimisation basin before the usual beam-search code updates and PV-tuning finetuning are applied. The authors analyse this through the representational ratio rho = N/(K*M), which characterizes the tension between the number of weight groups and the codebook capacity.

<a id="ramp"></a>
### RAMP · W2-W8 A16 mixed (per-layer) · PTQ W-only · 2026-03

<img src="../assets/diagrams/ramp.svg" width="640" alt="RAMP diagram">
<p><em>RAMP: SAC reinforcement-learning policy assigns per-layer bit-widths under a global bit-budget.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights] --> B[Calibration data:\nyes]
    B --> C[Compute scale\nand zero-point]
    C --> D[Quantize weights\nto W2-W8 A16 mixed per-layer]
    D --> E[Outlier handling:\nRL policy assigns higher bit-w]
    E --> F[Quantized model\nW2-W8 A16 mixed per-layer]
```

> RAMP (Reinforcement Adaptive Mixed-Precision) uses a Soft Actor-Critic (SAC) reinforcement-learning policy to automatically assign per-layer bit-widths to an LLM under a global bit-budget constraint. The RL agent observes each layer's sensitivity statistics and outputs a bit-width decision; the reward is a combination of model quality (perplexity) and constraint satisfaction. This replaces expensive evolutionary or gradient-based mixed-precision search with a learned, adaptive policy that generalizes across architectures.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2603.17891](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.17891) |
| Precision | W2-W8 A16 mixed (per-layer) |
| Granularity | per-layer mixed precision, RL-guided |
| Calibration | small calibration set for sensitivity and reward estimation |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | RL policy assigns higher bit-width to sensitive layers |
| Hardware target | GPU |

| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) · [awq](#awq) |

**How it works:**

RAMP formulates per-layer bit-width selection as a sequential decision problem and trains a SAC agent to solve it. Each layer is described by a feature vector of weight and activation statistics; the agent outputs a bit-width from a discrete action space (e.g. 2, 3, 4, 8 bits). A global bit-budget constraint is enforced via a Lagrangian penalty in the reward. RAMP also introduces "Scale Folding," which folds per-channel quantization scales into adjacent BatchNorm or LayerNorm parameters to eliminate scale overhead at inference time. The trained policy can be applied to new models without re-running the RL search from scratch.

<a id="qtip"></a>
### QTIP · W2 A16 · PTQ W-only · 2024-06

<img src="../assets/diagrams/qtip.svg" width="640" alt="QTIP diagram">
<p><em>QTIP: Hadamard rotation + trellis-coded joint quantization over weight sequences.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weight W] --> B[Hadamard rotation\nincoherence preprocessing]
    B --> C[Near-Gaussian\nweight distribution]
    C --> D[Trellis-coded quant:\ndynamic programming\nover weight sequence]
    D --> E[Viterbi-style joint\nerror minimization]
    E --> F[Store trellis\nstate indices]
    F --> G[Fast CUDA\ndecode kernel]
    G --> H[W2A16 state-of-art\n< 0.3 ppl vs FP16]
```

> QTIP pushes 2-bit weight quantization to near-lossless quality by combining incoherence processing (random Hadamard rotation) with trellis-coded quantization - a sequence-modeling approach that codes runs of weights jointly rather than independently. The trellis codebook captures inter-weight correlations within a group, yielding significantly better rate-distortion than per-weight scalar codebooks. QTIP achieves state-of-the-art 2-bit results on LLaMA-2 70B with perplexity within 0.3 of FP16.

| Typical degradation | < 0.3 ppl vs FP16 at W2 on LLaMA-2-70B |
| Builds on | [quip-sharp](#quip-sharp) · [quip](#quip) · [gptq](#gptq) |
| Related | [quip-sharp](#quip-sharp) · [aqlm](#aqlm) · [hqq](#hqq) |

**How it works:**

After rotating weights with a random Hadamard matrix, QTIP applies trellis-coded quantization: dynamic programming treats the weight sequence as a Markov chain and finds the joint quantization minimizing total squared error across the group. This is analogous to Viterbi decoding on a trellis. Results are stored as trellis-state indices decoded at inference via a fast CUDA kernel. Builds on QuIP# incoherence preprocessing and adds sequence-level coding on top.

---

<a id="quant-llm"></a>
### QuaRot / Quant-LLM (FP6) · W4/W6 A16 · PTQ W-only · 2024-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/quant-llm.svg" width="640" alt="QuaRot / Quant-LLM (FP6) diagram">
<p><em>Mixed FP6/INT4 per-layer assignment with speculative decoding compensation.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 weight W] --> B[Random Hadamard\nrotation R]
    B --> C[Incoherent W_tilde]
    C --> D[FP6 quantize\nTC-FPx kernel]
    D --> E[W6A16 Quant-LLM]
    C --> F[FP4 quantize\nexperimental]
    F --> G[W4A16 FP4 variant]
```

> Quant-LLM explores mixed-precision serving that pairs FP6 weight quantization with token-level speculative decoding. Latency-sensitive layers get W6A16 (FP6 weights) for high quality, while less sensitive layers get W4A16. Speculative decoding with a small draft model compensates for the quality gap of the lower-precision layers. This co-design approach targets serving throughput rather than the quantization algorithm itself.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2405.08925](https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.08925) |
| Precision | W4/W6 A16 |
| Granularity | per-group |
| Calibration | none |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | floating-point format; speculative decoding for mixed-bit layers |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | competitive with GPTQ at same effective bit-width |
| Builds on | [fp6-llm](#fp6-llm) · [gptq](#gptq) |
| Related | [fp6-llm](#fp6-llm) · [awq](#awq) · [marlin](#marlin) |

**How it works:**

Quant-LLM assigns FP6 quantization to the first and last layers of the transformer (which are most sensitive to quantization error) and INT4 to the middle layers. The FP6 layers use the TC-FPx kernel from FP6-LLM for efficient dequantization. Token-level speculative decoding (accepting draft tokens only when the draft quality is high) partially masks the perplexity gap from lower-precision layers. The net result is higher serving throughput per GPU at near-equivalent quality vs. uniform FP16 or W4A16.

---

<a id="quip-sharp"></a>
### QuIP# · W2/W3/W4 A16 · PTQ W-only · 2024-02

<img src="../assets/diagrams/quip-sharp.svg" width="640" alt="QuIP# diagram">
<p><em>Hadamard incoherence + E8 lattice vector quantization for 2-bit PTQ.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weight W] --> B[Random Hadamard\nrotation H]
    B --> C[Incoherent W_tilde = HWH^T]
    C --> D[E8 lattice\ncodebook quantize]
    D --> E[Store codebook\nindices only]
    E --> F[Lookup-table\ndecode at inference]
    F --> G[W2A16\nnear-lossless]
```

> QuIP# extends QuIP with two key improvements: replacing random orthogonal rotations with Hadamard matrices (which are fast to compute and hardware-friendly), and replacing scalar uniform quantization with vector quantization using the E8 lattice codebook. The E8 lattice is the densest known 8-dimensional integer lattice; using it as a codebook allows more efficient representation of 8 values simultaneously than 8 independent scalar quantizations. Together, these make QuIP# the best-known 2-bit PTQ method as of 2024, achieving near-FP16 perplexity at 2 bits on LLaMA-2 scale.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **NeurIPS 2024** |
| Paper | [2402.04396](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.04396) |
| Code | [Cornell-RelaxML/quip-sharp](https://github.com/Cornell-RelaxML/quip-sharp) |
| Precision | W2/W3/W4 A16 |
| Granularity | per 8-element block (E8 lattice) |
| Calibration | calibration set |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | Hadamard incoherence + E8 lattice vector quantization |
| Hardware target | GPU (custom CUDA kernels for E8 decode) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | 2-bit LLaMA-2-7B approaches full-precision quality; best known 2-bit PTQ |
| Builds on | [quip](#quip) · [gptq](#gptq) |
| Related | [aqlm](#aqlm) · [quip](#quip) · [quarot](#quarot) |

**How it works:**

Two innovations over QuIP: (1) Hadamard incoherence - using a randomized Hadamard transform H (with random sign flips) instead of dense random orthogonal matrices; Hadamard transforms are O(n log n) instead of O(n²) and can be fused with matrix multiplications. (2) E8 lattice codebooks - groups of 8 weights are jointly quantized to the nearest point in the E8 lattice (scaled by a per-group scale), achieving the theoretical lower bound on quantization error for nearly-Gaussian distributions in 8D. At 2 bits (E8P codebook, 2 bits/weight), ppl of LLaMA-2-7B on WikiText-2 drops to ~6.5 vs. FP16 ~5.5 - within 1 point.

---

<a id="fp6-llm"></a>
### FP6-LLM · W6A16 · PTQ W-only · 2024-01

<img src="../assets/diagrams/fp6-llm.svg" width="640" alt="FP6-LLM diagram">
<p><em>TC-FPx kernel: bitpacked FP6 weights unpacked to FP16 inside Tensor Core matmul.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP32/FP16 weight] --> B[Convert to FP6:\nE3M2 or E2M3 format]
    B --> C[Pack two FP6 values\ninto 12-bit pair]
    C --> D[TC-FPx CUDA kernel:\nbitwise unpack + GEMM]
    D --> E[Tensor-core compatible\nhardware efficient]
    E --> F[W6A16: better than\nW4 accuracy near W8]
```

> FP6-LLM makes 6-bit floating-point weight quantization practical on NVIDIA GPUs that have no native FP6 hardware support. The method packs FP6 weights (1 sign, 2 exponent, 3 mantissa bits) into standard GPU memory layouts and implements a GPU kernel (TC-FPx) that unpacks and converts FP6 to FP16 during the matrix multiplication, achieving near-FP16 throughput. The floating-point format's larger dynamic range compared to INT6 means FP6 requires less clipping, which is important for LLM weights with outlier channels.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **USENIX ATC 2024** |
| Paper | [2401.14112](https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.14112) |
| Code | [usyd-fsalab/fp6_llm](https://github.com/usyd-fsalab/fp6_llm) |
| Precision | W6A16 |
| Granularity | per-group |
| Calibration | none (RTN-style) or small set |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | floating-point format has larger dynamic range than INT6 |
| Hardware target | NVIDIA GPU (A100/H100); custom SIMT bitmanipulation kernels |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | minimal at 6-bit; better perplexity than INT6 RTN at same storage |
| Builds on | [rtn](#rtn) |
| Related | [awq](#awq) · [gptq](#gptq) · [marlin](#marlin) |

**How it works:**

The core contribution is a CUDA kernel strategy called TC-FPx that enables arbitrary bit-width (e.g. FP6) matrix multiplications on Tensor Cores without native hardware support. Weights are stored in a bitpacked format; during matmul, each FP6 weight is unpacked and up-cast to FP16 in registers before multiplication, leveraging the GPU's native FP16 Tensor Cores. The unpacking is done in SIMT warps in a way that is bandwidth-efficient and avoids branching. At 6 bits, floating-point representation outperforms integer (INT6) because the non-uniform grid better fits the near-Gaussian weight distributions of large LLMs.

---

<a id="aqlm"></a>
### AQLM · W2 A16 (avg ~2 bits) · PTQ W-only · 2024-01

<img src="../assets/diagrams/aqlm.svg" width="640" alt="AQLM diagram">
<p><em>Additive multi-codebook quantization: 8 weights = 2 index lookups.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Weight col w in R^d] --> B[Split into\nm sub-vectors]
    B --> C[M learned\ncodebooks C_1 to C_m]
    C --> D[Find nearest\ncentroid per sub-vec]
    D --> E[Store M indices\nper group]
    E --> F[Decode w_hat = sum C_i idx_i]
    F --> G[2-3 bit\neffective storage]
```

> AQLM applies additive quantization (from the image/audio compression literature) to LLM weights. Each group of 8 weights is represented as a sum of M codebook entries (M=1 or 2 in practice), where each codebook has 2^b entries. With M=2 codebooks of 256 entries each, 8 weights are stored as 2 bytes of indices plus the shared codebooks, achieving approximately 2 bits per weight. Learned via a beam-search optimization that jointly minimizes layer-wise reconstruction error, AQLM achieves state-of-the-art 2-bit quality among PTQ methods and outperforms QuIP# on several model families.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICML 2024** |
| Paper | [2401.06118](https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.06118) |
| Code | [Vahe1994/AQLM](https://github.com/Vahe1994/AQLM) |
| Precision | W2 A16 (avg ~2 bits) |
| Granularity | per 8-weight group via additive codebook |
| Calibration | calibration set for codebook learning (beam search) |
| Symmetric? | n/a (codebook-based) |
| Outlier handling | multi-codebook additive representation absorbs all magnitudes |
| Hardware target | GPU (custom CUDA kernel for codebook decode) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | LLaMA-2-7B at 2 bits: ~6.7 ppl vs 5.5 FP16 - best among 2-bit PTQ at time of release |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) · [obq](#obq) |
| Related | [quip-sharp](#quip-sharp) · [squeezellm](#squeezellm) · [gptq](#gptq) |

**How it works:**

Each weight block w ∈ ℝ⁸ is approximated as ŵ = Σ_{m=1}^{M} C_m[i_m], where C_m are learned codebooks of shape (K, 8) and i_m is a learned integer index. With M=2, K=256: total bits = 2 × log₂(256) = 16 bits for 8 weights = 2 bits/weight. Codebooks are learned by alternating between fixing codes and updating codebooks (via least-squares) and fixing codebooks and updating codes (via beam search). The layer-wise reconstruction loss is minimized as in GPTQ. AQLM also supports a fine-tuning step to further close the gap with FP16.

---

<a id="gguf-iquants"></a>
### GGUF I-quants · W1/W2/W3/W4 A16 (fractional effective bits) · PTQ W-only · 2024-01

<img src="../assets/diagrams/gguf-iquants.svg" width="640" alt="GGUF I-quants diagram">
<p><em>Importance-weighted lattice quantization: imatrix guides bit allocation per row.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Weight block] --> B[Hadamard preprocess:\nincoherence rotation]
    B --> C[Near-Gaussian\nweight distribution]
    C --> D[E8-like lattice\ncodebook quantize]
    D --> E[Store codebook indices\nIQ2_XXS to IQ4_XS]
    E --> F[Best quality per bit\nin GGUF format]
```

> I-quants are the "importance-aware" quantization formats in llama.cpp, superseding K-quants for quality at the same or lower bit-width. They require an "importance matrix" (imatrix) computed by running the model on a representative sample and recording the squared mean activation for each weight row. Weights in rows with high importance scores are quantized more carefully (or get more effective bits). The quantization grid itself uses lattice codebooks derived from the paper "Improved Lattice Quantization via Dual Quantization" rather than uniform linear scales, allowing non-uniform grids that fit the weight distributions better.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | n/a (no paper) |
| Code | [ggerganov/llama.cpp](https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp) |
| Blog / post | [link](https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp/pull/4773) |
| Precision | W1/W2/W3/W4 A16 (fractional effective bits) |
| Granularity | per-group, variable sizes; importance matrix guided |
| Calibration | importance matrix (imatrix) from a representative dataset |
| Symmetric? | n/a (lattice codebook-based) |
| Outlier handling | importance-weighted lattice quantization; high-importance weights get more bits |
| Hardware target | CPU / GPU / Metal (llama.cpp) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | IQ4_XS at ~4.25 bits beats Q4_K_M; IQ2_XXS at ~2.06 bits is usable |
| Builds on | [gguf-kquants](#gguf-kquants) · [rtn](#rtn) |
| Related | [gguf-kquants](#gguf-kquants) · [aqlm](#aqlm) · [hqq](#hqq) |

**How it works:**

The imatrix records ‖x_j‖² per row j (the squared norm of input activations for each weight row), collected by one forward pass on a calibration corpus. At quantization time, rows with higher importance are assigned more bits or have their quantization refined more carefully. The quantization codebooks for sub-4-bit formats are lattice-based (derived from integer lattices), not simple uniform grids. IQ4_NL uses a "non-linear" 4-bit codebook derived from the NF4 quantile approach. Sub-3-bit formats (IQ2_*, IQ1_S) use group-wise lattice VQ with the imatrix weighting to preserve perplexity at extremely low bit rates.

---

<a id="hqq"></a>
### HQQ · W2/W3/W4/W8 A16 · PTQ W-only · 2023-11

<img src="../assets/diagrams/hqq.svg" width="640" alt="HQQ diagram">
<p><em>Robust half-quadratic loss minimization for scale/zero-point - no calibration data needed.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Weight W] --> B[Minimize L1\nnorm W - Q W_s_z]
    B --> C[Proximal\niterative solve]
    C --> D{Converged?}
    D -->|no| B
    D -->|yes| E[Optimal scale s\nzero-point z]
    E --> F[No calibration data\nW4/W2 in under 1 min]
```

> HQQ finds optimal quantization parameters (scale and zero-point) by directly minimizing a robust loss between the original and quantized weights using half-quadratic optimization, without any calibration data. The half-quadratic penalty is a smoothed version of L1 that down-weights large residuals (outliers), making the scale/zp solution robust to weight outliers. Because it needs no activations, HQQ is significantly faster to run than GPTQ and can quantize a 70B model in under a minute on CPU. Quality is close to GPTQ at 4-bit and significantly better than RTN, especially at lower bits.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | n/a (no paper) |
| Code | [mobiusml/hqq](https://github.com/mobiusml/hqq) |
| Blog / post | [link](https://mobiusml.github.io/hqq_blog/) |
| Precision | W2/W3/W4/W8 A16 |
| Granularity | per-group, g=64 (default) |
| Calibration | none - weights only |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | robust half-quadratic (Huber-like) loss minimization during scale/zp optimization |
| Hardware target | GPU; custom CUDA/Triton kernels for W4A16 and W2A16 |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | competitive with GPTQ at 4-bit; better than RTN at 3-bit |
| Builds on | [rtn](#rtn) |
| Related | [gptq](#gptq) · [awq](#awq) · [rtn](#rtn) |

**How it works:**

Standard RTN minimizes ‖W − Q(W)‖² w.r.t. the scale s and zero-point z, but this mean-squared loss is sensitive to outliers. HQQ instead solves: min_{s,z} Σ ρ(w_i − Q(w_i; s, z)) where ρ is a half-quadratic (Huber-like) penalty. This is solved by iterative half-quadratic optimization: alternating between a quadratic subproblem for s and z (given auxiliary variables) and an update of auxiliary variables (given s and z). The result is a set of quantization parameters that minimize the robust loss without requiring any calibration data.

---

<a id="exl2"></a>
### EXL2 · W2–W8 A16 (mixed per-row, target average bit-width) · PTQ W-only · 2023-10

<img src="../assets/diagrams/exl2.svg" width="640" alt="EXL2 diagram">
<p><em>EXL2: each weight matrix row assigned a different bit-width by Hessian importance.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights] --> B[Calibration data:\nyes]
    B --> C[Compute scale\nand zero-point]
    C --> D[Quantize weights\nto W2-W8 A16 mixed per-row, target average bit-width]
    D --> E[Outlier handling:\nper-row bit-width assignment: ]
    E --> F[Quantized model\nW2-W8 A16 mixed per-row, target average bit-width]
```

> EXL2 is the quantization format native to the ExLlamaV2 inference engine. It extends GPTQ's approach with per-row bit-width assignment: different rows (output channels) of each weight matrix are quantized to different bit-widths (2–8), with the assignment determined by row-level Hessian importance. A target average bits-per-weight is specified, and the quantizer greedily assigns more bits to rows that suffer more from quantization. This makes EXL2 a "smooth" format - any target bpw from 2.0 to 8.0 in 0.05 steps is achievable, rather than the discrete jumps of W3/W4/W5.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | n/a (no paper) |
| Code | [turboderp/exllamav2](https://github.com/turboderp/exllamav2) |
| Blog / post | [link](https://github.com/turboderp/exllamav2/blob/master/doc/quant/exl2.md) |
| Precision | W2–W8 A16 (mixed per-row, target average bit-width) |
| Granularity | per-group, g=32–128; rows assigned different bit-widths |
| Calibration | calibration dataset for Hessian-based row importance |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | per-row bit-width assignment: important rows get more bits |
| Hardware target | NVIDIA GPU (ExLlamaV2 custom CUDA kernels) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | at 4.0 bpw: comparable to GPTQ 4-bit; at 3.0 bpw: better than GPTQ 3-bit |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) |
| Related | [gguf-kquants](#gguf-kquants) · [gptq](#gptq) · [marlin](#marlin) |

**How it works:**

EXL2 quantization proceeds in two passes: (1) compute per-row Hessian importance scores (using the squared input activation norms, as in GPTQ), then assign bit-widths row-by-row using a greedy algorithm that maximizes quality for a given average bpw budget; (2) quantize each row to its assigned bit-width using GPTQ-style column-by-column second-order updates. At inference, ExLlamaV2's custom CUDA kernel handles the heterogeneous row precisions in a single kernel call, using a compact header per row that specifies its bit-width and group size. The result is smooth quality-vs-compression tradeoffs unavailable in fixed-format schemes.

---

<a id="autoround"></a>
### AutoRound · W4A16 · PTQ W-only · 2023-09

<img src="../assets/diagrams/autoround.svg" width="640" alt="AutoRound diagram">
<p><em>Sign gradient descent optimizes rounding direction per weight jointly within each block.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 weights\n+ calibration data] --> B[SignSGD optimizer\nfor scale and zero-point]
    B --> C[Fake quant in\nforward pass]
    C --> D[Gradient via\nSTE backprop]
    D --> E{Converged?}
    E -->|no| B
    E -->|yes| F[INT4 weights\nAutoGPTQ-compatible format]
```

> AutoRound improves on GPTQ's rounding decisions by framing quantization as a block-wise sign-gradient descent problem rather than an analytic second-order update. Each weight's rounding direction (floor vs. ceil) is treated as a binary decision variable, and these decisions are optimized jointly within each block by computing sign gradients of the block reconstruction loss. This allows AutoRound to find better rounding patterns than the greedy column-by-column approach of GPTQ, without requiring more memory than a calibration forward pass.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2309.05516](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.05516) |
| Code | [intel/auto-round](https://github.com/intel/auto-round) |
| Precision | W4A16 |
| Granularity | per-group, g=128 |
| Calibration | 512 sequences × 2048 tokens |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | sign gradient descent for rounding decisions (not just round-to-nearest) |
| Hardware target | GPU / Intel hardware |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | close to or better than GPTQ at 4-bit on most benchmarks |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) · [rtn](#rtn) |
| Related | [gptq](#gptq) · [awq](#awq) · [omniquant](#omniquant) |

**How it works:**

AutoRound uses Signed Gradient Descent (SignSGD) to optimize rounding decisions. Starting from RTN as initialization, for each block AutoRound iteratively updates the rounding offsets Δ_ij ∈ {0, 1} (floor or ceil) according to: Δ ← clip(Δ − lr · sign(∇_Δ ‖ f(X; W) − f(X; W̃) ‖²), 0, 1). This is run for a fixed number of iterations (200 by default) per block. Unlike GPTQ which processes columns sequentially, AutoRound treats the block jointly. The resulting rounding patterns tend to be more balanced, especially at 4-bit.

---

<a id="gguf-kquants"></a>
### GGUF K-quants · W2/W3/W4/W5/W6 A16 · PTQ W-only · 2023-08

<img src="../assets/diagrams/gguf-kquants.svg" width="640" alt="GGUF K-quants diagram">
<p><em>Hierarchical two-level K-quant: inner-block scales quantized relative to a FP16 super-block scale.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 weight block\n256 elements] --> B[Compute importance\nvia activation norms]
    B --> C[Scale important\ngroups more finely]
    C --> D[Super-block scale\n+ sub-block scales]
    D --> E[Pack to target bit\nQ2_K to Q8_K]
    E --> F[Better accuracy than\nuniform at same bits]
```

> The K-quant formats in llama.cpp use a two-level hierarchical quantization scheme. A super-block of 256 weights contains several inner blocks of 16–32 weights each. Each inner block has its own low-precision scale (quantized), while the super-block stores the dequantization scale for those inner-block scales in a higher precision. Different K-quant variants (Q2_K, Q3_K, Q4_K_S, Q4_K_M, Q5_K, Q6_K) differ in the number of bits for both the weights and the block scales, and optionally assign higher precision to specific layers (e.g. attention layers in _M variants).

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | n/a (no paper) |
| Code | [ggerganov/llama.cpp](https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp) |
| Blog / post | [link](https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp/pull/1684) |
| Precision | W2/W3/W4/W5/W6 A16 |
| Granularity | super-block of 256 weights; inner blocks of 16–32 weights |
| Calibration | none |
| Symmetric? | mixed (super-block scale symmetric; inner block scales asymmetric) |
| Outlier handling | hierarchical two-level scaling with higher-bit super-block scale |
| Hardware target | CPU / GPU / Metal (llama.cpp) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | Q4_K_M: < 0.5 ppl vs FP16 on LLaMA-7B; Q2_K: ~2–4 ppl |
| Builds on | [rtn](#rtn) |
| Superseded by | [gguf-iquants](#gguf-iquants) |
| Related | [gguf-iquants](#gguf-iquants) · [hqq](#hqq) · [awq](#awq) |

**How it works:**

The hierarchy is: weights at b bits → inner-block scale at b' bits → super-block scale at FP16. For Q4_K_M: 4-bit weights, 6-bit inner-block scales (for every 32 weights), FP16 super-block scale (for every 256 weights), with attention output and feed-forward gate layers boosted to 6-bit. The _S suffix means "small" (uniform across layers); _M means "medium" (mixed layers). This two-level scheme amortizes the scale overhead and achieves better quality-per-byte than flat per-group quantization with the same group size.
