<a id="quip"></a>
### QuIP · W2/W4 A16 · PTQ W-only · 2023-07

<img src="../assets/diagrams/quip.svg" width="640" alt="QuIP diagram">
<p><em>Random orthogonal rotation makes weights incoherent; uniform quantization then works at 2-bit.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weight W] --> B[Random orthogonal\nrotation R]
    B --> C[Incoherent W_tilde = R W R^T]
    C --> D[Near-Gaussian\ndistribution]
    D --> E[Uniform scalar\nquantize W_tilde]
    E --> F[At inference:\ndecode via R^T * Q * R]
    F --> G[Incoherence enables\nlow-bit near-lossless]
```

> QuIP is the first method to achieve practical 2-bit LLM quantization with theoretical guarantees. The key insight is "incoherence processing": by multiplying weights and calibration inputs by a random orthogonal matrix before quantization (and the inverse after), the weight distribution becomes nearly Gaussian with no outliers, allowing simple uniform quantization to work well even at 2 bits. The quality improvement is dramatic - 2-bit QuIP outperforms all prior 2-bit methods by a large margin.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **NeurIPS 2023** |
| Paper | [2307.13304](https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.13304) |
| Code | [jerry-chee/QuIP](https://github.com/jerry-chee/QuIP) |
| Precision | W2/W4 A16 |
| Granularity | per-group after rotation |
| Calibration | calibration set for Hessian and rotation |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | random orthogonal pre/post-rotation to spread outliers uniformly |
| Hardware target | GPU (custom dequantization kernels needed for rotation) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | < 1 ppl at 2-bit LLaMA-2-7B vs. previous 2-bit methods (large improvement) |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) · [obq](#obq) |
| Superseded by | [quip-sharp](#quip-sharp) |
| Related | [quip-sharp](#quip-sharp) · [quarot](#quarot) · [spinquant](#spinquant) · [gptq](#gptq) |

**How it works:**

The weight matrix W is transformed as W̃ = U W Vᵀ where U and V are random orthogonal matrices (pre- and post-multiplication). An orthogonal transformation doesn't change the Frobenius norm of the error, but it makes the error more uniform across entries, reducing the maximum individual error from O(√d) to O(1). This "incoherence" property allows uniform scalar quantization of W̃ to work well. At inference, inputs are also pre-multiplied by V and the output is post-multiplied by Uᵀ. QuIP also includes a greedy coordinate descent (LDLQ) step to further reduce quantization error.

---

<a id="squeezellm"></a>
### SqueezeLLM · W4 A16 (sparse overlay) · PTQ W-only · 2023-06

<img src="../assets/diagrams/squeezellm.svg" width="640" alt="SqueezeLLM diagram">
<p><em>k-means non-uniform quantization + sensitivity-based sparse FP16 overlay.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Weight W] --> B[Fisher info\nsensitivity map]
    B --> C[Top-k sensitive\nweights → sparse FP16]
    C --> D[Rest → k-means\nclustered INT4]
    D --> E[Dense INT4\n+ sparse FP16 residual]
    E --> F[Non-uniform quant\nbetter than uniform at same bits]
```

> SqueezeLLM uses the same dense-and-sparse decomposition strategy as SpQR but combines it with non-uniform (k-means) quantization for the dense part rather than uniform grid quantization. The most sensitive weights (by Fisher information / gradient covariance) are stored in a sparse FP16 matrix; the remaining weights are clustered using k-means, with each weight pointing to one of 2^b centroids stored in a look-up table. The non-uniform grid better captures the actual weight distribution, reducing error vs. uniform quantization at the same bit-width.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICML 2024** |
| Paper | [2306.07629](https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.07629) |
| Code | [SqueezeAILab/SqueezeLLM](https://github.com/SqueezeAILab/SqueezeLLM) |
| Precision | W4 A16 (sparse overlay) |
| Granularity | per-group (dense), unstructured sparse |
| Calibration | small calibration set for sensitivity |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | sensitivity-based sparse extraction + non-uniform k-means quantization |
| Hardware target | GPU; custom sparse-dense kernel |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | < 0.1 ppl at 3-bit (LLaMA-7B vs FP16) with 0.45% sparse ratio |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) · [rtn](#rtn) |
| Related | [spqr](#spqr) · [aqlm](#aqlm) · [owq](#owq) |

**How it works:**

Two components work together: (1) Sensitivity-guided sparse extraction - Fisher information S_ij = (∇_w L)² · f(H_ij) scores each weight; top-k% become sparse FP16. (2) Non-uniform k-means quantization of the dense remainder - instead of a linear scale, centroids are learned offline (2^b of them per group), and each weight is stored as a b-bit index into the centroid table. This is a form of scalar vector quantization. At inference, a custom CUDA kernel handles both the sparse lookup and the centroid dequantization in a single pass.

---

<a id="spqr"></a>
### SpQR · W3/W4 A16 (sparse overlay) · PTQ W-only · 2023-06

<img src="../assets/diagrams/spqr.svg" width="640" alt="SpQR diagram">
<p><em>Sensitive weights kept as FP16 sparse; rest quantized to INT3/INT4.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weight W] --> B[Sensitivity via\nHessian H]
    B --> C{Outlier weight?}
    C -->|yes ~1%| D[Store in\nFP16 sparse]
    C -->|no ~99%| E[Quantize to\n3-4 bit group]
    D --> F[Sparse FP16\n+ dense INT3/4]
    E --> F
    F --> G[approx 3.9 bit avg\nnear-lossless]
```

> SpQR combines dense grouped quantization with a sparse FP16 overlay for the most sensitive weights. For each weight group, the Hessian-based sensitivity is computed, and the top ~1% of weights with the highest sensitivity score are extracted and stored in a sparse FP16 format alongside the quantized dense block. This lets SpQR achieve near-lossless 3-bit compression: the bulk of weights go to 3 bits, while a tiny fraction stay in FP16. The dense part is compatible with standard GPTQ-style quantization.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICLR 2024** |
| Paper | [2306.03078](https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.03078) |
| Code | [Vahe1994/SpQR](https://github.com/Vahe1994/SpQR) |
| Precision | W3/W4 A16 (sparse overlay) |
| Granularity | per-group (dense part), unstructured sparse (sensitive weights) |
| Calibration | 128 sequences, calibration for Hessian estimation |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | sensitive weights stored as FP16 sparse; density ~1% of weights |
| Hardware target | GPU; requires sparse kernel support for the FP16 overlay |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | < 1% relative perplexity increase at 3-bit on LLaMA-1-65B |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) · [obq](#obq) |
| Related | [squeezellm](#squeezellm) · [owq](#owq) · [gptq](#gptq) |

**How it works:**

SpQR extends GPTQ with a hybrid representation: each weight w_ij is scored by its sensitivity |∂²L/∂w_ij²| = [H]_{ii} / N (column Hessian diagonal). Weights with sensitivity above a threshold are stored as (index, FP16 value) sparse pairs; the rest are quantized to low bit-width with per-group scales. At inference, the sparse FP16 overlay is added on top of the dequantized dense matmul result. The overhead of the sparse overlay is negligible at 1% density, but its impact on quality is large since it captures the most error-sensitive weights.

---

<a id="owq"></a>
### OWQ · W3/W4 A16 (mixed) · PTQ W-only · 2023-06

<img src="../assets/diagrams/owq.svg" width="640" alt="OWQ diagram">
<p><em>Hessian-ranked column selection: top columns stay FP16, rest go INT3/4.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Weight W\n+ activation stats] --> B[Identify outlier-\ninfluenced columns]
    B --> C[Outlier cols:\nhigher bit width or FP16]
    C --> D[Remaining cols:\nlow-bit GPTQ quantize]
    D --> E[Mixed-precision\nper column]
    E --> F[Better accuracy\nthan uniform GPTQ]
```

> OWQ identifies the weight columns whose quantization errors propagate most destructively to the output - those corresponding to large-magnitude activation channels - and keeps them in FP16, quantizing everything else to low bits. This is complementary to SpQR: OWQ works at the column level (keeping entire columns at FP16) rather than individual weight sensitivity. The column selection criterion is the Hessian diagonal [H]_jj (the squared input activation norm for column j), which captures how much quantizing column j would affect the output.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **AAAI 2024** |
| Paper | [2306.02272](https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.02272) |
| Code | [xvyaward/owq](https://github.com/xvyaward/owq) |
| Precision | W3/W4 A16 (mixed) |
| Granularity | per-column (most), per-weight FP16 for sensitive |
| Calibration | calibration set for Hessian column norms |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | Hessian-norm-ranked weight columns kept at FP16 (mixed precision) |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | < 0.5 ppl at 3-bit LLaMA-7B with ~0.1% FP16 columns |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) · [rtn](#rtn) |
| Related | [spqr](#spqr) · [gptq](#gptq) · [awq](#awq) |

**How it works:**

For each linear layer, compute the Hessian diagonal [H]_jj = E[x_j²] (mean squared activation for input channel j). Columns j with the largest [H]_jj values are the most sensitive; the top few percent are kept at FP16 while the rest are quantized to W3 or W4 with GPTQ-style second-order correction. A small number of FP16 columns (0.1–0.3%) provides a large quality recovery because the outlier channels (which exist in the same positions across all tokens for large LLMs) are fully preserved.

---

<a id="awq"></a>
### AWQ · W4A16 · PTQ W-only · 2023-06

<img src="../assets/diagrams/awq.svg" width="640" alt="AWQ diagram">
<p><em>Activation-magnitude-guided channel scaling before standard quantization.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Calibration\nActivations X] --> B[Per-channel\nmagnitude norm_x_j]
    B --> C[Scale s_j = norm_x_j ^ alpha]
    C --> D[W_hat = Q of W times diag_s]
    D --> E[Absorb s^-1 into\nprevious layer]
    E --> F[W4A16\ndeployment]
```

> AWQ observes that not all weight channels are equally important - channels corresponding to high-magnitude activation dimensions cause disproportionately large quantization error. The solution is to scale those channels up by their activation magnitude (making them harder to quantize individually but reducing their contribution to output error), then divide the corresponding activation by the same scale to preserve mathematical equivalence. The scale can be found analytically or by a fast grid search. AWQ requires no Hessian computation and no per-weight optimization: it is one forward calibration pass plus a scale computation, making it much faster than GPTQ.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **MLSys 2024** |
| Paper | [2306.00978](https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.00978) |
| Code | [mit-han-lab/llm-awq](https://github.com/mit-han-lab/llm-awq) |
| Precision | W4A16 |
| Granularity | per-group, g=128 |
| Calibration | small activation sample (no labels required) |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | activation-magnitude-guided per-channel weight scaling before quantization |
| Hardware target | GPU; TinyChat / AutoAWQ W4A16 kernels (CUDA, Metal) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | < 0.3 ppl on LLaMA-7B at 4-bit (WikiText-2) |
| Builds on | [rtn](#rtn) |
| Related | [gptq](#gptq) · [owq](#owq) · [spqr](#spqr) · [smoothquant](#smoothquant) |

**How it works:**

For each linear layer, AWQ observes per-channel activation magnitudes s̃j = mean(|Xj|) and computes a scale factor sj = s̃j^α, where α ∈ [0,1] balances the difficulty between weights and activations. The transformation W' = W · diag(s) is applied to weights (scale up salient columns), and the inverse diag(s)⁻¹ is absorbed into the preceding layer (scale down the activations). The scaled W' is then quantized with standard RTN. The α parameter is found by a lightweight grid search minimizing layer-wise reconstruction loss. Because the scale is absorbed offline, inference overhead is zero.

---

<a id="bitsandbytes-nf4"></a>
### NF4 / QLoRA · W4A16 (NF4) · PTQ W-only · 2023-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/bitsandbytes-nf4.svg" width="640" alt="NF4 / QLoRA diagram">
<p><em>NF4 quantile grid: 16 levels at Gaussian quantiles; double-quantized per-group scale.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 weight values\nof one 64-element block] --> B[Normalize to -1 to 1\nabs max scaling]
    B --> C[Find nearest NF4 level\nfrom 16 non-uniform bins]
    C --> D[Store 4-bit NF4 index\nper weight]
    D --> E[At compute time:\ndequant to BF16]
    E --> F[Optimal for normal\ndistribution weights]
```

> The QLoRA paper introduced the NF4 (Normal Float 4-bit) data type: a 4-bit format whose quantization levels are spaced at the quantiles of the standard normal distribution, rather than linearly. Because pre-trained LLM weights are approximately normally distributed, NF4 achieves lower expected quantization error than uniform INT4 at the same bit-width. QLoRA also introduces "double quantization" (quantizing the per-group scales themselves to 8 bits) and paged optimizers to fit fine-tuning of 65B+ models into a single GPU. NF4 is implemented in bitsandbytes and is now the dominant format for memory-efficient inference of base models.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **NeurIPS 2023** |
| Paper | [2305.14314](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.14314) |
| Code | [artidoro/qlora](https://github.com/artidoro/qlora) |
| Precision | W4A16 (NF4) |
| Granularity | per-group, g=64 (double quantization) |
| Calibration | none for quantization; LoRA adapters are trained separately |
| Symmetric? | n/a (non-uniform NF4 grid) |
| Outlier handling | NF4 quantile grid inherently handles Gaussian tails; double quantization for scales |
| Hardware target | GPU (bitsandbytes CUDA kernels) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | < 0.5 ppl on LLaMA-7B; specifically designed to minimize quantization error for normally-distributed weights |
| Builds on | [rtn](#rtn) |
| Related | [gptq](#gptq) · [awq](#awq) · [hqq](#hqq) · [qlora](#qlora) |

**How it works:**

NF4 construction: given a standard normal distribution, compute the 16 quantiles q₀, q₁, ..., q₁₅ (including ±∞ boundaries). Normalize to [-1, 1]. These 16 values are the NF4 codebook. Quantize weight w as: round(w / s) → nearest NF4 code index (lookup table), where s = max(|W|) / max(NF4 codes). The quantiles are closer together near zero (where most weights concentrate) and farther at the tails, matching the Gaussian distribution's probability density. Double quantization: each per-group scale is itself stored as FP8 with a shared FP32 super-scale, saving an extra 0.125 bits/weight.

---

<a id="gptq"></a>
### GPTQ · W3/W4 A16 · PTQ W-only · 2022-10

<img src="../assets/diagrams/gptq.svg" width="640" alt="GPTQ diagram">
<p><em>Column-wise error compensation via inverse Hessian redistribution.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weight W] --> B[Compute Hessian\nH = 2XX^T]
    B --> C[Cholesky\ndecompose H^-1]
    C --> D{For each\ncolumn q}
    D --> E[Quantize col\nto INT4]
    E --> F[Redistribute error\nto cols q+1 onward]
    F --> D
    D --> G[INT4 W\n+ group scales]
```

> GPTQ processes each transformer layer independently, using an approximate inverse Hessian of the layer's input to compensate for quantization error column-by-column. Quantizing one column introduces a residual error, which is immediately redistributed to the remaining unquantized columns via an OBS-style update. A careful column-ordering and a precomputed Cholesky factorization of the Hessian make the whole thing tractable in minutes per layer. GPTQ produced the kernel ecosystem (Marlin, ExLlamaV2) that most 4-bit open-weight LLM inference runs on today, and was the first method to make sub-4-bit quality practical at LLaMA scale.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICLR 2023** |
| Paper | [2210.17323](https://arxiv.org/abs/2210.17323) |
| Code | [IST-DASLab/gptq](https://github.com/IST-DASLab/gptq) |
| Precision | W3/W4 A16 |
| Granularity | per-column (output), group size 128 typical |
| Calibration | 128 sequences × 2048 tokens, unlabeled |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | second-order (inverse Hessian) error compensation per column |
| Hardware target | GPU; Marlin / ExLlamaV2 W4A16 kernels for fast inference |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | < 0.5 ppl on LLaMA-7B at 4-bit; ~1–2 ppl at 3-bit (WikiText-2) |
| Builds on | [obq](#obq) · [obs](#obs) |
| Related | [awq](#awq) · [spqr](#spqr) · [quip](#quip) · [owq](#owq) |

**How it works:**

For each linear layer, GPTQ estimates H = 2 X Xᵀ (the expected squared input) as a proxy for the layer-wise loss Hessian. It then processes weight columns left-to-right: quantize column q to b bits, compute the residual error δq = (wq − ŵq), and propagate it to all remaining columns as Δ W[:,q+1:] -= δq · (H⁻¹[q, q+1:] / H⁻¹[qq]). The inverse Hessian is maintained via a blocked Cholesky factorization for numerical stability and cache efficiency. This is a direct application of the OBS (Optimal Brain Surgeon) framework to PTQ, applied layer-by-layer without any gradient computation.

---

<a id="obq"></a>
### OBQ · W3/W4 A16 · PTQ W-only · 2022-08

<img src="../assets/diagrams/obq.svg" width="640" alt="OBQ diagram">
<p><em>Optimal Brain Surgeon applied per-weight with exact error propagation.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Weight matrix W\n+ Hessian H] --> B[Compute per-weight\nsaliency score]
    B --> C[Select least-salient\nweight]
    C --> D[Quantize it to\nlow-bit value]
    D --> E[Redistribute error:\nW remaining -= delta * H^-1 row]
    E --> F{All weights\nquantized?}
    F -->|no| B
    F -->|yes| G[Low-bit W\nwith OBS error comp]
```

> OBQ applies the Optimal Brain Surgeon (OBS) second-order framework to weight quantization: for each weight quantized, the residual quantization error is exactly absorbed by updating all remaining unquantized weights. This gives the theoretically best PTQ solution within a layer given an inverse Hessian, but is O(d³) per layer - impractical at LLM scale. GPTQ is a fast approximation of OBQ that sacrifices exact per-weight optimality for column-parallel processing, reducing complexity to O(d² × columns).

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **NeurIPS 2022** |
| Paper | [2208.11580](https://arxiv.org/abs/2208.11580) |
| Code | [IST-DASLab/OBQ](https://github.com/IST-DASLab/OBQ) |
| Precision | W3/W4 A16 |
| Granularity | per-weight (unstructured) |
| Calibration | small calibration set |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | exact OBS second-order error propagation |
| Hardware target | CPU / GPU (no sparse kernel advantage without sparsity support) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | better than RTN at equal bit-width; slower than GPTQ |
| Builds on | [obs](#obs) |
| Superseded by | [gptq](#gptq) |
| Related | [gptq](#gptq) · [spqr](#spqr) |

**How it works:**

For each weight wq selected for quantization, the OBS update is: δ W = −(wq − Q(wq)) / [H⁻¹]qq × H⁻¹[q, :]. OBQ applies this exactly, in an order selected by minimum quantization error. The exact-order selection makes OBQ intractable at LLM scale (quadratic in the number of weights per row), but gives near-theoretical-optimal quality on small models.

---

<a id="obs"></a>
### OBS · n/a (pruning framework; later adapted to quantization) · PTQ W-only · 1993-01

<img src="../assets/diagrams/obs.svg" width="640" alt="OBS diagram">
<p><em>OBS: second-order weight change formulation and exact Hessian-based compensation.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Layer output error\nE = trace of H * delta_W^2] --> B[Choose weight\nwith min saliency]
    B --> C[Set weight to zero\n/ quantize it]
    C --> D[Update remaining\nweights via H^-1]
    D --> E{More weights?}
    E -->|yes| B
    E -->|no| F[Compressed layer\n+ error compensation]
```

> Optimal Brain Surgeon (OBS) is the classical second-order framework for weight pruning (and later, quantization). It computes, for each weight w_q, the increase in training loss from removing or changing that weight, using the full Hessian of the loss. After removing/changing weight q, OBS updates all remaining weights to minimize the resulting error: Δw = −(w_q / [H⁻¹]_qq) × H⁻¹[q, :]. This exact second-order compensation is the theoretical foundation for GPTQ, OBQ, and all subsequent Hessian-based quantization methods.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **NeurIPS 1992** |
| Paper | [303ed4c69846ab36c2904d3ba8573050-Paper.pdf](https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/1992/file/303ed4c69846ab36c2904d3ba8573050-Paper.pdf) |
| Precision | n/a (pruning framework; later adapted to quantization) |
| Granularity | per-weight |
| Calibration | training data (Hessian computed during training) |
| Symmetric? | n/a |
| Outlier handling | second-order Taylor expansion; exact error propagation |
| Hardware target | n/a (algorithm, not LLM-specific) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | n/a |
| Related | [obq](#obq) · [gptq](#gptq) |

**How it works:**

For a network with loss L(w) ≈ L(w₀) + ½(w−w₀)^T H (w−w₀), removing weight w_q introduces error δL = w_q² / (2[H⁻¹]_qq). The optimal compensation update for the remaining weights is Δw = −(w_q / [H⁻¹]_qq) × H⁻¹[:, q]. OBS generalizes this to quantization: setting w_q to its quantized value ŵ_q (instead of zero) introduces error (w_q − ŵ_q)² / (2[H⁻¹]_qq), and the same Hessian update compensates. The key computational challenge is inverting H, which is O(d³) - the bottleneck that GPTQ and OBQ address with approximations.

---

<a id="rtn"></a>
### RTN · W4A16 · PTQ W-only · unknown

<img src="../assets/diagrams/rtn.svg" width="640" alt="RTN diagram">
<p><em>Round-to-nearest: scale, round, clip - the universal baseline.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights] --> B[Calibration data:\nno]
    B --> C[Compute scale\nand zero-point]
    C --> D[Quantize weights\nto W4A16]
    D --> E[Outlier handling:\nnone - no compensation mechani]
    E --> F[Quantized model\nW4A16]
```

> Round-to-nearest is the simplest possible quantization strategy: compute a linear scale from the weight range, divide each weight by that scale, round to the nearest integer, and clip to the representable range. It requires no data, no calibration, and no compute beyond a single pass over the weights. It is the universal baseline against which all other methods are measured. At 4-bit it typically degrades perplexity by 1–3 points on LLaMA-scale models, and at 3-bit or below it degrades rapidly. Every method in this gallery claims to beat RTN at the same bit-width.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | n/a (no paper) |
| Precision | W4A16 |
| Granularity | per-channel or per-group (g=128 typical) |
| Calibration | none - weights only |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | none - no compensation mechanism |
| Hardware target | any (no custom kernels required) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | ~1–3 ppl on LLaMA-7B at 4-bit; ~8–15 ppl at 3-bit (vs. FP16) |
| Superseded by | [gptq](#gptq) · [awq](#awq) · [hqq](#hqq) |
| Related | [gptq](#gptq) · [awq](#awq) · [owq](#owq) |

**How it works:**

Given a weight tensor W, compute s = (max(W) − min(W)) / (2^b − 1) for asymmetric quantization, then q = clip(round(W/s), 0, 2^b − 1). Dequantize as Ŵ = (q − z) × s. Granularity choices (per-tensor, per-channel, per-group) trade storage overhead for accuracy. Per-group with g=128 is the minimum quality bar for LLM deployment today.

---

---

## Post-Training Quantization - Weights + Activations

Quantizing both weights and activations enables integer matrix multiplication (W8A8 or W4A8), which is compute-bound-friendly and achieves higher throughput than W-only on GPU. The challenge is handling activation outliers without sacrificing accuracy.

<a id="shift-and-sum"></a>
### Shift-and-Sum · W4A4 / W4A6 / W6A6 · PTQ W+A · 2026-06

<img src="../assets/diagrams/shift-and-sum.svg" width="640" alt="Shift-and-Sum diagram">
<p><em>Shift-and-Sum: high-attention value tokens are duplicated with symmetric shifts, each quantized, then aggregated so rounding errors cancel; calibration resampling aligns codebook frequency with predicted probabilities.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights + Activations] --> B[Handle outliers:\nduplicating value tokens with ]
    B --> C[Quantize weights\nto low-bit]
    B --> D[Quantize activations\nto low-bit]
    C --> E[INT GEMM\ntensor cores]
    D --> E
    E --> F[W4A4 / W4A6 / W6A6\nquantized inference]
```

> This is a post-training quantization method aimed at visual autoregressive (VAR) image generators rather than language models. The authors notice two problems unique to VAR: the attention-value products carry large quantization error at coarse generation scales where attention concentrates on a few value tokens, and the calibration set under- or over- represents certain codebook entries relative to how often the model actually predicts them. To fix the first, they make extra copies of the value tokens that receive high attention, nudge each copy by a small symmetric offset above and below the original value, quantize all of them, and average the results so the rounding errors cancel out. To fix the second, they resample the calibration tokens so that each VQVAE codebook entry appears in proportion to its predicted probability. The method needs only calibration data and no retraining, and it works across VAR depths on class-conditional generation, inpainting, outpainting, and editing.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICLR 2026** |
| Paper | [2606.16131](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16131) |
| Code | [cvlab-yonsei/Shift-and-Sum](https://github.com/cvlab-yonsei/Shift-and-Sum) |
| Blog / post | [link](http://cvlab.yonsei.ac.kr/projects/Shift-and-Sum/) |
| Precision | W4A4 / W4A6 / W6A6 |
| Granularity | per-channel weights; per-token activations (attention-value path) |
| Calibration | calibration set with resampling to align codebook-entry sampling frequency with predicted probabilities |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | duplicating value tokens with large attention scores and applying symmetric shifts around the original value, then aggregating the quantized duplicates so per-token quantization error cancels in the attention-value product |
| Hardware target | GPU (integer-arithmetic inference for VAR generators) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | VAR-d24 on ImageNet 256x256 at W4A6: FID 3.96 / IS 230.7; VAR-d30 at W4A6: FID 4.59 (vs FP16 baseline) |
| Related | [smoothquant](#smoothquant) |

**How it works:**

For value tokens v that attract large attention weights, the method creates K symmetrically shifted duplicates v ± kΔ around the original value, quantizes each duplicate independently, and sums/averages the dequantized results; because the shifts are symmetric, the per- duplicate rounding errors are anti-correlated and largely cancel, yielding a lower-error estimate of the attention-value product than quantizing v once. The weights and remaining activations are quantized with standard uniform symmetric grids at 4-6 bits. A complementary resampling calibration step reassigns tokens from over-sampled to under-sampled codebook entries so the calibration statistics match the model's predicted token distribution, which improves activation range estimates. The whole pipeline is post-training (calibration only, no gradient updates).

---

<a id="mosaicquant"></a>
### MosaicQuant · W4A8 (also W4A4) · PTQ W+A · 2026-06

<img src="../assets/diagrams/mosaicquant.svg" width="640" alt="MosaicQuant diagram">
<p><em>Dense 4-bit base plus Hessian-selected sparse 4-bit residual, fused into one INT4 GEMM by ZipperEngine.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights + Activations] --> B[Handle outliers:\ninlier-outlier disaggregation:]
    B --> C[Quantize weights\nto low-bit]
    B --> D[Quantize activations\nto low-bit]
    C --> E[INT GEMM\ntensor cores]
    D --> E
    E --> F[W4A8 also W4A4\nquantized inference]
```

> MosaicQuant is a post-training method that pushes both weights and activations to 4 bits without keeping any values in higher precision. Instead of the usual trick of storing rare large-magnitude outlier weights in a separate high-precision format, it first quantizes the entire weight matrix to a single dense 4-bit representation, accepting that outliers get crushed. It then measures which weight blocks hurt the layer output most, using a diagonal- Hessian-based score, and adds a second sparse 4-bit residual only on those blocks to claw back the lost accuracy. Because both the base and the correction are 4-bit, the whole computation stays in one uniform low-bit pipeline; a fused kernel called ZipperEngine folds the sparse residual into the dense 4-bit matrix multiply so there is no extra precision conversion or memory traffic. It needs only a small calibration set and keeps accuracy close to FP16 on LLaMA3 and Qwen3 while running faster than the FP16 baseline.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2606.15652](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15652) |
| Precision | W4A8 (also W4A4) |
| Granularity | per-channel symmetric weights; per-token dynamic activations (W4A8) / per-group activations (W4A4); block-level residual selection |
| Calibration | 128 prompts from WikiText-2, 2048 tokens each |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | inlier-outlier disaggregation: dense 4-bit base plus a sparse 4-bit residual on error-critical blocks chosen by a Hessian-guided output-aware criterion (diagonal-Hessian Delta scoring) |
| Hardware target | NVIDIA GPU (RTX 4090); ZipperEngine fuses sparse residual into dense INT4 GEMM via an overlapped pipeline |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | WikiText-2 PPL: LLaMA3-8B W4A8 10.2 vs FP16 8.6; Qwen3-8B W4A8 10.6 vs FP16 9.7 (W4A4 11.2); Qwen3-8B 6-task zero-shot avg 70.6 vs FP16 71.6; up to 1.24x speedup over W16A16 |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) · [spqr](#spqr) · [squeezellm](#squeezellm) |
| Related | [atom](#atom) · [qserve](#qserve) · [quarot](#quarot) · [marlin](#marlin) · [awq](#awq) |

**How it works:**

MosaicQuant disaggregates inliers and outliers entirely within 4-bit precision: a dense 4-bit base captures inliers faithfully while outliers are quantized away, and a sparse 4-bit residual then compensates the resulting error only on the most error-critical weight blocks. Those blocks are picked by a Hessian-guided, output-aware criterion: for each input channel the diagonal Hessian estimate weights how strongly that channel's quantization error distorts the layer output, concentrating the residual budget where it matters. To keep execution uniform, ZipperEngine fuses the sparse residual blocks into the dense INT4 GEMM through an overlapped pipeline that interleaves dequant, compute, and reduce-add, avoiding the precision conversion and extra data movement that mixed-precision outlier handling normally incurs.

<a id="trainable-smooth-rotation"></a>
### Trainable SmoothRot · W4A4 · PTQ W+A · 2026-06

<img src="../assets/diagrams/trainable-smooth-rotation.svg" width="640" alt="Trainable SmoothRot diagram">
<p><em>Quantile-robust scale migration plus rotation, with constrained gradient-learned per-channel scales, for W4A4 activation-weight quantization.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights + Activations] --> B[Handle outliers:\nquantile-robust scale migratio]
    B --> C[Quantize weights\nto low-bit]
    B --> D[Quantize activations\nto low-bit]
    C --> E[INT GEMM\ntensor cores]
    D --> E
    E --> F[W4A4\nquantized inference]
```

> This is a post-training method for W4A4 LLM quantization that refines the SmoothRot pipeline, which combines SmoothQuant-style scale migration with a rotation transform to tame activation outliers. The authors argue that fixed, max-based scaling pushes too much outlier energy from activations into weights (over-migration), so they replace the max statistic with a high quantile to set scales more robustly and then fine-tune the per-channel scales with a small constrained gradient search. Both the smoothing strength and the quantile are treated as searchable knobs, and a lightweight training step adjusts the channel scales while keeping the model weights frozen and the transform mathematically equivalent. On LLaMA-3.2-1B they report consistent per-layer error reductions over the SmoothRot baseline, with the largest gains on down-projection layers.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2606.09927](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.09927) |
| Precision | W4A4 |
| Granularity | per-channel activation scales; per-layer rotation applied within decoder blocks |
| Calibration | calibration activations used to fit quantile statistics and to optimize the transform parameters |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | quantile-robust scale migration plus rotation, with constrained gradient-learned per-channel scales to avoid over-migration of outlier energy |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | On LLaMA-3.2-1B at W4A4, selected-layer error improves up to 18.5% over the SmoothRot baseline (12% from joint alpha,q search; 11.1% from quantile-only policy search); replaying the best policy on all decoder-block down-projection layers cuts full-layer mean error from 97.51 to 78.08 (19.9%). No perplexity/accuracy numbers reported. |
| Builds on | [smoothquant](#smoothquant) · [quarot](#quarot) · [spinquant](#spinquant) |
| Related | [flatquant](#flatquant) · [duquant](#duquant) · [affine-quant](#affine-quant) · [omniquant](#omniquant) · [outlier-suppression-plus](#outlier-suppression-plus) |

**How it works:**

SmoothRot-style equivalent transforms factor a linear layer's computation as X W = (X diag(s)^-1 R^-1)(R diag(s) W), where diag(s) migrates per-channel outlier magnitude from activations into weights and R is a rotation that further spreads outlier energy. The paper replaces the usual max-based rule for s with a high-quantile statistic so a few extreme samples do not inflate the scale and cause over-migration, and it jointly searches the smoothing exponent alpha together with the quantile level q. It then adds a constrained gradient-based optimization that learns the per-channel scales directly to minimize layer- wise reconstruction error under W4A4, while the transform remains an exact equivalence so no model weights are modified.

<a id="offq"></a>
### OffQ · W4A4KV4 · PTQ W+A · 2026-06

<img src="../assets/diagrams/offq.svg" width="640" alt="OffQ diagram">
<p><em>Top-1 PCA + rotation concentrate activation outliers into one channel, which is absorbed as a shared offset to enable uniform-grid W4A4KV4.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights + Activations] --> B[Handle outliers:\ntop-1 PCA finds a low-dimensio]
    B --> C[Quantize weights\nto low-bit]
    B --> D[Quantize activations\nto low-bit]
    C --> E[INT GEMM\ntensor cores]
    D --> E
    E --> F[W4A4KV4\nquantized inference]
```

> OffQ is a post-training method for running large language models with 4-bit weights, 4-bit activations, and a 4-bit KV cache. Activations contain a few extreme outlier values that wreck low-bit quantization; OffQ first uses a top-1 PCA to find the small subspace where these outliers live, then applies a rotation that herds the outlier energy into a single channel. Instead of giving that channel special treatment, OffQ subtracts its magnitude as a shared offset, which lowers the spread of the activation values so an ordinary uniform grid can represent them well. Because the grid stays uniform and the precision stays the same everywhere, the result is friendly to standard integer hardware while keeping accuracy close to full precision.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2606.07116](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07116) |
| Precision | W4A4KV4 |
| Granularity | per-channel symmetric weights (GPTQ); per-group asymmetric activations (g=128) |
| Calibration | small calibration set used to estimate the outlier subspace (top-1 PCA) and per-layer offsets, and for GPTQ weight quantization |
| Symmetric? | mixed |
| Outlier handling | top-1 PCA finds a low-dimensional outlier subspace; a rotation concentrates outlier energy into a single channel, whose magnitude is then absorbed as a shared additive offset, shrinking activation variance for uniform-grid quantization |
| Hardware target | NVIDIA GPU (INT4 Tensor Cores; uniform-grid, uniform-precision for deployment) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | W4A4KV4 WikiText PPL 5.77 on LLaMA-2-7B and 6.98 on LLaMA-3-8B (vs SpinQuant 6.0 / QuaRot 7.8, SpinQuant 7.4, ResQ 7.1); ~65.5 avg zero-shot accuracy on LLaMA-3-8B across ARC-e/c, BoolQ, HellaSwag, OBQA, PIQA, SIQA, WinoGrande |
| Builds on | [quarot](#quarot) · [spinquant](#spinquant) · [resq](#resq) · [gptq](#gptq) |
| Related | [flatquant](#flatquant) · [duquant](#duquant) · [quip](#quip) |

**How it works:**

OffQ treats activation outliers as a structured, low-rank phenomenon. A top-1 PCA over calibration activations identifies the dominant outlier direction, and an orthogonal rotation aligns it so that nearly all outlier magnitude collapses into one channel (similar in spirit to Hadamard/learned rotations in QuaRot/SpinQuant, but targeted at the principal outlier component). The magnitude of that concentrated channel is then converted into a shared additive offset that is folded into the linear layer, removing the outlier's contribution to the activation standard deviation. With the distribution flattened, OffQ applies deployment-friendly uniform-grid quantization: per-group (g=128) asymmetric activations and per-channel symmetric GPTQ weights, extending to the KV cache for full W4A4KV4.

<a id="fair-calib"></a>
### FAIR-Calib · W4A4 · PTQ W+A · 2026-06

<img src="../assets/diagrams/fair-calib.svg" width="640" alt="FAIR-Calib diagram">
<p><em>FAIR-Calib: Stage I teacher probing of frontier/reliability position priors feeds a Stage II instability-reweighted hidden-state MSE calibration for W4A4 diffusion-LLM quantization.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights + Activations] --> B[Handle outliers:\naffine flattening transforms i]
    B --> C[Quantize weights\nto low-bit]
    B --> D[Quantize activations\nto low-bit]
    C --> E[INT GEMM\ntensor cores]
    D --> E
    E --> F[W4A4\nquantized inference]
```

> FAIR-Calib is a post-training quantization recipe built specifically for diffusion language models, which generate text by iteratively refining masked tokens and then irreversibly commit them. The authors observe that low-bit quantization can flip borderline token choices right at the model's write frontier, and because those choices get locked in, the error propagates through the rest of the sequence. The method first probes a full-precision teacher to learn which token positions are most fragile, then runs a layer-wise calibration that weights the per-position reconstruction error so that fragile frontier positions are protected more strongly. It works at W4A4 for both weights and activations and avoids running full diffusion rollouts during calibration.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICML 2026** |
| Paper | [2606.06547](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06547) |
| Precision | W4A4 |
| Granularity | per-channel symmetric weights, per-token symmetric activations |
| Calibration | off-policy layer-wise calibration minimizing reweighted hidden-state MSE; ~512 GSM8K prompts for frontier probing plus 128 WikiText-2 sequences, 1024-token calibration length |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | affine flattening transforms inherited from FlatQuant, combined with instability-reweighting that protects fragile write-frontier token decisions |
| Hardware target | GPU (low-bit W4A4 inference for diffusion LLMs) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | On LLaDA-Base W4A4, 61.09% average over 10 benchmarks (vs 59.37% FlatQuant, 58.78% QuaRot); LLaDA-1.5 72.75% avg; Dream-Base 64.64% avg (vs 62.08% FlatQuant); GSM8K 64.75% vs 57.24% on LLaDA-Base |
| Builds on | [flatquant](#flatquant) · [quarot](#quarot) |
| Related | [gptq](#gptq) · [omniquant](#omniquant) |

**How it works:**

Stage I probes a full-precision teacher model to build a position prior that combines how often a position lands on the decoding frontier with its reliability across masked diffusion steps. Stage II performs off-policy, layer-wise calibration that minimizes a hidden-state MSE reweighted by this prior, so quantization error is spent preferentially on protecting unstable frontier-token decisions rather than already-stable ones. The reweighted objective is theoretically motivated as a surrogate for minimizing output KL divergence, and the off- policy formulation avoids expensive end-to-end diffusion sampling during calibration. It layers on top of FlatQuant-style affine flattening transforms to keep weights and activations quantization-friendly at W4A4.

---

<a id="morphoquant"></a>
### MorphoQuant · W4A4 · PTQ W+A · 2026-06

<img src="../assets/diagrams/morphoquant.svg" width="640" alt="MorphoQuant diagram">
<p><em>MorphoQuant for W4A4 omni-modal LLMs: DABC offloads per-channel long-tailed outliers into channel biases, and MDQFO co-optimizes the quantization grid with the outlier masks for consistent alignment across modalities.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights + Activations] --> B[Handle outliers:\nDistribution-Aware Bias Compen]
    B --> C[Quantize weights\nto low-bit]
    B --> D[Quantize activations\nto low-bit]
    C --> E[INT GEMM\ntensor cores]
    D --> E
    E --> F[W4A4\nquantized inference]
```

> MorphoQuant is a post-training method for compressing omni-modal LLMs (models that jointly handle text, image, audio, and video, such as Qwen2.5-Omni) down to 4-bit weights and 4-bit activations. The difficulty it targets is that different modalities produce very different activation distributions and outlier patterns, so a single quantizer tuned for one modality wrecks accuracy on the others. It tackles this in two parts: first it shifts the heavy long- tailed outliers in each channel into a learned per-channel bias term, leaving a tighter, more uniform set of values that 4-bit quantization can represent well; second it jointly tunes the quantization grid and the outlier-selection masks so the same scheme stays well- aligned across all modalities. On Qwen2.5-Omni it reports W4A4 accuracy that edges past the weight-only W4A16 reference on ScienceQA while also covering MMMU and Video-MME, using only a small 128-sample calibration set and no gradient training of the base model.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2606.04349](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04349) |
| Precision | W4A4 |
| Granularity | per-channel (channel-wise weights and activations; per-channel bias compensation with per-channel outlier masks) |
| Calibration | 128 multimodal samples (calibration set for the search/optimization phase) |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | Distribution-Aware Bias Compensation (DABC): selectively absorbs long-tailed outliers into channel-wise biases so the remaining dense inliers can be discretized at high precision |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | W4A4 reaches 76.63% on ScienceQA with Qwen2.5-Omni, above the 75.88% W4A16 QLoRA baseline; also evaluated on MMMU and Video-MME |
| Builds on | [smoothquant](#smoothquant) · [awq](#awq) · [omniquant](#omniquant) · [atom](#atom) · [outlier-suppression-plus](#outlier-suppression-plus) |
| Related | [duquant](#duquant) · [quarot](#quarot) · [atom](#atom) · [omniquant](#omniquant) |

**How it works:**

Two components address cross-modal distribution heterogeneity. (1) Distribution-Aware Bias Compensation (DABC) identifies long-tailed outliers per channel and absorbs them into a channel-wise bias instead of forcing them through the low-bit quantizer; this protects outlier magnitudes while letting the dense inlier mass be discretized at full effective resolution. (2) Morphology-Directed Quantization Function Optimization (MDQFO) co-optimizes the quantization grid (scales/clipping) together with the per-channel outlier bias masks so the quantizer is consistently aligned across the differing modal distributions. The combination is a calibration-only PTQ pipeline (128 multimodal samples) that needs no fine- tuning of the model weights.

<a id="star-quant"></a>
### STaR-Quant · W4A4 · PTQ W+A · 2026-06

<img src="../assets/diagrams/star-quant.svg" width="640" alt="STaR-Quant diagram">
<p><em>STaR-Quant: State-Guided Activation Transformation splits masked/unmasked tokens into separate activation spaces with a shared weight transform, and Temporal Attention Compensation applies a block-diagonal affine correction to quantized attention across denoising steps.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights + Activations] --> B[Handle outliers:\nstate-guided activation transf]
    B --> C[Quantize weights\nto low-bit]
    B --> D[Quantize activations\nto low-bit]
    C --> E[INT GEMM\ntensor cores]
    D --> E
    E --> F[W4A4\nquantized inference]
```

> STaR-Quant is a post-training quantization recipe aimed specifically at diffusion language models (DLLMs), which produce text by iteratively un-masking tokens rather than decoding left to right. The authors observe two problems that ordinary LLM quantizers miss: masked and unmasked tokens have very different activation ranges within a single denoising step, and small quantization errors compound over the many denoising iterations. To handle the first, masked and unmasked tokens are routed through separate activation transformation spaces while sharing one static weight-side transform, so a single quantized weight matrix still serves both. To handle the second, a lightweight block-diagonal affine correction is applied to the quantized attention to keep the attention output close to its full-precision counterpart across steps. The method needs only a small calibration set and no fine-tuning, and targets 4-bit weight and 4-bit activation deployment.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2606.04945](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04945) |
| Precision | W4A4 |
| Granularity | per-token activations, per-channel weights |
| Calibration | 128 segments sampled from WinoGrande |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | state-guided activation transformation (separate transform spaces for masked vs unmasked tokens) plus a unified static weight-side transformation; temporal attention compensation corrects quantized attention with a block-diagonal affine map |
| Hardware target | GPU (INT4 weight-activation kernels); reports up to 1.69x speedup and 3.14x memory saving over FP16 |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | W4A4 on LLaDA-8B reaches 49.12 average accuracy across 9 benchmarks (TruthfulQA-MC2, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag, WinoGrande, PIQA, MMLU, C-EVAL, GSM8K, HumanEval), +2.78 over the DLLMQuant++ baseline; consistent gains also reported on LLaDA-1.5-8B and Dream 7B |
| Builds on | [awq](#awq) · [quarot](#quarot) |
| Related | [smoothquant](#smoothquant) · [rtn](#rtn) |

**How it works:**

STaR-Quant tackles two failure modes unique to masked-denoising diffusion LLMs. State-Guided Activation Transformation (SGAT) assigns masked and unmasked tokens to distinct activation transformation spaces (since their distributions differ within a step) but pairs them with one shared static weight-side transformation, so weights remain singly quantized while activations are normalized per state. Temporal Attention Compensation (TAC) then corrects the quantized attention representation through a lightweight block-diagonal affine mapping, counteracting the error that otherwise accumulates over the iterative denoising trajectory. Together these enable W4A4 weight-activation quantization with per-token activation and per- channel weight scales, asymmetric, calibrated on 128 WinoGrande segments without any gradient training.

---

<a id="infoquant"></a>
### InfoQuant · W4A4KV4 · PTQ W+A · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/infoquant.svg" width="640" alt="InfoQuant diagram">
<p><em>PSOT reshapes activations to a tighter range with higher dispersion (info-theoretic target) before W4A4KV4 quantization; outlier tokens are adaptively discounted during fitting.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights + Activations] --> B[Handle outliers:\nPeak Suppression Orthogonal Tr]
    B --> C[Quantize weights\nto low-bit]
    B --> D[Quantize activations\nto low-bit]
    C --> E[INT GEMM\ntensor cores]
    D --> E
    E --> F[W4A4KV4\nquantized inference]
```

> InfoQuant is a training-free post-training method for running LLMs with 4-bit weights, activations, and KV cache. Its starting point is that simply making activations look numerically smoother does not make them easy to quantize: a low-bit uniform grid wastes precision when the range stays wide or when most values pile up near the mean. The authors analyze quantization error from an information-theoretic angle and argue that good activations need both a tighter range and enough spread across that range. They then apply learned orthogonal transformations to reshape activations toward that target before quantizing, with an extra step that decides which outlier tokens to down-weight while fitting the transforms so the fit stays stable. The transforms are orthogonal, so they can be folded into the surrounding weight matrices and add no extra inference cost.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.26175](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26175) |
| Code | [LLIKKE/InfoQuant](https://github.com/LLIKKE/InfoQuant) |
| Precision | W4A4KV4 |
| Granularity | per-token (activations); per-channel weights with GPTQ, group size 128 |
| Calibration | small unlabeled calibration set used to optimize the orthogonal transforms and clipping |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | Peak Suppression Orthogonal Transformation that shrinks activation range while raising dispersion, plus adaptive outlier-token selection during optimization |
| Hardware target | GPU (INT4 inference; transforms absorbed into weights, orthogonal so matmul-preserving) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | W4A4KV4 LLaMA-2-7B: 5.89 WikiText-2 ppl / 63.16% avg zero-shot; LLaMA-2-13B: 5.18 ppl / 66.33% avg; preserves ~97% of FP accuracy on average |
| Builds on | [quarot](#quarot) · [spinquant](#spinquant) · [gptq](#gptq) |
| Related | [quarot](#quarot) · [spinquant](#spinquant) · [duquant](#duquant) · [flatquant](#flatquant) |

**How it works:**

InfoQuant recasts activation transformation as quantizer-facing distribution design. An information-theoretic analysis of uniform-quantizer error shows that quantization-friendly activations should jointly minimize numerical range and maximize normalized dispersion within that range. To produce such distributions it introduces Peak Suppression Orthogonal Transformation (PSOT): orthogonal matrices optimized with a peak-suppression objective that reduces the activation range while increasing dispersion, kept matmul-preserving so they fold into adjacent weights offline. An adaptive outlier-token selection scheme identifies and discounts the most disruptive tokens during PSOT optimization to make the fit robust, and a learnable activation clipping step (LAC) further tunes the quantization range. Weights are quantized per-channel with GPTQ.

---

<a id="torq"></a>
### TORQ · W4A4 (both weights and activations in MXFP4 e2m1) · PTQ W+A · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/torq.svg" width="640" alt="TORQ diagram">
<p><em>TORQ: macro inter-block rotation (Schur-Horn) equalizes variance across MXFP4 blocks; micro intra-block rotation (max-entropy) fills the FP4 codebook.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights + Activations] --> B[Handle outliers:\ntwo-level orthogonal rotation:]
    B --> C[Quantize weights\nto low-bit]
    B --> D[Quantize activations\nto low-bit]
    C --> E[INT GEMM\ntensor cores]
    D --> E
    E --> F[W4A4 both weights and activations in MXFP4 e2m1\nquantized inference]
```

> TORQ is a training-free post-training method for quantizing both the weights and activations of an LLM to the MXFP4 microscaling block format (4-bit floating point, e2m1, with each block of 32 elements sharing one 8-bit exponent). The authors observe that naively casting activations to MXFP4 fails in two ways: some blocks carry far more variance than others, and within a block the limited set of FP4 codewords is poorly used. TORQ fixes this with two orthogonal rotations applied before quantization. The first rotation rebalances variance evenly across blocks, and the second reshapes the distribution inside each block so it covers the available codewords more fully. Because the rotations are orthogonal they can be folded into the surrounding weight matrices, so inference stays in plain MXFP4 with no extra runtime cost while recovering most of the accuracy lost by direct rounding.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.19561](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19561) |
| Precision | W4A4 (both weights and activations in MXFP4 e2m1) |
| Granularity | MXFP4 blocks of 32 elements with a shared E8M0 exponent scale |
| Calibration | 128 text segments from WikiText2 |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | two-level orthogonal rotation: inter-block (Schur-Horn) rotation equalizes variance across blocks, intra-block (max-entropy) rotation spreads values across the FP4 codebook |
| Hardware target | MX-capable GPUs (NVIDIA Blackwell with native MXFP4 Tensor Cores) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | WikiText2 PPL +2.43 on LLaMA3-8B (8.57 vs 6.14 BF16); +0.82 on Qwen3-32B (8.43 vs 7.61 BF16); zero-shot avg accuracy gap ~-1.19% on Qwen3-32B (73.63% vs 74.82% BF16) |
| Builds on | [quarot](#quarot) · [spinquant](#spinquant) · [mx-formats](#mx-formats) |
| Related | [quarot](#quarot) · [spinquant](#spinquant) · [duquant](#duquant) · [flatquant](#flatquant) · [nvfp4](#nvfp4) · [mx-formats](#mx-formats) |

**How it works:**

TORQ targets two structural imbalances in MXFP4 activation quantization: inter-block variance imbalance (high-variance blocks dominate the shared-scale error) and intra-block codebook under-utilization. It applies a macro-equilibrium rotation, constructed per token position across the blocks using the Schur-Horn theorem, to redistribute variance uniformly over the 32-element MXFP4 blocks so no single block's dynamic range dominates its shared E8M0 scale. It then applies a micro-alignment rotation inside each block, optimized with a codebook-occupancy / maximum-entropy objective, so activation values are spread across the e2m1 codeword set rather than clustering. Both transformations are orthogonal, so they preserve the matmul exactly and are absorbed into adjacent weights offline, keeping inference in standard MXFP4. The procedure is training-free and uses 128 WikiText2 calibration segments.

<a id="splitq"></a>
### SplitQ · W4A8 · PTQ W+A · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/splitq.svg" width="640" alt="SplitQ diagram">
<p><em>SplitQ separates text-specific and vision-specific outlier channels (MOCD), then applies dual per-modality low-rank affine correction branches (ACC) to enable low-bit (down to W3A2) weight+activation quantization of VLMs.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights + Activations] --> B[Handle outliers:\nmodality-specific outlier chan]
    B --> C[Quantize weights\nto low-bit]
    B --> D[Quantize activations\nto low-bit]
    C --> E[INT GEMM\ntensor cores]
    D --> E
    E --> F[W4A8\nquantized inference]
```

> SplitQ is a post-training quantization method aimed at vision-language models, where the activations coming from text tokens and from image tokens have very different statistics. The authors observe that the extreme-valued (outlier) channels for the two modalities sit in different places, so treating all channels uniformly hurts low-bit accuracy. SplitQ first sorts channels into groups that are shared, text-only, or vision-only based on per-modality scoring, and handles each group's outliers separately. It then attaches two small learnable branches, one per modality, that are fit on a calibration set to undo the error introduced by quantizing to as few as 3 or 4 bits, letting weights and activations both be pushed to low precision with limited accuracy loss.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.19929](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19929) |
| Code | [EMVision-NK/SplitQ](https://github.com/EMVision-NK/SplitQ) |
| Precision | W4A8 |
| Granularity | per-channel modality-specific decoupling (activations); per-group/per-layer learned affine (weights) |
| Calibration | small multimodal calibration set to fit the learnable correction branches |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | modality-specific outlier channel decoupling (MOCD): isolates salient outlier channels separately for the text and vision modalities, then applies per-modality low-rank affine correction (ACC) |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | Qwen2.5-VL-7B retains 93.5% of FP16 (69.5 vs 74.3 avg) at W3A3 and 98.9% (73.5) at W4A4 across MMMU, OCRBench, TextVQA, SEED-Bench, VizWiz, ScienceQA |
| Builds on | [smoothquant](#smoothquant) · [flatquant](#flatquant) |
| Related | [duquant](#duquant) · [omniquant](#omniquant) |

**How it works:**

Modality-specific Outlier Channel Decoupling (MOCD) scores each channel separately per modality (vision channels by maximum absolute activation, text channels by percentile rank within each token plus within-cluster variance) and partitions channels into three disjoint sets: modality-compatible, text-specific, and vision-specific, so each modality's outliers are isolated cheaply. Adaptive Cross-Modal Calibration (ACC) then adds dual lightweight learnable low-rank branches (a FlatQuant-style learnable affine parameterization, with separate matrices Us/Vs for shared and Uc/Vc for modality channels) that dynamically compensate for modality-induced quantization error. Together they let weights and activations be quantized to W4A8 down to W3A2 while preserving most of FP16 accuracy on VLM benchmarks.

---

<a id="dirotq"></a>
### DiRotQ · W4A4 · PTQ W+A · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/dirotq.svg" width="640" alt="DiRotQ diagram">
<p><em>PCA-derived rotation aligns dominant activation variance to a low-rank subspace kept at higher precision; remaining coordinates and weights are quantized to 4-bit (W4A4).</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights + Activations] --> B[Handle outliers:\nPCA-derived orthogonal rotatio]
    B --> C[Quantize weights\nto low-bit]
    B --> D[Quantize activations\nto low-bit]
    C --> E[INT GEMM\ntensor cores]
    D --> E
    E --> F[W4A4\nquantized inference]
```

> DiRotQ is a post-training scheme for running diffusion transformers (image generators like PixArt-Sigma and FLUX) with both weights and activations at 4 bits. The trouble with 4-bit activations in these models is that a few directions in the activation space carry most of the variance, and crushing them to 4 bits wrecks image quality. DiRotQ uses PCA on calibration activations to find that high-variance, low-rank subspace, then rotates each layer's activations into the PCA basis so the important directions line up with a handful of coordinates. Those coefficients are kept at higher precision while everything else drops to 4-bit, and weights are quantized with a GPTQ-style pass. Because the rotation is orthogonal it leaves the layer's math unchanged, so it folds into the weights with no extra runtime cost. The authors also add a vision-language-model-based judge to score perceptual quality under aggressive compression, and report 4-bit image quality close to higher-precision baselines with measurable memory and latency wins.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.16732](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16732) |
| Precision | W4A4 |
| Granularity | per-token activations (rotated into PCA basis); per-group weights (GPTQ) |
| Calibration | calibration set used to estimate the activation covariance / PCA rotation and to run GPTQ weight quantization |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | PCA-derived orthogonal rotation that concentrates dominant activation variance into a low-rank subspace whose coefficients are kept at higher precision; remaining components quantized to 4-bit |
| Hardware target | NVIDIA GPU (INT4); reported on RTX 4090 |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | PixArt-Sigma on MJHQ-30K at W4A4: FID 15.9 / PSNR 19.1 dB, vs SVDQuant FID 18.9 / PSNR 17.6; FLUX.1-dev: 2.1x memory reduction and 2.3x speedup vs BF16 on an RTX 4090 |
| Builds on | [quarot](#quarot) · [spinquant](#spinquant) · [gptq](#gptq) |
| Related | [quarot](#quarot) · [spinquant](#spinquant) · [flatquant](#flatquant) · [duquant](#duquant) |

**How it works:**

For each linear layer Y = XW, DiRotQ computes a calibration-time PCA of the activations X and forms an orthogonal rotation R whose leading axes align with the principal (high- variance) directions. Activations are rotated as XR and weights pre-rotated as R^T W offline; since R R^T = I the layer output is preserved exactly. After rotation the dominant variance lives in a small set of leading coordinates, which are assigned higher precision, while the remaining (now low-variance) coordinates are quantized to 4 bits per-token. Weights are quantized to 4 bits with a GPTQ-style error-compensating pass. This mixed- precision-in-the-rotated-basis design targets the heavy-tailed, low-rank activation structure specific to diffusion transformers rather than relying on a fixed random Hadamard rotation.

<a id="conqur"></a>
### ConQuR · W4A4 · PTQ W+A · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/conqur.svg" width="640" alt="ConQuR diagram">
<p><em>Closed-form orthogonal Procrustes rotations align activations to hypercube corners, evenly spreading energy for W4A4.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights + Activations] --> B[Handle outliers:\northogonal rotations that alig]
    B --> C[Quantize weights\nto low-bit]
    B --> D[Quantize activations\nto low-bit]
    C --> E[INT GEMM\ntensor cores]
    D --> E
    E --> F[W4A4\nquantized inference]
```

> ConQuR is a post-training calibration method for low-bit weight-activation quantization of LLMs that tackles activation outliers using learned orthogonal rotations. Instead of training rotations with gradient descent over the orthogonal group or relying on large stored activation corpora, it frames the goal as aligning normalized activations with the corners of an inscribed hypercube so that activation energy is spread evenly across dimensions. This alignment objective has a closed-form solution through the orthogonal Procrustes problem, making each rotation update cheap. An online calibration loop refines the rotations as each calibration sample passes through, so activations never need to be written to disk and the rotations adapt to the quantized activation distribution.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.10793](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10793) |
| Precision | W4A4 |
| Granularity | per-channel/per-group (weights); per-token (activations) |
| Calibration | online rotation calibration over a small calibration set; rotations updated as samples are processed without storing activations on disk |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | orthogonal rotations that align normalized activations with the corners of an inscribed hypercube, spreading activation energy evenly across dimensions to suppress outliers |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | W4A4 WikiText-2 perplexity around 6.04 (Llama-2-7B) and 6.22 (Llama-3-8B); competitive with or better than prior rotation methods across perplexity and commonsense reasoning |
| Builds on | [quarot](#quarot) · [spinquant](#spinquant) |
| Related | [quarot](#quarot) · [spinquant](#spinquant) · [flatquant](#flatquant) · [duquant](#duquant) |

**How it works:**

ConQuR learns per-layer orthogonal rotation matrices for activation quantization by minimizing the distance between normalized (rotated) activations and the corners of an inscribed hypercube, which encourages a balanced spread of magnitude across channels and reduces the dynamic range that outliers create. Because the target is a set of corner points, the optimal rotation is obtained in closed form via the orthogonal Procrustes problem (SVD of the cross-covariance), avoiding gradient-based optimization on the Stiefel/orthogonal manifold used by methods like SpinQuant. Calibration is performed online: rotations are incrementally updated as calibration samples stream through the network, so no activation corpus is stored and the rotations adapt to the actual quantized-activation statistics.

---

<a id="loraq"></a>
### LoRaQ · W4A8 · PTQ W+A · 2026-04

<img src="../assets/diagrams/loraq.svg" width="640" alt="LoRaQ diagram">
<p><em>LoRaQ: a data-free low-rank branch absorbs 4-bit quantization error and is itself quantized for a fully sub-16-bit diffusion-transformer pipeline.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights + Activations] --> B[Handle outliers:\nlow-rank auxiliary branch that]
    B --> C[Quantize weights\nto low-bit]
    B --> D[Quantize activations\nto low-bit]
    C --> E[INT GEMM\ntensor cores]
    D --> E
    E --> F[W4A8\nquantized inference]
```

> LoRaQ quantizes large diffusion transformers to 4-bit by attaching a small low-rank linear branch alongside each quantized layer to soak up the error introduced by rounding the main weights. Unlike earlier low-rank-compensation schemes that keep this auxiliary branch in 16-bit precision and need a calibration dataset to initialize it, LoRaQ computes the branch factors in closed form without any data and then quantizes the branch itself down to 8-, 6-, or 4-bit. The result is a pipeline where every part runs below 16 bits while preserving image quality. On PixArt-Sigma and SANA, it matches or beats prior low-rank methods at the same memory budget.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2604.18117](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.18117) |
| Precision | W4A8 |
| Granularity | block-wise: SINT4 in blocks of 64 with FP16 per-block scale; MX format in blocks of 32 with 8-bit power-of-two scale |
| Calibration | data-free closed-form optimization of the low-rank compensation factors (no calibration set); adopts SmoothQuant-style activation smoothing as a complementary step |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | low-rank auxiliary branch that absorbs the high-magnitude quantization-error components, combined with activation smoothing and rotation between the low-rank factors |
| Hardware target | GPU / resource-constrained accelerators |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | PixArt-Sigma SINT4 (20 steps): FID 16.9, ImageReward 0.898, LPIPS 0.309 on MJHQ; SANA-1.6B SINT4: FID 16.1, ImageReward 1.09, LPIPS 0.182 on MJHQ |
| Related | [smoothquant](#smoothquant) · [quarot](#quarot) · [flatquant](#flatquant) |

**How it works:**

For each linear layer, LoRaQ models the quantization residual W - Q(W) and fits a low-rank approximation U V^T to it in closed form (data-free), so the branch output U V^T x compensates the error of the quantized main path Q(W) x. Because the compensation is derived analytically rather than calibrated on data, the low-rank factors can themselves be quantized (W8A8, W6A6, or W4A8) without re-introducing large error, removing the high- precision (W16A16) branch assumed by prior work and yielding a fully sub-16-bit forward pass. It additionally adopts SmoothQuant-style activation smoothing and optimizes rotations between the low-rank factors to better condition the residual before quantization.

<a id="osc"></a>
### OSC · W4A4 · PTQ W+A · 2026-04

<img src="../assets/diagrams/osc.svg" width="640" alt="OSC diagram">
<p><em>OSC splits each activation matrix into a fixed set of profiled outlier channels (routed through an FP16 branch GEMM after sub-tensor extraction) and the remaining channels (4-bit GEMM); results are merged, with diffuse-outlier layers falling back to higher precision.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 Weights + Activations] --> B[Handle outliers:\noffline profiling identifies f]
    B --> C[Quantize weights\nto low-bit]
    B --> D[Quantize activations\nto low-bit]
    C --> E[INT GEMM\ntensor cores]
    D --> E
    E --> F[W4A4\nquantized inference]
```

> OSC is a post-training 4-bit weight and activation quantization method that handles the activation outliers which normally break low-bit formats. It relies on the observation that the largest outliers sit in the same fixed set of channels across all tokens, so it profiles these channels offline and routes them through a separate high-precision path while everything else goes through fast 4-bit matrix multiplication. The outlier channels are gathered into a small dense tensor at runtime to keep the high-precision work cheap, and layers whose outliers are too spread out are switched to a higher-precision format. This avoids the runtime rotation or dynamic-detection overhead of earlier outlier-handling schemes while keeping accuracy close to full precision.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2604.12782](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.12782) |
| Precision | W4A4 |
| Granularity | group-wise (group sizes 16/32/64, primary G=32), per-group outlier-channel identification |
| Calibration | 3 sequences of 512 tokens randomly sampled from the Pile dataset, used for offline outlier-channel profiling |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | offline profiling identifies fixed high-magnitude outlier channels, which are extracted into a compact dense sub-tensor and computed in a separate FP16 branch GEMM while the rest runs in a 4-bit GEMM; layers with diffused outliers fall back to higher precision |
| Hardware target | modern AI accelerator with FP16:FP8:FP4 throughput ratio of 1:2:4; 1.78x speedup over W8A8 GEMM baseline |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | Average accuracy drop of 2.19 points on Qwen3-8B and 1.12 points on Qwen3-30B-A3B vs FP16, averaged over MMLU (5-shot), GSM8K (8-shot), and ARC-Challenge/Easy (25-shot) |
| Builds on | [outlier-suppression](#outlier-suppression) · [atom](#atom) · [nvfp4](#nvfp4) · [mx-formats](#mx-formats) |
| Related | [quarot](#quarot) · [spinquant](#spinquant) · [smoothquant](#smoothquant) · [duquant](#duquant) · [flatquant](#flatquant) · [qserve](#qserve) · [owq](#owq) · [spqr](#spqr) |

**How it works:**

OSC runs a dual-path GEMM: a low-precision 4-bit path for ordinary activation channels and a 16-bit branch path for outlier channels. An offline group-wise profiling stage finds the channels that persistently hold high-magnitude outliers and sets layer-wise thresholds; at inference these scattered channels are coalesced via structured sub-tensor extraction into a compact dense tensor so the high-precision branch stays small. A static suppression pipeline fuses both paths into the quantized matmul, and an adaptive fallback promotes layers with diffuse (non-clustered) outliers to a higher-precision format rather than forcing them into the 4-bit path. Because the outlier channels are fixed and determined offline, there is no per-token rotation or dynamic-detection cost at runtime.
