
> BitDistiller makes sub-4-bit (2-bit and 3-bit) quantization practical by combining GPTQ initialization with self-distillation: the FP16 version of the same model serves as a teacher, and the quantized model is fine-tuned to match the teacher's output distributions (using KL divergence on logits). Because the teacher and student have the same architecture, this is "self-distillation." The distillation training signal is much richer than cross-entropy on labels, recovering quality that would otherwise be lost at extreme bit-widths.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ACL 2024** |
| Paper | [2402.10631](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.10631) |
| Precision | W2/W3 A16 |
| Granularity | per-group |
| Calibration | fine-tuning on public text corpus |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | self-distillation from FP16 teacher; GPTQ initialization of quantized weights |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | yes |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | significantly better than PTQ at W2/W3; approaches W4 PTQ quality at W3 |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) · [qlora](#qlora) |
| Related | [llm-qat](#llm-qat) · [efficientqat](#efficientqat) · [pv-tuning](#pv-tuning) |

**How it works:**

BitDistiller starts from a GPTQ-quantized model (good PTQ initialization) and fine-tunes it with a distillation loss: L = KL( softmax(z_teacher / T) ‖ softmax(z_student / T) ) where z are logits, T is temperature, and the teacher is the FP16 base model. The quantized weights (INT2/INT3) are kept frozen in their quantized form; the quantization scales (per-group) are the only parameters that get gradient updates from the distillation loss. This is lighter than full QAT (no STE over weights), just learning optimal scale parameters. The combination of GPTQ init + self-distillation scale training achieves W3 quality close to W4 GPTQ.

---

<a id="ir-qlora"></a>
### IR-QLoRA · W4A16 (NF4 base) + BF16 LoRA · QAT / QFT · 2024-02

<img src="../assets/diagrams/ir-qlora.svg" width="640" alt="IR-QLoRA diagram">
<p><em>Per-layer NF4 level adaptation (information calibration) + confidence-guided LoRA updates.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[QLoRA model\nINT4 + LoRA] --> B[Measure inter-layer\nfeature correlation]
    B --> C[Identify redundant\nLoRA rank dimensions]
    C --> D[Prune redundant\nrank components]
    D --> E[Reinitialize pruned\nrank with new directions]
    E --> F{Iterations done?}
    F -->|no| B
    F -->|yes| G[Compact LoRA\nbetter QLoRA baseline]
```

> IR-QLoRA identifies that the NF4 quantization grid used in QLoRA is fixed (based on the standard normal distribution) and may not be optimal for every layer's actual weight distribution. IR-QLoRA adapts the NF4 quantization levels per layer by minimizing the information loss (measured by the KL divergence between the original and quantized weight distributions), and additionally uses a confidence-guided LoRA training approach where LoRA updates are weighted by the model's prediction confidence to avoid wasted gradient on easy examples.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICML 2024** |
| Paper | [2402.05445](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.05445) |
| Precision | W4A16 (NF4 base) + BF16 LoRA |
| Granularity | per-group |
| Calibration | fine-tuning data |
| Symmetric? | n/a (NF4) |
| Outlier handling | information calibration of NF4 quantization levels per layer; confidence-guided LoRA |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | yes |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | consistently outperforms QLoRA on downstream tasks |
| Builds on | [qlora](#qlora) · [loftq](#loftq) |
| Related | [qlora](#qlora) · [loftq](#loftq) · [apiq](#apiq) |

**How it works:**

Two components: (1) Information Calibration - the NF4 quantization levels for each layer are shifted slightly from the standard-normal quantiles to the empirical quantiles of that layer's actual weight distribution, minimizing KL divergence between Q(W) and W. This is a cheap per-layer histogram operation requiring no data. (2) Confidence-guided LoRA - during fine-tuning, the LoRA gradient for each training example is scaled by (1 − p(y|x)), the model's uncertainty, focusing updates on harder examples where the quantized model is most uncertain.

---

<a id="apiq"></a>
### ApiQ · W2A16 (base) + BF16 LoRA · QAT / QFT · 2024-02

<img src="../assets/diagrams/apiq.svg" width="640" alt="ApiQ diagram">
<p><em>Activation-aware LoRA initialization for 2-bit base - minimizes layer-wise output error.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Calibration activations] --> B[Compute activation\ncovariance stats]
    B --> C[Design per-layer\nquantization grid]
    C --> D[Grid aligned to\nactivation principal components]
    D --> E[Quantize W on\naligned grid]
    E --> F[Better W4A16\nactivation-aware grid]
```

> ApiQ extends the QLoRA/LoftQ approach to the extreme 2-bit regime. At 2 bits, the quantization error of the base model is so large that naive QLoRA (random LoRA init + 2-bit base) fails to converge to good quality. ApiQ addresses this by using an activation-aware initialization of the LoRA adapters that directly minimizes the output error of each layer before fine-tuning starts, analogous to LoftQ but tailored to the higher-error 2-bit case and using activations rather than pure weight error.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **EMNLP 2024** |
| Paper | [2402.05898](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.05898) |
| Precision | W2A16 (base) + BF16 LoRA |
| Granularity | per-group |
| Calibration | fine-tuning data |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | activation-aware initialization of LoRA adapters for 2-bit quantized base |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | yes |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | competitive fine-tuning quality at 2-bit base; better than QLoRA+2-bit GPTQ |
| Builds on | [qlora](#qlora) · [loftq](#loftq) |
| Related | [qlora](#qlora) · [loftq](#loftq) · [ir-qlora](#ir-qlora) |

**How it works:**

ApiQ initializes LoRA adapters by minimizing the layer-wise output error: ‖ W_FP16 X − (Q(W) + B·A) X ‖² where X is drawn from a calibration set. This is solved approximately by taking B·A = W_FP16 − Q(W) (the quantization error) and computing the rank-r approximation via SVD of the activation-weighted error (X^T (W − Q(W))^T). The key difference from LoftQ: the initialization accounts for the activation distribution X, not just the weight matrix. For 2-bit models where ‖ W − Q(W) ‖ is large, this better initialization is critical for convergence.

---

<a id="loftq"></a>
### LoftQ · W4A16 (quantized base) + BF16 LoRA · QAT / QFT · 2023-10

<img src="../assets/diagrams/loftq.svg" width="640" alt="LoftQ diagram">
<p><em>Alternating quantization of residual and SVD initialization of LoRA adapters.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 pretrained W] --> B[SVD: W approx A times B\nlow-rank init]
    B --> C[Quantize W - A times B\nresidual to INT4]
    C --> D[New residual\nR = W - Q_int4]
    D --> E[SVD of R to update\nA and B]
    E --> F{Iterations done?}
    F -->|no| C
    F -->|yes| G[Aligned INT4 base\n+ LoRA A, B init]
    G --> H[Fine-tune A, B\nbetter start than random]
```

> LoftQ improves on QLoRA by better initializing the LoRA adapters. In QLoRA, LoRA adapters start from random initialization and must both learn the task and compensate for the quantization error in the base model. LoftQ alternately optimizes the quantization of the base model and the initialization of LoRA adapters so that (quantized base + LoRA adapters) closely approximates the original FP16 weights before fine-tuning begins. This warm-start initialization reduces the gap between the quantized-and-adapter model and FP16, giving better convergence and higher accuracy at the same number of fine-tuning steps.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICLR 2024** |
| Paper | [2310.08659](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.08659) |
| Code | [yxli2123/LoftQ](https://github.com/yxli2123/LoftQ) |
| Precision | W4A16 (quantized base) + BF16 LoRA |
| Granularity | per-group |
| Calibration | none for quantization phase |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | SVD-initialized LoRA adapters compensate for quantization error before fine-tuning starts |
| Hardware target | GPU (bitsandbytes or GPTQ backend) |
| Training needed? | yes |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | better starting point than QLoRA for fine-tuning; reduces quantization error propagation |
| Builds on | [qlora](#qlora) · [gptq](#gptq) |
| Related | [qlora](#qlora) · [qa-lora](#qa-lora) · [apiq](#apiq) · [ir-qlora](#ir-qlora) |

**How it works:**

LoftQ alternates between two steps: (1) Fix LoRA adapters (A, B) and quantize the residual W − B·A with any PTQ method (e.g. NF4 or GPTQ) to get Q̂(W − B·A). (2) Fix the quantization Q̂ and update (A, B) via SVD to minimize ‖ W − Q̂ − B·A ‖_F. After N iterations (typically 5), the LoRA adapters are initialized to compensate for the quantization error, and the base is quantized accordingly. Fine-tuning then starts from this better initialization. The alternating optimization is fast (no gradients - just SVD and PTQ).

---

<a id="qa-lora"></a>
### QA-LoRA · W4A16 (group-wise) · QAT / QFT · 2023-09

<img src="../assets/diagrams/qa-lora.svg" width="640" alt="QA-LoRA diagram">
<p><em>Group-wise LoRA adapters that absorb cleanly into quantization parameters at merge.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[INT4 quantized\nbase model] --> B[Group-wise LoRA:\nrank = group size]
    B --> C[Adapter A, B have\nsame granularity as quant groups]
    C --> D[Fine-tune adapters\nwith QA-aware gradients]
    D --> E[Merge A, B into\nquantized weights]
    E --> F[No extra inference\noverhead after merge]
```

> QA-LoRA identifies a problem with QLoRA: when the LoRA adapters are merged back into the base model after fine-tuning, the merged weights are no longer well-fit by the original quantization grid. QA-LoRA rethinks the adapter design so that the merged (adapter + quantized base) can be re-quantized cleanly: adapters are designed to be absorbed into the group-wise quantization parameters (scale + zero-point) rather than added as a separate floating-point correction. The result is a merged quantized model that is properly quantized after fine-tuning, enabling clean deployment without a separate re-quantization step.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **NeurIPS 2023** |
| Paper | [2309.14717](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.14717) |
| Precision | W4A16 (group-wise) |
| Granularity | per-group, adapter output merged into group-wise quantization scale |
| Calibration | fine-tuning data |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | LoRA updates absorbed into group-wise quantization parameters at merge time |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | yes |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | better than QLoRA at low LoRA rank; adapter merges cleanly into quantized model |
| Builds on | [qlora](#qlora) · [gptq](#gptq) |
| Related | [qlora](#qlora) · [loftq](#loftq) · [ir-qlora](#ir-qlora) |

**How it works:**

In QA-LoRA, LoRA adapters are applied per-group: each rank-r adapter acts on a group of g weight elements, modifying the effective group scale and zero-point. The adapter output B·A is added to the group-level dequantization of Q(W_group), and since B·A is low-rank across the group dimension, it can be absorbed into the group-wise quantization parameters after fine-tuning. The number of parameters per adapter is r × (g_in + g_out) per group - small and proportional to the group size rather than the full channel count.

---

<a id="llm-qat"></a>
### LLM-QAT · W4A8KV4 / W4A8 · QAT / QFT · 2023-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/llm-qat.svg" width="640" alt="LLM-QAT diagram">
<p><em>Data-free QAT: model generates its own training data, STE backprops through quantization.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 pretrained\nLLM] --> B[Insert fake quant\nnodes throughout]
    B --> C[Forward: quantize\nweights + activations]
    C --> D[Loss on labeled data\nor self-supervised]
    D --> E[Backprop through\nSTE for quant nodes]
    E --> F{Converged?}
    F -->|no| C
    F -->|yes| G[Quantized LLM\nbetter than PTQ at same bits]
```

> LLM-QAT performs quantization-aware training of LLMs without any task data by using the model's own generations as training examples - the model "teaches itself" to be robust to quantization noise. This data-free approach avoids privacy concerns and the cost of collecting task-specific data. During QAT, the straight-through estimator (STE) provides gradients through the quantization step, and the training objective is standard next-token prediction on the self-generated text. The method also quantizes the KV cache to 4 bits, which was novel at the time.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2305.17888](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.17888) |
| Precision | W4A8KV4 / W4A8 |
| Granularity | per-channel |
| Calibration | data-free: uses model's own generations as pseudo-training data |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | QAT optimization naturally adapts weights to quantization grid |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | yes |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | W4A8 comparable to PTQ methods; W4A8KV4 enables 4-bit KV with minimal loss |
| Builds on | [rtn](#rtn) |
| Related | [qlora](#qlora) · [bitdistiller](#bitdistiller) · [efficientqat](#efficientqat) |

**How it works:**

Training data is generated by sampling from the (FP16) model itself using temperature sampling. The model is then fine-tuned on these samples with simulated quantization inserted: all weight matrices and activations have fake quantization applied in the forward pass (quantize then dequantize), and the STE carries gradients through. Both weights and activations are jointly quantized to W4A8. A separate QAT phase for the KV cache (quantizing K and V to INT4 during generation) allows W4A8KV4 models. The result is models that are inherently robust to quantization at inference.

---

<a id="qlora"></a>
### QLoRA · W4A16 (NF4 base) + BF16 LoRA adapters · QAT / QFT · 2023-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/qlora.svg" width="640" alt="QLoRA diagram">
<p><em>NF4 frozen base + BF16 LoRA adapters: fine-tune 65B on one 48GB GPU.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 base model] --> B[Quantize to NF4\n4-bit frozen]
    B --> C[Frozen NF4\nbase weights]
    C --> D[LoRA adapters\ndelta W = B times A in BF16]
    D --> E[Forward: dequant NF4 to BF16 + delta W]
    E --> F[Backprop only\nthrough adapters A, B]
    F --> G[65B model\nfits on 48 GB GPU]
```

> QLoRA makes fine-tuning 65B+ parameter models possible on a single 48GB GPU by keeping the base model frozen in 4-bit NF4 quantization and training only lightweight LoRA adapters in BF16. The quantized base model provides a good initialization; the LoRA adapters learn the task-specific residual correction without having to update the quantized weights directly. QLoRA introduces NF4 (the quantile-spaced 4-bit normal float), double quantization (quantizing the quantization scales themselves), and paged optimizers to handle gradient checkpointing on consumer hardware.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **NeurIPS 2023** |
| Paper | [2305.14314](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.14314) |
| Code | [artidoro/qlora](https://github.com/artidoro/qlora) |
| Precision | W4A16 (NF4 base) + BF16 LoRA adapters |
| Granularity | per-group g=64 (NF4 double-quant) |
| Calibration | none for base quantization; fine-tuning on task data |
| Symmetric? | n/a (NF4) |
| Outlier handling | NF4 quantile grid; LoRA adapters learn residual corrections |
| Hardware target | NVIDIA GPU (bitsandbytes NF4 kernels) |
| Training needed? | yes |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | approaches full-precision LoRA fine-tuning quality on most tasks |
| Builds on | [bitsandbytes-nf4](#bitsandbytes-nf4) |
| Related | [loftq](#loftq) · [qa-lora](#qa-lora) · [ir-qlora](#ir-qlora) · [apiq](#apiq) |

**How it works:**

QLoRA freezes the base model weights in NF4 quantization and adds rank-r LoRA adapter matrices (A ∈ ℝ^{d×r}, B ∈ ℝ^{r×d}) to each linear layer. During a forward pass, the NF4 weights are dequantized to BF16 for the matmul (no INT4×INT4 compute - dequant before multiply), and the LoRA output is added: h = W_NF4×x + B×A×x. Only A and B have gradients; the quantized W_NF4 is frozen. The NF4 quantization error acts as a form of noise that the LoRA adapters learn to compensate for. Paged optimizers handle out-of-memory spikes by offloading optimizer states to CPU RAM during gradient checkpointing.

---

<a id="peqa"></a>
### PEQA · W4A16 (INT4 weight) · QAT / QFT · 2023-04

<img src="../assets/diagrams/peqa.svg" width="640" alt="PEQA diagram">
<p><em>PEQA: fine-tune only per-column scale multipliers γ on frozen INT4 weights.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Quantized base model\nINT4 / INT8] --> B[Freeze quantized\nbase weights]
    B --> C[Add task-specific\nFP16 bias vectors]
    C --> D[Fine-tune only\nbias parameters]
    D --> E[Tiny parameter count\n<< full fine-tune]
    E --> F[Task-adapted\nquantized model]
```

> PEQA fine-tunes quantized LLMs by keeping the quantized integer weights frozen and only training per-column scale factors alongside the original dequantization scale. Rather than adding separate adapter matrices (like QLoRA), PEQA modifies the quantization scale itself - a minimal number of parameters that changes the effective floating-point value of the entire quantized column. This is computationally light and avoids the overhead of separate adapter matrices, while still giving the model freedom to shift its weight distribution during fine-tuning.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **NeurIPS 2023** |
| Paper | [2304.02384](https://arxiv.org/abs/2304.02384) |
| Precision | W4A16 (INT4 weight) |
| Granularity | per-column |
| Calibration | fine-tuning data |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | fine-tuning adapts scale parameters alongside quantized weights |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | yes |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | approaches full-precision LoRA fine-tuning quality |
| Builds on | [rtn](#rtn) · [gptq](#gptq) |
| Related | [qlora](#qlora) · [loftq](#loftq) · [qa-lora](#qa-lora) |

**How it works:**

For a quantized column w̃ = s · q_int (where s is the dequantization scale and q_int is the stored integer vector), PEQA introduces a learnable multiplier γ per column: w_eff = γ · s · q_int. During fine-tuning, only γ (one scalar per column) is updated; q_int remains frozen. The number of trainable parameters is one per column of each linear layer - much fewer than LoRA adapters. Despite this minimal parameterization, the scale multiplier can significantly shift the effective weight values column-wide, allowing meaningful task adaptation.

---

---

## Extreme Low-Bit & Binary/Ternary Quantization

Methods targeting 1–2 bits per weight, including binary {-1, +1}, ternary {-1, 0, +1}, and sub-2-bit codebook approaches. At these bit-widths quantization error is substantial and most methods require QAT or architectural changes. The frontier of model compression research.

<a id="twla"></a>
### TWLA · W1.58A4 · Sub-2-bit · 2026-06

<img src="../assets/diagrams/twla.svg" width="640" alt="TWLA diagram">
<p><em>TWLA pipeline: E2M-ATQ ternary weight quantizer, KOTMS Kronecker orthogonal tri-modal reshaping that also suppresses activation outliers, and ILA-AMP inter-layer-aware mixed-precision activation bit allocation, producing a W1.58A4 model.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Training from scratch\nor PTQ] --> B[W1.58A4\ntarget format]
    B --> C[Binary or ternary\nweight representation]
    C --> D[Scale factors\nto compensate range]
    D --> E[Matmuls become\nadditions or lookups]
    E --> F[Extreme compression\nwith quality tradeoff]
```

> TWLA is a post-training quantization recipe that compresses large language models down to ternary (1.58-bit) weights paired with 4-bit activations, so both sides of every matrix multiply run in low precision rather than keeping activations in high precision. It tackles three obstacles at once: ternarizing weights without exploding layer output error, reshaping weight distributions so ternary values fit them well while also taming activation outliers, and deciding how many activation bits each layer needs. The result is a fully low-bit pipeline that keeps accuracy close to higher-precision baselines while enabling faster end- to-end inference, without any gradient retraining.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICML 2026** |
| Paper | [2606.13054](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13054) |
| Code | [Kishon-zzx/TWLA](https://github.com/Kishon-zzx/TWLA) |
| Precision | W1.58A4 |
| Granularity | per-channel (row-wise) ternary weights with row-wise threshold/scale; per-token activations with mixed-precision (per-layer) bit allocation |
| Calibration | small calibration set used to compute layer-output reconstruction error and second-order inter-layer interaction costs |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | Kronecker-structured shared orthogonal rotation (KOTMS) that reshapes weights into tri-modal ternary-friendly distributions while statistically suppressing activation outliers; inter-layer-aware mixed-precision activation allocation (ILA-AMP) |
| Hardware target | GPU inference; low-bit weight x low-bit activation matmul for end-to-end acceleration |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | At W1.58A4: LLaMA-2-70B reaches 4.77 WikiText-2 perplexity and 71.10 average zero-shot accuracy; LLaMA-3-8B reaches 12.83 WikiText-2 perplexity and 55.23 average zero-shot accuracy |
| Builds on | [bitnet-b158](#bitnet-b158) · [quarot](#quarot) · [flatquant](#flatquant) |
| Related | [bitnet-b158](#bitnet-b158) · [billm](#billm) · [spectra](#spectra) · [quarot](#quarot) · [spinquant](#spinquant) |

**How it works:**

Three components work together. E2M-ATQ (Euclidean-to-Manifold Asymmetric Ternary Quantizer) ternarizes weights through a two-stage optimization, starting from a Euclidean-distance initialization and then relocating points on a manifold to minimize layer-output reconstruction error with an asymmetric ternary mapping. KOTMS (Kronecker Orthogonal Tri- Modal Shaping) applies a Kronecker-structured orthogonal rotation that pushes weights into a tri-modal distribution well-matched to ternary levels, and because the same rotation also acts on activations it statistically suppresses activation outliers. ILA-AMP (Inter-Layer Aware Activation Mixed Precision) allocates activation bits across layers by modeling adjacent-layer second-order interaction costs, accounting for how the shared rotation changes per-layer quantization gain and preventing error cascades from a few weak layers.

---

<a id="sage-ptq"></a>
### SAGE-PTQ · W1A16 · Sub-2-bit · 2026-06

<img src="../assets/diagrams/sage-ptq.svg" width="640" alt="SAGE-PTQ diagram">
<p><em>Saliency split (per-channel multi-bit salient + binary unsalient groups) with graph-estimated group counts to minimize scale-storage overhead at ~1.03 bits/weight.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Training from scratch\nor PTQ] --> B[W1A16\ntarget format]
    B --> C[Binary or ternary\nweight representation]
    C --> D[Scale factors\nto compensate range]
    D --> E[Matmuls become\nadditions or lookups]
    E --> F[Extreme compression\nwith quality tradeoff]
```

> SAGE-PTQ is a weight-only post-training method that pushes average storage down to about 1.03 bits per weight by attacking an overlooked cost: the bits spent on quantization scales themselves. It splits each weight matrix into a small salient set and a large unsalient set using distributional statistics, keeps the salient weights at a few bits of precision (2 to 4), and binarizes everything else. To keep scale overhead near zero it uses just one per- channel scale for the salient weights and a single scalar per unsalient binary group, and it picks how many groups each layer needs by treating subsampled unsalient weights as a sparse graph. An adaptive threshold sets the salient ratio separately for each matrix. The result is a 1-bit-class quantizer that reports much lower perplexity than prior binary PTQ methods while adding almost no metadata.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2606.05429](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05429) |
| Precision | W1A16 |
| Granularity | per-channel scale for salient weights; one scalar per unsalient binary group; per-layer group count chosen by graph model |
| Calibration | calibration set used to compute weight saliency statistics (post-training, no labels) |
| Symmetric? | mixed |
| Outlier handling | saliency separation: tiny salient fraction (~1%) kept at multi-bit (2-4 bit) precision, remaining weights binarized; adaptive per-matrix saliency thresholding |
| Hardware target | GPU (reports 1.5x faster decoding on NVIDIA L40) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | WikiText2 perplexity: 5.87 on LLaMA-2-7B, 7.51 on LLaMA-2-70B, 6.74 on LLaMA-3-8B (vs 55.8 for BiLLM on LLaMA-3-8B), all at ~1.03 bits/weight |
| Builds on | [billm](#billm) · [pb-llm](#pb-llm) |
| Related | [billm](#billm) · [pb-llm](#pb-llm) · [onebit](#onebit) · [spqr](#spqr) |

**How it works:**

The method observes that ultra-low-bit quantizers spend a non-trivial fraction of their storage on per-group scaling factors, so it minimizes that hidden cost rather than only the weight bits. Weights are partitioned into salient and unsalient sets via distributional statistics, with an adaptive per-matrix saliency threshold choosing the salient ratio (often around 1%). Salient weights get multi-bit precision (4-bit in outlier-heavy early layers, 2-bit later) sharing a single per-channel scale, while unsalient weights are binarized and share one scalar per group. The number of unsalient groups per layer is estimated by modeling subsampled unsalient weights as a sparse graph, balancing reconstruction accuracy against the scale budget so the combined cost lands near 1.03 weight bits plus ~0.004 scaling bits per matrix.

---

<a id="spectral-llm"></a>
### Spectral-LLM · W2A16 · Sub-2-bit · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/spectral-llm.svg" width="640" alt="Spectral-LLM diagram">
<p><em>Spectral-LLM: per-layer Walsh-Hadamard rotation plus Walsh-basis influence rescaling biases AutoRound's 2-bit rounding toward high-energy channels before OpenVINO IR export.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Training from scratch\nor PTQ] --> B[W2A16\ntarget format]
    B --> C[Binary or ternary\nweight representation]
    C --> D[Scale factors\nto compensate range]
    D --> E[Matmuls become\nadditions or lookups]
    E --> F[Extreme compression\nwith quality tradeoff]
```

> This method adds a single math-preserving preprocessing step in front of an off-the-shelf weight-only quantizer (Intel AutoRound). For each linear layer it applies a Walsh-Hadamard rotation to the weight matrix and then rescales the matrix columns according to how much activation energy each coordinate carries in the Walsh basis, an importance signal borrowed from Boolean-function influence analysis. The effect is to push the rounding budget toward the coordinates that matter most, so that when AutoRound quantizes the rotated-and-scaled weights down to 2 bits, the reconstruction error lands on the least important directions. Because the transform is invertible, accuracy is unchanged in full precision, and the quantized weights export to OpenVINO IR and run identically on Intel CPU, NPU, and Arc GPU. On small decoder-only models it cuts WikiText-2 perplexity by roughly 15 to 58 percent relative to plain AutoRound at 2-bit.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.25203](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25203) |
| Code | [gogipav14/spectral-llm](https://github.com/gogipav14/spectral-llm) |
| Precision | W2A16 |
| Granularity | per-group integer rounding (g=64 in repo examples), with per-coordinate column rescaling |
| Calibration | calibration set used to estimate Walsh-basis activation energy and drive AutoRound reconstruction |
| Symmetric? | mixed (AutoRound integer rounding; asymmetric Gamma=diag(gamma)U construction for RoPE-compatible attention routes) |
| Outlier handling | Walsh-Hadamard rotation spreads energy across coordinates, then per-coordinate Walsh-basis activation-energy rescaling biases rounding toward high-spectral-energy (high-influence) channels |
| Hardware target | Intel NPU + Arc dGPU + CPU via OpenVINO IR export |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | W2A16 perplexity reductions of 15-58% vs vanilla AutoRound on WikiText-2: Qwen3-0.6B 136.76 -> 88.99, Qwen2.5-1.5B 36.93 -> 21.84 (also evaluated on TinyLlama-1.1B and a 135M model) |
| Builds on | [autoround](#autoround) · [quarot](#quarot) |
| Related | [spinquant](#spinquant) · [quip-sharp](#quip-sharp) · [aqlm](#aqlm) · [omniquant](#omniquant) · [gptq](#gptq) |

**How it works:**

Each linear weight matrix W is left-multiplied by a Walsh-Hadamard transform (an orthogonal, hence loss-invariant, rotation), and its columns are then rescaled by per-coordinate Walsh- basis activation energy, a measure of each spectral coordinate's influence on the output. This biases the subsequent per-group integer rounding toward high-energy / high-influence channels, concentrating quantization error on low-influence directions. The rotated, rescaled weights are handed to Intel AutoRound as the reconstruction-error quantizer, and the inverse rotation/scale is folded back so the operation is mathematically equivalent to the original layer at full precision. Variants include asymmetric constructions and per-pair SO(2) rotations that commute with RoPE for attention layers.

---

<a id="bwla"></a>
### BWLA · W1A6 (also W1A4/W1A8/W1A16 evaluated) · Sub-2-bit · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/bwla.svg" width="640" alt="BWLA diagram">
<p><em>Learned orthogonal-Kronecker transform symmetrizes weights into a bimodal form for clean 1-bit binarization while suppressing activation tails; proximal SVD projection adds low-rank refinement, enabling W1 weights with low-bit activations.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Training from scratch\nor PTQ] --> B[W1A6 also W1A4/W1A8/W1A16 evaluated\ntarget format]
    B --> C[Binary or ternary\nweight representation]
    C --> D[Scale factors\nto compensate range]
    D --> E[Matmuls become\nadditions or lookups]
    E --> F[Extreme compression\nwith quality tradeoff]
```

> BWLA is a post-training quantization recipe that pushes weights down to a single bit while also lowering activations to around 6 bits, so that both sides of each matrix multiply run in low precision and the whole layer actually speeds up rather than being bottlenecked by full-precision activations. The challenge it targets is that binary-weight methods usually have to keep activations in high precision because of their heavy-tailed distributions; BWLA instead applies a learned orthogonal transform that reshapes the weight distribution into a symmetric two-peak form (which binarizes cleanly) and at the same time flattens the activation tails. A second low-rank correction step recovers extra accuracy at little cost. The result is usable 1-bit-weight, low-bit-activation models without any retraining.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ACL 2026** |
| Paper | [2605.00422](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.00422) |
| Precision | W1A6 (also W1A4/W1A8/W1A16 evaluated) |
| Granularity | per-channel binary weights with learned orthogonal-Kronecker transforms; low-bit per-token activations |
| Calibration | 128 text segments sampled from Wikitext2 |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | Orthogonal-Kronecker Transformation (learned via EM) reshapes weights into symmetric bimodal forms and suppresses activation heavy tails / incoherence; Proximal SVD Projection adds low-rank refinement |
| Hardware target | GPU (evaluated on NVIDIA A6000) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | Qwen3-32B at W1A6: Wikitext2 perplexity 11.92 (vs ~38 from prior SOTA); >70% relative gain across five zero-shot tasks; 3.26x inference speedup |
| Builds on | [billm](#billm) · [quarot](#quarot) |
| Related | [billm](#billm) · [onebit](#onebit) · [pb-llm](#pb-llm) · [quarot](#quarot) · [spinquant](#spinquant) |

**How it works:**

The Orthogonal-Kronecker Transformation (OKT) learns an orthogonal mapping, parameterized in Kronecker-factored form for efficiency, by minimizing an objective via expectation- maximization. Applied to the weights it converts their unimodal distribution into a symmetric bimodal shape that is well matched to 1-bit binarization, while the same rotation applied to the activation path suppresses heavy tails and reduces incoherence so activations can be quantized to low bits. The Proximal SVD Projection (PSP) then performs a lightweight low-rank refinement through proximal SVD projection to further improve quantizability with minimal overhead. The whole pipeline is post-training (no gradient fine-tuning) and uses a small Wikitext2 calibration set.

---

<a id="lbllm"></a>
### LBLLM · W(1+1)A4 · Sub-2-bit · 2026-04

<img src="../assets/diagrams/lbllm.svg" width="640" alt="LBLLM diagram">
<p><em>LBLLM three-stage pipeline: PTQ initialization, layer-wise distillation of binary weights and group-wise bitmaps with full-precision activations, then learned 4-bit activation quantizers, producing a W(1+1)A4 model.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Training from scratch\nor PTQ] --> B[W1+1A4\ntarget format]
    B --> C[Binary or ternary\nweight representation]
    C --> D[Scale factors\nto compensate range]
    D --> E[Matmuls become\nadditions or lookups]
    E --> F[Extreme compression\nwith quality tradeoff]
```

> LBLLM compresses LLM weights to roughly two effective bits (one binary weight matrix plus a one-bit per-group bitmap) while keeping activations at 4 bits, and reaches usable accuracy after fine-tuning on only 0.016B tokens on a single GPU. Rather than learning weights and activations at the same time, it runs three separate phases: a post-training quantization warm start, then layer-wise distillation that fits the binary weights and per-group parameters with activations left in full precision, and finally a short phase that learns the activation quantizers. Splitting the work this way keeps training stable and avoids the two quantization steps fighting each other. The result improves on prior binarization methods in the comparable two-bit-weight, four-bit-activation regime across language modeling, commonsense QA, and language understanding.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2604.19167](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.19167) |
| Code | [JimmyCrave/LBLLM](https://github.com/JimmyCrave/LBLLM) |
| Precision | W(1+1)A4 |
| Granularity | group-wise, group size 128 (binary weight matrix plus a binary group-assignment bitmap selecting between two scale/offset pairs) |
| Calibration | small calibration/training corpus of 0.016B tokens used for layer-wise distillation |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | learnable activation clipping factors and learnable knee points that split activation ranges into three regions with separate quantization parameters |
| Hardware target | single GPU |
| Training needed? | yes |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | 9.18 WikiText-2 perplexity on LLaMA-2-7B at W(1+1)A4, versus 32.48 (BiLLM), 19.14 (ARB-LLM) and 10.19 (OneBit) baselines |
| Builds on | [billm](#billm) · [onebit](#onebit) · [pb-llm](#pb-llm) |
| Related | [billm](#billm) · [onebit](#onebit) · [pb-llm](#pb-llm) · [bitdistiller](#bitdistiller) · [omniquant](#omniquant) |

**How it works:**

Each linear layer stores a binary weight matrix together with a binary group-assignment bitmap; the bitmap routes every weight to one of two groups, each with its own learned scale and offset, so two binary signals together encode four states (about two bits) at group size 128. Training proceeds in three decoupled stages: (1) a PTQ initialization for a good starting point, (2) layer-wise knowledge distillation that learns the binary weights, group bitmaps, and quantization parameters while activations stay full-precision, and (3) learning dynamic 4-bit activation quantizers using learnable clipping factors and learnable knee points that partition the activation range into three regions. Decoupling weight and activation quantization reduces interference and yields more stable optimization than joint training.

---

<a id="tequila"></a>
### Tequila · W1.58 A16 (ternary {-1, 0, +1} PTQ) · Sub-2-bit · 2025-09

<img src="../assets/diagrams/tequila.svg" width="640" alt="Tequila diagram">
<p><em>Tequila: deadzone-free threshold optimization for ternary {-1,0,+1} PTQ of pretrained LLMs.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Training from scratch\nor PTQ] --> B[W1.58 A16 ternary -1, 0, +1 PTQ\ntarget format]
    B --> C[Binary or ternary\nweight representation]
    C --> D[Scale factors\nto compensate range]
    D --> E[Matmuls become\nadditions or lookups]
    E --> F[Extreme compression\nwith quality tradeoff]
```

> Ternary quantization (mapping weights to {-1, 0, +1}) suffers from a "deadzone" problem: near-zero weights are incorrectly mapped to 0, destroying information about small but non-negligible weights. Tequila introduces a deadzone-free ternary quantization scheme that carefully calibrates the thresholds for zero-assignment to minimize reconstruction error. Unlike BitNet b1.58 which requires training from scratch, Tequila is a post-training method that brings ternary quantization quality to pretrained FP16 models.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICLR 2026** |
| Paper | [2509.23809](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.23809) |
| Precision | W1.58 A16 (ternary {-1, 0, +1} PTQ) |
| Granularity | per-group with deadzone-free threshold |
| Calibration | small calibration set for threshold optimization |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | deadzone-free thresholding prevents over-assignment to zero |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | state-of-art ternary PTQ; better than round-to-nearest ternary |
| Builds on | [bitnet-b158](#bitnet-b158) · [hqq](#hqq) · [gptq](#gptq) |
| Related | [bitnet-b158](#bitnet-b158) · [bitnet-2b4t](#bitnet-2b4t) · [pb-llm](#pb-llm) · [hqq](#hqq) |

**How it works:**

Standard ternary quantization assigns w to 0 if |w| < threshold, otherwise to +scale or -scale. This creates a deadzone of size 2*threshold around zero where weight information is permanently lost. Tequila optimizes the threshold per group to minimize the L2 reconstruction error subject to a target zero-fraction constraint. The deadzone-free formulation ensures small weights contribute to the scale estimation even when rounded to zero, improving the scale value and reducing reconstruction error compared to standard ternary PTQ.

---

<a id="littlebit"></a>
### LittleBit · sub-1-bit (effective ~0.1 bits per weight) · Sub-2-bit · 2025-09

<img src="../assets/diagrams/littlebit.svg" width="640" alt="LittleBit diagram">
<p><em>LittleBit: weight matrix factorized into FP16 codebook C + ultra-low-bit latent codes Z.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Training from scratch\nor PTQ] --> B[sub-1-bit effective approx 0.1 bits per weight\ntarget format]
    B --> C[Binary or ternary\nweight representation]
    C --> D[Scale factors\nto compensate range]
    D --> E[Matmuls become\nadditions or lookups]
    E --> F[Extreme compression\nwith quality tradeoff]
```

> LittleBit pushes quantization to the extreme: it achieves effective bit-widths of ~0.1 bits per weight through latent factorization. Rather than quantizing individual weights, LittleBit factorizes the weight matrix into a shared latent codebook and per-weight latent codes. The latent codes are quantized to extremely few bits (or even single bits), while the shared codebook captures the bulk of the information. This is related to tensor factorization and vector quantization but targets extreme sub-1-bit regimes that are beyond previous methods.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **NeurIPS 2025** |
| Paper | [2506.13771](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.13771) |
| Precision | sub-1-bit (effective ~0.1 bits per weight) |
| Granularity | global latent space factorization |
| Calibration | calibration set for latent factor optimization |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | latent factorization absorbs distribution into low-dimensional space |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | significant at sub-1-bit; best known result at this compression ratio |
| Builds on | [aqlm](#aqlm) · [quip-sharp](#quip-sharp) · [qtip](#qtip) |
| Related | [aqlm](#aqlm) · [qtip](#qtip) · [quip-sharp](#quip-sharp) · [bitnet](#bitnet) |

**How it works:**

LittleBit decomposes each weight layer W into W = C * Z, where C is a learned latent codebook (shared across the layer) and Z is a matrix of binary or ternary latent codes. The codes Z are quantized to very low bit-width (1-2 bits), while C is kept in higher precision (FP16) but is small enough that the total storage is sub-1-bit average. The decomposition is optimized via alternating minimization on calibration data. The extreme compression sacrifices some accuracy but enables deployment scenarios where memory is the absolute bottleneck.

<a id="bitnet-2b4t"></a>
### BitNet b1.58 2B4T · W1.58A8 · Sub-2-bit · 2025-04

<img src="../assets/diagrams/bitnet-2b4t.svg" width="640" alt="BitNet b1.58 2B4T diagram">
<p><em>BitNet b1.58 2B4T: first open ternary LLM at 2B params; BitNet.cpp CPU inference runtime.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[2B param architecture\nBitNet b1.58 design] --> B[Train on 4T tokens\nfrom scratch]
    B --> C[Ternary weights -1/0/+1\nthroughout training]

    D --> E[First open-source\n1-bit 2B model]
    E --> F[Competitive with\nFP16 Llama-3.2-1B/3B]
```

> BitNet b1.58 2B4T is the first openly-released full-scale ternary LLM at 2 billion parameters trained on 4 trillion tokens. Microsoft releases model weights, training code, and the BitNet.cpp inference library that achieves CPU-efficient inference without GPUs. The model demonstrates that ternary (W1.58) LLMs can be trained at full scale and achieve quality competitive with similarly-sized FP16 models, while consuming a fraction of the memory and enabling energy-efficient inference on commodity hardware.

Same architecture as BitNet b1.58 (absmean ternary quantizer, INT8 activations, STE training), but at 2B parameters trained on 4T tokens - significantly more data than prior BitNet releases. The model uses the Llama 3 architecture with BitLinear replacing standard Linear layers. The open release includes a dedicated BitNet.cpp runtime that exploits ternary arithmetic (no multiplications) for CPU inference, achieving 2-5× throughput improvement over FP16 llama.cpp inference on consumer CPUs.

<a id="spectra"></a>
### Spectra · W1.58A8 (ternary), W4A16, FP16 · Sub-2-bit · 2024-07

<img src="../assets/diagrams/spectra.svg" width="640" alt="Spectra diagram">
<p><em>Spectra: systematic study training ternary, quantized, and FP16 LLMs at matched scales.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Train LLM from scratch\non internet-scale data] --> B[INT4 / INT8 / FP16\nweight formats]
    B --> C[No quantization error:\nweights never float]
    C --> D[Release family:\nSpectra-99M to 3.9B]
    D --> E[Benchmark across\nbit-widths and tasks]
    E --> F[Integer-trained models\noutperform PTQ at same bits]
```

> Spectra is a comprehensive empirical study and model suite that trains LLMs from scratch across three precision regimes - ternary (BitNet b1.58 style), quantized (W4A16 GPTQ), and FP16 - at multiple model sizes (54M to 3.9B parameters) on 300B tokens. The goal is to rigorously compare the quality-vs-efficiency tradeoffs across precisions at scale, providing training recipes and released checkpoints for each configuration. The study confirms that ternary models can approach FP16 quality at 3B+ parameters given sufficient training.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2407.12327](https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.12327) |
| Precision | W1.58A8 (ternary), W4A16, FP16 |
| Granularity | per-tensor (ternary) |
| Calibration | training from scratch |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | training from scratch with ternary constraint |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | yes |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | ternary Spectra (54M–3.9B) studied systematically vs quantized and FP16 baselines |
| Builds on | [bitnet-b158](#bitnet-b158) |
| Related | [bitnet-b158](#bitnet-b158) · [matmul-free](#matmul-free) · [bitnet](#bitnet) |

**How it works:**

Spectra trains each model (54M, 134M, 400M, 830M, 3.9B) independently from scratch in all three regimes on the same RedPajama dataset and evaluates on a consistent set of downstream tasks and perplexity benchmarks. The ternary variant uses the BitNet b1.58 absmean quantizer with STE. The key findings: (1) ternary models need ~2× more parameters than FP16 to match perplexity; (2) quantized-from-scratch (W4A16) outperforms PTQ of FP16 models at small scale; (3) ternary models have significantly better memory and inference efficiency at equivalent quality.

<a id="matmul-free"></a>
### MatMul-free LM · W1.58A8 (ternary weights, similar to BitNet b1.58) · Sub-2-bit · 2024-06

<img src="../assets/diagrams/matmul-free.svg" width="640" alt="MatMul-free LM diagram">
<p><em>Ternary linear layers + gated linear recurrence: no matrix multiplications in the entire model.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Transformer block] --> B[Replace all linear\nlayers]
    B --> C[Ternary weights\n-1, 0, +1]
    C --> D[Binary activations\nHardtanh clipped]
    D --> E[Attention alternative:\nTokenMixer replaces\nQK^T matmul]
    E --> F[Pure additions\nno multiplications]
    F --> G[1-bit W, 1-bit A\ntrue integer inference]
```

> MatMul-free LM eliminates all matrix multiplications from the transformer by replacing linear layers with ternary-weight operations (implementable as add/subtract) and replacing the standard attention mechanism with a linear-time RNN-like token mixing (based on RWKV/GRU dynamics). The result is a language model where the dominant compute operation - matrix multiplication - is replaced by additions, enabling much lower power consumption on hardware that does not support efficient float matrix multiply.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2406.02528](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.02528) |
| Code | [ridgerchu/matmulfreellm](https://github.com/ridgerchu/matmulfreellm) |
| Precision | W1.58A8 (ternary weights, similar to BitNet b1.58) |
| Granularity | per-tensor |
| Calibration | training from scratch |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | ternary weights avoid extreme weight values; trained from scratch |
| Hardware target | FPGA / custom hardware ideal; CPU with optimized kernels |
| Training needed? | yes |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | approaches comparable-size FP32 models up to 2.7B parameters |
| Builds on | [bitnet-b158](#bitnet-b158) |
| Related | [bitnet-b158](#bitnet-b158) · [bitnet](#bitnet) · [onebit](#onebit) |

**How it works:**

Two innovations: (1) Linear layers use ternary weight matrices (BitNet b1.58 style), so W·x becomes a sum/difference of x elements selected by W ∈ {-1,0,+1}. No multiplications are needed in the linear layers. (2) The attention mechanism is replaced by a token mixing layer based on a gated linear recurrence (MLGRU), which can also be computed without matrix multiplication. The combination produces a model that runs without any floating-point multiplications in its core computation, making it suitable for highly energy-efficient hardware implementations.

<a id="bitnet-b158"></a>
### BitNet b1.58 · W1.58A8 · Sub-2-bit · 2024-02

<img src="../assets/diagrams/bitnet-b158.svg" width="640" alt="BitNet b1.58 diagram">
<p><em>Ternary {-1,0,+1} weights via absmean quantizer; matmul becomes add-only (no multiplications).</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Training from scratch] --> B[Ternary weights\n-1, 0, +1]
    B --> C[Straight-through\nestimator STE]
    C --> D[AbsMean\nact quantization INT8]
    D --> E[MatMuls become\nadditions only]
    E --> F[1.58-bit model\nmatches FP16 at 3B+]
```

> BitNet b1.58 (named after log₂(3) ≈ 1.58 bits) represents LLM weights as ternary values {-1, 0, +1}, one step above binary. The addition of zero is significant: it allows the model to implement magnitude selection (zeroing out some directions) which binary weights cannot do. Training uses a simple absmean quantizer (scale by mean absolute value, round to nearest of {-1, 0, +1}) with STE. The reported result is that BitNet b1.58 at 3.9B parameters matches full-precision LLaMA-3B on perplexity and most benchmarks, while using a fraction of the memory and enabling inference with add-only arithmetic (no multiplications).

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2402.17764](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.17764) |
| Code | [microsoft/BitNet](https://github.com/microsoft/BitNet) |
| Precision | W1.58A8 |
| Granularity | per-tensor (weights); per-token (activations) |
| Calibration | training from scratch |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | ternary representation absorbs zero (dead weights); absmean quantizer |
| Hardware target | custom kernels; llama.cpp BitNet.cpp branch for CPU |
| Training needed? | yes |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | matches FP16 LLaMA-3B/7B quality at same parameter count (reported) |
| Builds on | [bitnet](#bitnet) |
| Related | [bitnet](#bitnet) · [onebit](#onebit) · [billm](#billm) · [matmul-free](#matmul-free) |

**How it works:**

Each weight matrix W is quantized per-tensor as: γ = mean(|W|), W̃ = clip(round(W/γ), -1, 1). This yields ternary values {-1, 0, +1}. Activations use per-token INT8 quantization. The matmul W̃ x with ternary W can be computed without any multiplications: result = sum(x_i where W̃_i=+1) - sum(x_i where W̃_i=-1). On hardware with popcount instructions or dedicated binary/ternary compute, this is much more energy-efficient than float32 or INT8 matmul. The zero weight values effectively prune neurons without explicit pruning, allowing the model to learn a sparse-like internal structure.

<a id="onebit"></a>
### OneBit · W1A16 (sign + magnitude decomposition) · Sub-2-bit · 2024-02

<img src="../assets/diagrams/onebit.svg" width="640" alt="OneBit diagram">
<p><em>Sign-value decomposition: W = sign(W) × row_scale × col_scale; sign stored at 1-bit.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Weight W] --> B[Sign-value decomp:\nW = V times sign S]
    B --> C[V: value vectors\nper output channel]
    C --> D[S: binary sign\nmatrix INT1]
    D --> E[Forward: W_hat = V times S]
    E --> F[STE training\nfrom scratch or QAT]
    F --> G[1-bit with value\nvectors = better than plain binary]
```

> OneBit decomposes each weight matrix W as W ≈ sign(W) · diag(v₁) · diag(v₂)^T where v₁ and v₂ are magnitude vectors (FP16). The sign matrix is stored at 1 bit; the two magnitude vectors are stored at full precision. This is a sign-value decomposition analogous to SVD-1 (rank-1 SVD) but in the sign domain. The magnitude vectors capture the scale information that binary weights cannot represent. Combined with knowledge distillation from the FP16 teacher, OneBit achieves quality close to W4 quantization at an effective bit-width of ~1.5 bits.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **NeurIPS 2024** |
| Paper | [2402.11295](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.11295) |
| Precision | W1A16 (sign + magnitude decomposition) |
| Granularity | sign matrix (1-bit) + magnitude vectors (FP16) |
| Calibration | knowledge distillation from FP16 model |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | magnitude vectors capture scale information; distillation recovers quality |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | yes |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | approaches W4 PTQ quality at 1.5 effective bits with distillation |
| Builds on | [bitnet](#bitnet) |
| Related | [bitnet-b158](#bitnet-b158) · [billm](#billm) · [pb-llm](#pb-llm) |

**How it works:**

Decompose W ∈ ℝ^{m×n} as W ≈ s₁ s₂ᵀ ⊙ B where B ∈ {-1,+1}^{m×n} is the sign matrix and s₁ ∈ ℝ^m, s₂ ∈ ℝ^n are per-row and per-column scale vectors. The storage cost is mn/8 bytes (sign matrix) + (m+n) × 2 bytes (scale vectors). For a 4096×4096 layer: 2MB for signs + 32KB for scales ≈ 2.03MB total vs. 32MB for FP16 - about 15.7× compression. Training uses the standard knowledge distillation loss (KL divergence) from the FP16 teacher to recover quality lost to the coarse 1-bit representation.

---

<a id="billm"></a>
### BiLLM · W1 A16 (post-training) · Sub-2-bit · 2024-02

<img src="../assets/diagrams/billm.svg" width="640" alt="BiLLM diagram">
<p><em>Hessian-weighted greedy 1-bit binarization + sparse FP16 residual for highest-error weights.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[LLM weights] --> B[Bell-shaped analysis:\nidentify salient values]
    B --> C[Salient set:\nresidual approximation]
    C --> D[Non-salient set:\nbinary +1/-1]
    D --> E[Optimal rescaling\nfor binary group]
    C --> F[Store residuals\nin sparse format]
    D --> F
    F --> G[Post-training\n1-bit with residuals]
```

> BiLLM achieves state-of-the-art 1-bit post-training quantization of LLMs using a Hessian-based binarization strategy. Unlike random or magnitude-based binarization, BiLLM selects the binary weight values (per column scale + sign) to minimize the Hessian-weighted reconstruction error, analogous to GPTQ but for binary. A residual mechanism stores the highest-error weights as sparse FP16 corrections. The combination makes the best-known 1-bit PTQ results without any training, demonstrating that binary LLMs are achievable purely from post-training optimization.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICML 2024** |
| Paper | [2402.04291](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.04291) |
| Code | [Aaronhuang-778/BiLLM](https://github.com/Aaronhuang-778/BiLLM) |
| Precision | W1 A16 (post-training) |
| Granularity | per-row (binary part); residual stored as sparse FP16 |
| Calibration | calibration set for Hessian |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | structured Hessian-based binarization; sparse residual for salient weights |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | best post-training 1-bit results to date; ~2–3 ppl gap vs W4 GPTQ on LLaMA |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) · [pb-llm](#pb-llm) |
| Related | [pb-llm](#pb-llm) · [bitnet](#bitnet) · [onebit](#onebit) |

**How it works:**

For each linear layer, BiLLM solves the binary quantization problem by selecting the sign vector b ∈ {-1, +1}^d and scale α to minimize ‖ W − α·diag(b) ‖_H² (Hessian- weighted error). Unlike GPTQ which rounds continuously, the binary decision is made by a greedy column-by-column selection that iteratively updates each sign bit to minimize the Hessian-weighted residual. Additionally, a sparse residual (top-k% of highest-error weights stored as FP16) is maintained on top of the binary base, similar to SpQR's sparse overlay but for the binary setting.

---

<a id="bitnet"></a>
### BitNet · W1A8 · Sub-2-bit · 2023-10

<img src="../assets/diagrams/bitnet.svg" width="640" alt="BitNet diagram">
<p><em>BitLinear: binary {-1,+1} weights + INT8 activations, trained from scratch with STE.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 training] --> B[Binary weights\n+1 or -1 via sign]
    B --> C[Straight-through\nestimator STE]
    C --> D[Scale factor\nalpha = mean of abs W]
    D --> E[W_tilde = alpha times sign W]
    E --> F[MatMuls replaced\nby additions + scaling]
    F --> G[1-bit LLM\ntraining from scratch]
```

> BitNet proposes training LLMs with binary ({-1, +1}) weight matrices from scratch, with 8-bit activations. All weight matrices in the transformer (attention projections, FFN weights) are replaced with "BitLinear" layers that store weights as binary and apply a per-tensor scale at dequantization. Training uses the STE to propagate gradients through the binary step. BitNet is the first to show that billion-scale LLMs can be trained with 1-bit weights without catastrophic collapse - the key is training from scratch rather than post-hoc binarization.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2310.11453](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.11453) |
| Code | [microsoft/BitNet](https://github.com/microsoft/BitNet) |
| Precision | W1A8 |
| Granularity | per-tensor (weights); per-token (activations) |
| Calibration | training from scratch |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | training from scratch; absmax weight normalization before binarization |
| Hardware target | custom hardware / CPU (no native 1-bit GPU support) |
| Training needed? | yes |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | lags FP16 at same parameter count; scales to close the gap; requires more params |
| Superseded by | [bitnet-b158](#bitnet-b158) |
| Related | [bitnet-b158](#bitnet-b158) · [onebit](#onebit) · [billm](#billm) |

**How it works:**

Each weight w in a BitLinear layer is binarized as: b = sign(w), and a per-tensor scale α = mean(|W|) is stored. During forward pass: y = Q_act(x) · (b · α) where Q_act is INT8 activation quantization. Gradients backprop through b via STE: ∂L/∂w ≈ ∂L/∂b (treating sign as identity). The absmax normalization before binarization (w̃ = w / max(|w|)) ensures the binary weights are ±1 symmetrically. LayerNorm before each BitLinear layer is critical to make activation distributions more Gaussian and INT8-quantizable. Training with this setup scales to 30B parameters.

---

<a id="pb-llm"></a>
### PB-LLM · W1/W16 A16 (binary weights + FP16 for salient subset) · Sub-2-bit · 2023-09

<img src="../assets/diagrams/pb-llm.svg" width="640" alt="PB-LLM diagram">
<p><em>Hessian-guided partial binarization: salient weights at FP16, rest binary.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 LLM weights] --> B[Compute per-weight\nHessian sensitivity]
    B --> C{Salient weight?}
    C -->|yes top-p%| D[Keep in FP16\nor INT8]
    C -->|no| E[Binarize to +1/-1]
    E --> F[Scale factor\nper group]
    D --> G[Mixed: FP16 salient\n+ binary rest]
    F --> G
```

> PB-LLM ("Partially Binarized") proposes a hybrid strategy where a fraction of weights are binarized ({-1, +1}) while the most salient weights (by Hessian-based importance) are retained at full precision (FP8 or FP16). This avoids the catastrophic perplexity collapse of full binarization while still achieving very low average bit-width. The saliency metric is the Hessian diagonal, and the method is applied post-training without QAT - making it a PTQ approach despite being in the extreme low-bit category.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICLR 2024** |
| Paper | [2310.00034](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.00034) |
| Precision | W1/W16 A16 (binary weights + FP16 for salient subset) |
| Granularity | per-tensor (binary part); FP16 for salient |
| Calibration | calibration set for saliency |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | salient weights identified by Hessian and kept at FP8/FP16; rest binarized |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | significantly better than full binarization; approaches W3 PTQ quality at ~1.5 effective bits |
| Builds on | [rtn](#rtn) · [gptq](#gptq) |
| Related | [spqr](#spqr) · [bitnet](#bitnet) · [billm](#billm) |

**How it works:**

For each linear layer, compute saliency s_ij = [H]_ii · w_ij² (Hessian diagonal × weight squared) for each weight. Keep the top-p% of weights (by saliency) at FP16 and binarize the rest. The effective bit-width is approximately 1 + p × 15 bits (the binary part is ~1 bit, the FP16 part is 16 bits weighted by fraction p). At p=0.5%, effective bits ≈ 1.08; at p=10%, ≈ 2.5. A key finding is that the salient weights form a sparse pattern with no structure (unlike pruning where rows or columns can be dropped), so sparse matrix operations are needed.

---

---

## KV-Cache Quantization

The attention KV cache grows linearly with context length and becomes the dominant memory consumer at long contexts. Quantizing K and V tensors to 4 or fewer bits reduces memory pressure, increases effective batch size, and enables longer contexts - at the cost of a small attention quality penalty.

<a id="kvarn"></a>
### KVarN · W16A16KV2 · KV Quant · 2026-06

<img src="../assets/diagrams/kvarn.svg" width="640" alt="KVarN diagram">
<p><em>KVarN: per-head Hadamard rotation then dual-axis variance normalization before 2-bit KV quantization.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[K and V tensors\nfrom attention] --> B[Compression strategy:\nper-head Hadamard rotation in ]
    B --> C[Quantize or prune\nKV to W16A16KV2]
    C --> D[Store compressed\nKV cache]
    D --> E[Decompress during\nattention computation]
    E --> F[Lower memory\nlonger context]
```

> KVarN compresses the KV cache to 2 bits without any calibration data, targeting the setting where long reasoning chains make the cache the memory bottleneck. The authors observe that quantization error behaves differently during autoregressive decoding than in prefill-only evaluations: errors compound across decoding steps, mostly because individual tokens get the wrong scale. To fix this they first apply a per-head Hadamard rotation to spread out channel outliers, then iteratively normalize variance along both the token and channel axes before round-to-nearest quantization. All transforms run online at inference, so no per-model calibration pass is needed, and the method ships as vLLM kernels with low runtime overhead.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2606.03458](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03458) |
| Code | [huawei-csl/KVarN](https://github.com/huawei-csl/KVarN) |
| Precision | W16A16KV2 |
| Granularity | per-head Hadamard rotation; keys quantized per-channel, values per-token; group size 128; ~2.3 bits/element average with FP16 sink (128) and trailing (128) tokens |
| Calibration | none (calibration-free; transforms applied online at inference) |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | per-head Hadamard rotation in the channel dimension plus iterative dual-axis variance normalization (Sinkhorn-style) to correct outlying token scales |
| Hardware target | GPU; vLLM kernels (0.18% quantization overhead, <=1.4% dequantization overhead) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | Qwen3-4B at 2-bit KV: AIME24 60.0% vs 61.1% FP16, MATH500 79.2% vs 82.6%, HumanEval 88.4% vs 88.8% |
| Builds on | [rotatekv](#rotatekv) · [kivi](#kivi) · [quarot](#quarot) |
| Related | [kivi](#kivi) · [kvquant](#kvquant) · [rotatekv](#rotatekv) · [skvq](#skvq) · [pmkvq](#pmkvq) |

**How it works:**

Keys and values are first rotated per-head with a Hadamard matrix in the channel dimension to make their distributions more uniform and suppress outlier channels. The core addition is a dual-scaling variance normalization: scales are computed and balanced across both the token axis and the channel axis using an iterative Sinkhorn-style procedure, which corrects the per-token scale errors the authors identify as the main driver of error accumulation during decoding. After normalization, K and V are quantized with asymmetric round-to-nearest (keys per-channel, values per-token, group size 128), keeping the first 128 sink tokens and last 128 tokens in FP16. The whole pipeline is calibration-free and runs online.

---

<a id="hqmq"></a>
### HQMQ · W16A16KV4 (~3.79-5 effective bits per KV element) · KV Quant · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/hqmq.svg" width="640" alt="HQMQ diagram">
<p><em>Each 4-element KV chunk becomes a quaternion: radius scalar-quantized, direction snapped to the product of a 24-point Hurwitz group and a random per-head codebook (24S codes from S stored), with Med3x outlier chunks kept in FP16.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[K and V tensors\nfrom attention] --> B[Compression strategy:\nMed3x per-batch median-multipl]
    B --> C[Quantize or prune\nKV to W16A16KV4 approx 3.79-5 effective bits per KV element]
    C --> D[Store compressed\nKV cache]
    D --> E[Decompress during\nattention computation]
    E --> F[Lower memory\nlonger context]
```

> HQMQ compresses the KV cache of an LLM without any calibration data or training. It splits each key or value vector into chunks of four numbers and treats each chunk as a quaternion, separating it into a magnitude (the radius) and a direction (a point on the unit 3-sphere). The radius is quantized with a plain uniform scalar quantizer using a per-token-max scale, while the direction is snapped to one of a fixed set of points. Those points come from multiplying the 24 unit quaternions of the Hurwitz group (the corners of the 24-cell) by a small per-layer, per-head codebook of randomly drawn unit quaternions, so a codebook of S entries gives 24*S effective directions while only S need to be stored. Because left- multiplying by a unit quaternion is a rotation of the sphere, the random seed barely affects accuracy. A cheap per-batch step pulls out the few chunks whose norm exceeds three times the batch median and keeps them in FP16, which is enough to handle outlier-heavy architectures. The result is near-lossless KV compression down to roughly four bits per element, shrinking a 128k-context Llama-3-70B cache from 43 GB to 8.5 GB.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.27646](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27646) |
| Precision | W16A16KV4 (~3.79-5 effective bits per KV element) |
| Granularity | per-(layer, head) codebook; 4-element chunks treated as quaternions; per-token-max radius scale |
| Calibration | none (calibration-free) |
| Symmetric? | n/a (direction quantized to discrete codewords on S^3; radius via uniform scalar quantizer) |
| Outlier handling | Med3x per-batch median-multiplier extraction: chunks with norm > C*median (C=3) stored as FP16 4-tuples |
| Hardware target | GPU (reduces KV-cache memory footprint) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | matches FP16 within 0.02-0.03 ppl at ~5 bits on Mistral-7B and Qwen3-8B (with Med3x, 0.02-0.10 ppl on Qwen2.5-7B/Qwen3-8B where naive INT4 collapses to 1e4+ ppl); zero-shot accuracy matches FP16 at 3.79 bits on Mistral-7B |
| Builds on | [rtn](#rtn) · [kivi](#kivi) |
| Related | [kivi](#kivi) · [kvquant](#kvquant) · [rotatekv](#rotatekv) · [palu](#palu) |

**How it works:**

Each 4-element chunk x of K or V is written in polar form x = r*u with radius r = ||x|| and unit direction u in S^3. The direction is quantized multiplicatively: u is matched to the product q_p * q_s, where q_p ranges over the 24-element Hurwitz group 2T (binary tetrahedral group; the 24 vertices of the 24-cell, pairwise 60 degrees apart) and q_s ranges over a per-(layer, head) secondary codebook of S random unit quaternions. This yields 24*S codewords from only S stored parameters; because left-multiplication is an S^3 isometry, random codebooks suffice and seed variation in end-task perplexity is under 1.5%. The radius is quantized separately with a uniform scalar quantizer (b_r in [3,6] bits, per-token-max scale). An outlier step (Med3x) marks any chunk whose norm exceeds C=3 times the per-batch median chunk norm and stores it as an FP16 4-tuple, with C=3 fixed across all models and no calibration.

<a id="quantized-keys-steal-attention"></a>
### QKSA · W16A16KV2 · KV Quant · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/quantized-keys-steal-attention.svg" width="640" alt="QKSA diagram">
<p><em>Convex softmax inflates quantized-key attention mass (Jensen bias); an analytic per-score correction from key step size and query norm cancels it before softmax.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[K and V tensors\nfrom attention] --> B[Compression strategy:\nanalytic per-attention-score a]
    B --> C[Quantize or prune\nKV to W16A16KV2]
    C --> D[Store compressed\nKV cache]
    D --> E[Decompress during\nattention computation]
    E --> F[Lower memory\nlonger context]
```

> When the KV cache of a video diffusion model is quantized to very low bit-widths, the rounding noise on the cached keys does not average out inside the attention softmax. Because the softmax exponential is convex, the noise systematically inflates how much attention mass the quantized (past) keys receive, draining attention away from the unquantized current chunk. The authors call this the Jensen bias and show it is the main reason INT2 KV caches degrade. They derive a closed-form estimate of the bias from the key quantization step sizes and the query norm, then subtract it from each attention score before the softmax. The correction needs no extra memory, no calibration data, and no retraining, and adds almost no compute, letting INT2 KV caches reach near-BF16 video quality while beating INT4 at half the memory.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICML 2026 Workshops (SCALE, F2S)** |
| Paper | [2605.26266](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26266) |
| Precision | W16A16KV2 |
| Granularity | per-token / per-group KV-cache quantization (granularity not explicitly fixed in abstract) |
| Calibration | none - correction is computed analytically on-the-fly from quantization step sizes and query norm |
| Symmetric? | n/a |
| Outlier handling | analytic per-attention-score additive correction that subtracts the softmax Jensen bias instead of suppressing outliers |
| Hardware target | GPU (video diffusion inference; correction fuses into the attention kernel with negligible overhead) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | at INT2 KV cache recovers most quality lost to quantization, approaching BF16 and outperforming INT4 at ~50% of its memory, on MAGI-1, SkyReels-V2, and HY-WorldPlay video diffusion models |
| Builds on | [kivi](#kivi) |
| Related | [kvquant](#kvquant) · [kivi](#kivi) · [sageattention](#sageattention) |

**How it works:**

For each attention score s_i = q . k_i, quantizing the key adds zero-mean noise, but the softmax weight exp(s_i) is convex, so E[exp(s_i + noise)] > exp(s_i) by a Jensen gap proportional to the score variance. The score variance is estimated analytically from the per-key quantization step size and the query vector norm, giving a second-order Taylor approximation of the bias for every cached key. This per-score correction term is subtracted from the logits before softmax so the inflated attention mass of quantized keys is removed, restoring the correct balance between past (quantized) and current (full-precision) tokens. The whole correction is data-free and computed on-the-fly at inference time.

---

<a id="rcbeqa"></a>
### RCBEQA · W16A16KV8/KV4 · KV Quant · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/rcbeqa.svg" width="640" alt="RCBEQA diagram">
<p><em>Tiered KV cache (INT8 keys / INT4 values on GPU, FP16 originals in RAM) with online per-head per-step error bounds driving adaptive precision and a multi-stage fallback ladder to exact attention.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[K and V tensors\nfrom attention] --> B[Compression strategy:\nonline per-head per-step error]
    B --> C[Quantize or prune\nKV to W16A16KV8/KV4]
    C --> D[Store compressed\nKV cache]
    D --> E[Decompress during\nattention computation]
    E --> F[Lower memory\nlonger context]
```

> This work treats KV-cache quantization as a computation that is checked at run time rather than trusted blindly. Keys are stored as INT8 and values as INT4 in GPU memory, while the original FP16 keys and values are kept in ordinary system RAM. For every attention step and every head, the method computes two error quantities online: how much the quantized keys distort the attention weight distribution, and how much the quantized values distort the reconstructed output. These computed bounds decide, on the fly, whether the quantized result is acceptable or whether the system should climb a ladder of fallback options, ending if necessary in an exact attention recomputation using the FP16 originals from RAM. The aim is not faster inference but safe deployment of aggressive KV compression: each attention result is guaranteed to be either within a known bound of the FP16 reference or exactly recovered.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.20868](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20868) |
| Precision | W16A16KV8/KV4 |
| Granularity | per-head, per-step (per attention computation); INT8 keys, INT4 values |
| Calibration | none (error bounds computed online at runtime, not from calibration data) |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | online per-head per-step error bounds drive adaptive precision and a multi-stage fallback ladder that can recover exact FP16 attention from RAM-resident originals |
| Hardware target | GPU (quantized KV in GPU memory) with FP16 originals retained in system RAM |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | Matches dense FP16 KV within noise on PG-19 (language modelling) and NIAH/RULER (retrieval) for LLaMA-3.1-8B at contexts up to 128K; recovers catastrophic failures seen in naive INT8/INT4 baselines. Value-sensitive short-context tasks show a controlled compression/fidelity trade-off, removable via tighter value tolerances or FP16-value fallback. |
| Builds on | [kivi](#kivi) · [kvquant](#kvquant) |
| Related | [kvquant](#kvquant) · [kivi](#kivi) · [skvq](#skvq) · [gear](#gear) · [sageattention](#sageattention) |

**How it works:**

A tiered KV cache stores INT8 keys and INT4 values on the GPU while the FP16 originals stay in system RAM as a deterministic fallback source. A two-term error decomposition gives per- head, per-step bounds on (i) attention-distribution distortion caused by key quantization and (ii) value reconstruction error. These bounds are evaluated online and used both to select precision adaptively and to trigger a multi-stage fallback ladder; when a bound is exceeded the system escalates, ultimately recomputing the exact dense attention output from the RAM-resident FP16 originals. The certification is local (per-head, per-step): it guarantees each attention computation is either bounded relative to an FP16 reference or exactly recovered, rather than guaranteeing end-to-end model correctness.
