<a id="oscar"></a>
### OSCAR · W16A16KV2 · KV Quant · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/oscar.svg" width="640" alt="OSCAR diagram">
<p><em>OSCAR: offline spectral covariance-aware rotations (eigendecomp + Hadamard + bit-reversal) and per-token clip thresholds enable ~2.28-bit asymmetric INT2 KV cache with fused SGLang kernels.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[K and V tensors\nfrom attention] --> B[Compression strategy:\ncovariance-aware rotations ei]
    B --> C[Quantize or prune\nKV to W16A16KV2]
    C --> D[Store compressed\nKV cache]
    D --> E[Decompress during\nattention computation]
    E --> F[Lower memory\nlonger context]
```

> OSCAR is a post-training method for compressing the KV cache of large language models down to roughly 2 bits per element so that long reasoning traces and 128K-token contexts fit in far less memory. The core observation is that what matters is not how well the rotated keys and values themselves quantize, but how well attention outputs survive quantization, so the rotations are chosen to be aware of the attention computation rather than being generic Hadamard transforms. All of the expensive analysis (the rotations and the clipping thresholds) is done once offline from a small calibration set, leaving a fixed rotation and clip per layer that can be applied cheaply at serving time. At inference, keys and values are rotated, clipped, and packed into INT2 with token-wise scales and zero-points, with a small number of sink and recent tokens left in BF16. The result keeps accuracy close to BF16 on reasoning and long-context tasks while cutting KV-cache memory about 8x.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.17757](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17757) |
| Code | [FutureMLS-Lab/OSCAR](https://github.com/FutureMLS-Lab/OSCAR) |
| Blog / post | [link](https://oscar-quantize.github.io/) |
| Precision | W16A16KV2 |
| Granularity | per-token affine asymmetric INT2 (block size 128 for scale/zero metadata); BF16 sink/recent-token protection windows |
| Calibration | one-time offline calibration on a small set to estimate attention-aware covariances and per-layer/per-token clip thresholds |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | covariance-aware rotations (eigendecomposition of attention-aware target covariances, composed with Hadamard mixing + bit-reversal permutation) plus offline percentile-based clip thresholds and small BF16 sink/recent-token windows |
| Hardware target | GPU (fused Triton kernels for rotation/quantization/packing; integrated into SGLang paged attention and vLLM) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | 3.78-point gap vs BF16 on Qwen3-4B-Thinking-2507 and 1.42-point gap on Qwen3-8B across AIME25, GPQA-Diamond, HumanEval, LiveCodeBench v6, and MATH-500; near-BF16 on Qwen3-32B and GLM-4; effective ~2.28 bits/KV element |
| Builds on | [kivi](#kivi) · [kvquant](#kvquant) · [quarot](#quarot) |
| Related | [kivi](#kivi) · [kvquant](#kvquant) · [rotatekv](#rotatekv) · [skvq](#skvq) · [gear](#gear) |

**How it works:**

For each layer, OSCAR builds separate rotations for keys and values by eigendecomposing attention-aware target covariances (the rotation for keys comes from the query-side covariance C_Q = U_Q Lambda_Q U_Q^T so that R_k := U_Q aligns the key subspace with how queries actually read it, and the value rotation comes from the softmax/score-weighted covariance C_S), then composes these spectral rotations with a Hadamard mixing matrix and a bit-reversal permutation to spread remaining energy evenly across channels. Offline calibration also fits per-layer percentile-based clip thresholds (typical c_K ~ 0.96, c_V ~ 0.92) to bound outliers before rounding. At serving time the rotated, clipped K/V are quantized with token-wise affine asymmetric INT2 (scale + zero-point, block size 128 metadata), keeping sink and recent tokens in BF16, giving about 2.28 effective bits per KV element. Fused Triton kernels perform rotation, quantization, and packing inside SGLang's paged attention, yielding up to ~7x throughput at large batch sizes and up to ~3x batch-1 decode speedup.

<a id="vericache"></a>
### VeriCache · W16A16KV2 (drafts with 2-4 bit or token-dropped KV; verifies against full FP16 KV) · KV Quant · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/vericache.svg" width="640" alt="VeriCache diagram">
<p><em>VeriCache: a compressed in-GPU KV cache drafts tokens while the off-GPU full KV cache is swapped in to verify them, overlapping HBM-bound drafting with PCIe-bound verification for lossless output.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[K and V tensors\nfrom attention] --> B[Compression strategy:\nverification against the full ]
    B --> C[Quantize or prune\nKV to W16A16KV2 drafts with 2-4 bit or token-dropped KV; verifies against full FP16 KV]
    C --> D[Store compressed\nKV cache]
    D --> E[Decompress during\nattention computation]
    E --> F[Lower memory\nlonger context]
```

> VeriCache makes lossy KV-cache compression produce exactly the same tokens as full-precision decoding. The problem it targets is that token-dropping and KV quantization methods drift further from the full-cache output the longer a model generates, which breaks long outputs like code and tool-call sequences. VeriCache borrows the draft-and-verify pattern from speculative decoding: it cheaply drafts tokens using a compressed KV cache kept in GPU memory, then verifies those drafts against a full KV cache that lives off the GPU (in CPU memory or on disk). Because drafting is HBM-bandwidth-bound and the full-cache swap is PCIe/network-bound, the two are run in parallel so verification overhead is largely hidden, recovering much of the throughput of the underlying compression method while guaranteeing identical results.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.17613](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17613) |
| Precision | W16A16KV2 (drafts with 2-4 bit or token-dropped KV; verifies against full FP16 KV) |
| Granularity | per-token / per-channel via the underlying compressor (KIVI, KVQuant, RotateKV, SnapKV, etc.) |
| Calibration | none - wraps an existing compressor; no extra calibration data |
| Symmetric? | n/a (inherits from the wrapped KV compression method) |
| Outlier handling | verification against the full KV cache: every drafted token is checked, so any compression-induced error is corrected rather than tolerated |
| Hardware target | GPU with off-GPU full-KV storage (CPU RAM / local or remote disk); evaluated on NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 96GB and 2xH100 NVL (TP=2) over PCIe 5.0 |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | lossless - bit-identical to full-KV decoding (KL divergence < 0.01 nats, attributable to hardware nondeterminism) on Mistral-24B, Qwen-32B, Llama-70B across ComplexFuncBench, LongGenBench, GSM8K-Long, PISanitizer, LMCache-trace |
| Builds on | [kivi](#kivi) · [kvquant](#kvquant) · [snapkv](#snapkv) |
| Related | [kivi](#kivi) · [kvquant](#kvquant) · [snapkv](#snapkv) · [gear](#gear) · [skvq](#skvq) |

**How it works:**

The compressed KV cache (any token-dropping or quantization scheme exposed through a uniform compressor interface) drafts a window of candidate tokens. The full KV cache, stored off-GPU to save HBM, is streamed back in to recompute attention and verify the drafts in a single batched forward pass, accepting the longest correct prefix exactly as in speculative decoding so the final output is provably identical to full-KV decoding. The core systems contribution is overlapping the HBM-bandwidth-bound compressed-KV drafting with the PCIe/network-bandwidth-bound full-KV swap-in, plus scheduling that minimizes the cost of bringing the full cache on-chip only for verification.

<a id="triaxialkv"></a>
### TriAxialKV · W16A16KV2-KV4 mixed · KV Quant · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/triaxialkv.svg" width="640" alt="TriAxialKV diagram">
<p><em>TriAxialKV: each KV token gets a triaxial tag (recency, modality, semantic role); a budgeted allocator assigns INT2/INT4 per tag under a fixed memory budget, served via custom Triton kernels.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[K and V tensors\nfrom attention] --> B[Compression strategy:\nbudgeted bit allocation that k]
    B --> C[Quantize or prune\nKV to W16A16KV2-KV4 mixed]
    C --> D[Store compressed\nKV cache]
    D --> E[Decompress during\nattention computation]
    E --> F[Lower memory\nlonger context]
```

> Agentic LLM workloads such as computer-use agents build up very long KV caches because they keep around screenshots, tool outputs, and long histories. TriAxialKV compresses that cache by giving each token a tag along three axes: how recent it is, what modality it came from (text vs image), and its semantic role in the trace. Tokens judged more important keep more bits (INT4) while the rest are squeezed to INT2, all under a fixed memory budget. The authors wrap this in a serving system with per-tag calibration, memory management, and custom Triton decode kernels so the compression turns into real throughput gains.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.17170](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17170) |
| Precision | W16A16KV2-KV4 mixed |
| Granularity | per-token mixed precision via triaxial tag (recency, modality, semantic role); per-tag calibration |
| Calibration | per-tag sensitivity calibration on agentic traces; no gradient training |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | budgeted bit allocation that keeps sensitive tokens (recent, image-modality, or semantically important) at INT4 while pushing the rest to INT2 |
| Hardware target | GPU (custom Triton decode kernels; integrated into SGLang) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | matches BF16 KV-cache accuracy of SGLang on OSWorld with Qwen3-VL-32B-Thinking, while supporting 4.5x KV cache size and ~30% higher end-to-end throughput |
| Builds on | [kivi](#kivi) · [kvquant](#kvquant) · [skvq](#skvq) |
| Related | [kvquant](#kvquant) · [kivi](#kivi) · [skvq](#skvq) · [pmkvq](#pmkvq) · [snapkv](#snapkv) · [zipcache](#zipcache) |

**How it works:**

Each KV-cache token is assigned a triaxial tag describing its temporal recency, its source modality, and its semantic role in the agentic trace. A calibration step measures per-tag sensitivity and then a budgeted allocator chooses INT2 or INT4 for each tag so total memory stays under a target while accuracy-critical tokens retain precision. The scheme is realized end-to-end with mixed-precision storage, a memory manager, and custom Triton decode kernels integrated into SGLang, converting the size reduction into higher decode throughput.

---

<a id="fibquant"></a>
### FibQuant · W16A16KV2 (also fractional-bit and sub-1-bit operating points) · KV Quant · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/fibquant.svg" width="640" alt="FibQuant diagram">
<p><em>FibQuant: normalize and Haar-rotate a KV block into a spherical-Beta source, then encode with a shared radial-angular codebook (Beta-quantile radii x Fibonacci-lattice directions) for fixed-address random-access codes.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[K and V tensors\nfrom attention] --> B[Compression strategy:\nnormalization store norm plu]
    B --> C[Quantize or prune\nKV to W16A16KV2 also fractional-bit and sub-1-bit operating points]
    C --> D[Store compressed\nKV cache]
    D --> E[Decompress during\nattention computation]
    E --> F[Lower memory\nlonger context]
```

> FibQuant compresses the key-value cache used during long-context inference, where the cache grows with context, batch, layers, and heads and is re-read at every decoding step. It keeps the same lightweight pipeline used by rotation-based scalar codecs (store a per-block norm, apply a shared random rotation, then encode) but observes that after the rotation a block of consecutive coordinates is not independent values; it is a point on a sphere following a spherical-Beta distribution. Instead of quantizing one coordinate at a time with scalar tables, FibQuant uses a single shared codebook over that spherical geometry, picking radii from Beta quantiles and directions from quasi-uniform Fibonacci/Roberts-Kronecker lattices, then refining with Lloyd-Max. Because every token maps to a fixed-size, fixed-address code, the cache stays randomly accessible and the method needs no calibration data, while supporting a continuous range of bit-rates including fractional and sub-one-bit settings.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.11478](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11478) |
| Precision | W16A16KV2 (also fractional-bit and sub-1-bit operating points) |
| Granularity | per-block of k consecutive coordinates (per-token, normalize-then-rotate) |
| Calibration | none - calibration-free fixed-rate codebook |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | normalization (store norm) plus shared Haar rotation; spherical-Beta source modeling absorbs scale via radial-angular code |
| Hardware target | GPU long-context inference; random-access fixed-address per-token codes, no variable-length addresses |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | TinyLlama-1.1B: within 0.10 perplexity of fp16 at 4x compression; 3.6x lower perplexity than scalar TurboQuant at b=2 (8x). GPT-2 small KV caches: attention cosine similarity 0.99 at 5x to 0.95 at 34x. |
| Builds on | [turboqu](#turboqu) |
| Related | [turboqu](#turboqu) · [kvquant](#kvquant) · [kivi](#kivi) · [quarot](#quarot) |

**How it works:**

After normalizing a KV block and applying a shared Haar rotation, the k consecutive coordinates form a spherical-Beta source on the unit ball rather than a product of independent scalars. FibQuant replaces the scalar quantization tables of rotation-based codecs with one shared radial-angular vector codebook matched to this canonical source: Beta-quantile radii set the magnitude levels, Fibonacci/Roberts-Kronecker quasi-uniform lattice points set the directions on the sphere, and multi-restart Lloyd-Max refines the cells. The construction is proven to strictly improve on its scalar product specialization at matched rate, with the high-rate gain decomposing into a cell-shaping factor and a density-matching factor. The fixed-rate design yields fixed-address per-token codes (preserving random access) and a dense rate axis down to sub-one-bit without calibration or variable-length addressing.

<a id="windowquant"></a>
### WindowQuant · W16A16KV2-KV4 mixed (avg ~3.17-bit) · KV Quant · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/windowquant.svg" width="640" alt="WindowQuant diagram">
<p><em>WindowQuant: text-similarity scores assign per-window KV bit-widths; same-precision windows reordered for efficient mixed-precision attention.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[K and V tensors\nfrom attention] --> B[Compression strategy:\nwindow-level mixed precision k]
    B --> C[Quantize or prune\nKV to W16A16KV2-KV4 mixed avg approx 3.17-bit]
    C --> D[Store compressed\nKV cache]
    D --> E[Decompress during\nattention computation]
    E --> F[Lower memory\nlonger context]
```

> WindowQuant compresses the key-value cache for video-language models, where the KV cache is dominated by large numbers of visual tokens. Instead of quantizing every token uniformly or token-by-token, it groups tokens into fixed-size windows and assigns each window a bit-width based on how similar that window's visual content is to the text prompt, so windows that matter more for the answer are kept at higher precision and less relevant windows are compressed harder. To keep the resulting mixed-precision layout fast on hardware, it reorders the cache windows so that windows sharing the same bit-width are computed together, avoiding the irregular memory access that usually makes mixed-precision slow. The method is post-training and only needs a small calibration sample to set the similarity-based thresholds.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ACM TACO** |
| Paper | [2605.02262](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.02262) |
| Precision | W16A16KV2-KV4 mixed (avg ~3.17-bit) |
| Granularity | per-window group-wise (default window size 32 tokens) |
| Calibration | sampled calibration set for window-level visual-text similarity / hidden-state similarity |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | window-level mixed precision keeps high-similarity (more informative) windows at higher bit-width; window reordering groups same-precision windows for efficient computation |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | On LLaVA-OneVision-Qwen2-7B: NExT-QA, EgoSchema, IntentQA accuracy within ~1-2% of FP16 baseline while reaching ~3.17 average bits; ~3x decoding throughput and 0.8-6.48 GB KV memory saved |
| Builds on | [kivi](#kivi) · [kvquant](#kvquant) |
| Related | [kivi](#kivi) · [kvquant](#kvquant) · [pmkvq](#pmkvq) · [skvq](#skvq) · [qaq](#qaq) |

**How it works:**

WindowQuant has two parts. (1) Window-level quantization search: visual tokens are split into windows (default 32 tokens); a similarity score between each window and the text prompt is computed, and windows are assigned bit-widths (e.g. INT2 vs INT4, with the most important content kept at FP16) so that more text-relevant windows receive higher precision under an average-bit budget (~3.17 bits). Quantization within a window is asymmetric group-wise round-to-nearest with per-group scale and zero-point. (2) Window-level KV cache computation: before attention, windows are reordered so windows of the same precision are contiguous, eliminating the hardware inefficiency (irregular access, branch divergence) that normally penalizes mixed-precision KV caches, which yields roughly 3x decoding throughput and several GB of memory savings.

<a id="ratequant"></a>
### RateQuant · W16 A16 KV2-8 mixed (per-head, 2.5-6.0 average bits) · KV Quant · 2026-04

<img src="../assets/diagrams/ratequant.svg" width="640" alt="RateQuant diagram">
<p><em>RateQuant: fit a per-quantizer distortion curve and per-head sensitivities, then reverse-waterfill bits across attention heads.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[K and V tensors\nfrom attention] --> B[Compression strategy:\ninherits the base quantizer's ]
    B --> C[Quantize or prune\nKV to W16 A16 KV2-8 mixed per-head, 2.5-6.0 average bits]
    C --> D[Store compressed\nKV cache]
    D --> E[Decompress during\nattention computation]
    E --> F[Lower memory\nlonger context]
```

> RateQuant decides how many bits to give each attention head when compressing the key-value cache, instead of using the same bit-width everywhere. The authors observe that heads differ a lot in how much they matter, and that each underlying KV quantizer has its own error- versus-bits curve with a different decay rate. If you allocate bits using the wrong curve, the head ranking can flip and you end up worse than just using uniform bits, a failure they call distortion model mismatch. RateQuant measures each head's sensitivity and fits the actual distortion curve of whichever quantizer is in use from a tiny calibration set, then computes the bit assignment in closed form. It works as a layer on top of existing KV quantizers (KIVI, QuaRot, TurboQuant) and adds essentially no inference cost.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.06675](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06675) |
| Precision | W16 A16 KV2-8 mixed (per-head, 2.5-6.0 average bits) |
| Granularity | per-head bit allocation (optionally separate keys/values, 2N components) |
| Calibration | 16 WikiText-2 sequences (length 512) for gradient sensitivity plus a per-quantizer distortion-curve fit; ~1.6s for 8B, ~4.2s for 32B on one H200 |
| Symmetric? | mixed |
| Outlier handling | inherits the base quantizer's outlier handling (e.g. QuaRot Hadamard rotations, KIVI per-token asymmetric values); RateQuant only allocates bits |
| Hardware target | GPU (calibrated on NVIDIA H200); static ~2KB lookup table, zero inference overhead |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | Qwen3-8B WikiText-2 at 2.5 avg bits: lowers KIVI perplexity 49.3 -> 14.9; raises QuaRot by 6.6 PPL (34.88 -> 28.33); TurboQuant at 4.0 bits 9.94 -> 9.67 (FP16 baseline 9.53) |
| Builds on | [kivi](#kivi) · [quarot](#quarot) · [turboqu](#turboqu) |
| Related | [kvquant](#kvquant) · [pmkvq](#pmkvq) · [skvq](#skvq) · [ramp](#ramp) |

**How it works:**

RateQuant treats per-head KV-cache bit allocation as a rate-distortion problem. It estimates each head's importance with gradient-based sensitivity weights, fits a per-quantizer exponential distortion model D(b)=alpha*beta^(-b) from a small calibration pass, and then solves for the optimal per-head bit count using a reverse-waterfilling result: b*_i = b_bar + (ln w_i - ln w_bar)/ln beta, with a greedy integer rounding step (assign each next bit to the head with maximum marginal distortion reduction). Because beta is measured for the specific base quantizer rather than assumed, RateQuant avoids distortion model mismatch where applying one quantizer's decay rate to another inverts the head ranking and hurts accuracy. It optionally allocates keys and values separately, doubling the number of components to 2N.

<a id="saw-int4"></a>
### SAW-INT4 · W16A16KV4 · KV Quant · 2026-04

<img src="../assets/diagrams/saw-int4.svg" width="640" alt="SAW-INT4 diagram">
<p><em>Per-token INT4 K/V quantization preceded by block-diagonal Hadamard rotation (block size 64-128), fused into a paged-KV FlashAttention kernel.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[K and V tensors\nfrom attention] --> B[Compression strategy:\nblock-diagonal Hadamard rotati]
    B --> C[Quantize or prune\nKV to W16A16KV4]
    C --> D[Store compressed\nKV cache]
    D --> E[Decompress during\nattention computation]
    E --> F[Lower memory\nlonger context]
```

> SAW-INT4 compresses the KV cache to 4 bits in a way that survives the constraints of an actual serving stack rather than only a lab benchmark. The authors find that pairing plain per-token (token-wise) INT4 quantization with a block-diagonal Hadamard rotation gives the best balance of accuracy and speed, because the rotation spreads out the large-magnitude channels in the keys that otherwise wreck naive INT4. They build the rotation directly into a fused quantization kernel that fits paged KV-cache layouts and FlashAttention-style execution, so it adds no measurable runtime cost over unrotated INT4. The framing is that KV-cache compression should be treated as a joint accuracy-and-systems problem: the memory layout, paging, and fused attention path constrain which quantization designs are actually usable.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2604.19157](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.19157) |
| Code | [togethercomputer/saw-int4](https://github.com/togethercomputer/saw-int4) |
| Precision | W16A16KV4 |
| Granularity | per-token (token-wise), per-head; block-diagonal Hadamard with block size 64-128 |
| Calibration | none (rotation matrices are data-free Hadamard transforms) |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | block-diagonal Hadamard rotation (block size 64-128) redistributes channel outliers before per-token INT4 quantization, applied primarily to keys |
| Hardware target | NVIDIA H100 GPU; SGLang serving with PagedAttention and a fused rotation-quantization CUDA/Triton kernel matching plain INT4 throughput |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | Qwen3-4B-Thinking (32k seq, mean over GPQA-Diamond/HumanEval/LiveCodeBench v6/AIME25/MATH500): BF16 75.64 vs INT4+BDR-128 73.78 (~1.9 pt drop); naive INT4 collapses to 0. Qwen3-8B: BF16 70.84 vs INT4+BDR-64 69.86 (~1 pt drop). |
| Builds on | [kivi](#kivi) · [quarot](#quarot) |
| Related | [kvquant](#kvquant) · [rotatekv](#rotatekv) · [spinquant](#spinquant) · [skvq](#skvq) |

**How it works:**

Keys and values are quantized to asymmetric INT4 with per-token (and per-head) scales and zero-points. Before quantizing, a block-diagonal Hadamard rotation (block size 64-128) is applied so that outlier channels, concentrated in the keys, are mixed across each block and no longer dominate a single quantization range; naive INT4 without this rotation collapses to near-zero accuracy. The rotation is composed so it can be fused with the quantization step and executed inside a paged-KV-cache, FlashAttention-style attention kernel, keeping throughput equal to plain INT4. The design choice is deliberately simple (rotation + per- token INT4) because more elaborate schemes do not fit the memory-layout and fused-execution constraints of real serving.

<a id="dont-waste-bits"></a>
### Don't Waste Bits! · W16A16KV2/4/8 (mixed; per-token chosen from {2,4,8,FP16}) · KV Quant · 2026-04

<img src="../assets/diagrams/dont-waste-bits.svg" width="640" alt="Don't Waste Bits! diagram">
<p><em>Per-token controller MLP maps {entropy, rarity, attention variance, confidence} to a precision level {2/4/8/FP16}; salient tokens get more bits, low-importance tokens fewer.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[K and V tensors\nfrom attention] --> B[Compression strategy:\nimportant/uncertain tokens are]
    B --> C[Quantize or prune\nKV to W16A16KV2/4/8 mixed; per-token chosen from 2,4,8,FP16]
    C --> D[Store compressed\nKV cache]
    D --> E[Decompress during\nattention computation]
    E --> F[Lower memory\nlonger context]
```

> This method treats KV-cache precision as a per-token budgeting problem rather than picking one fixed bit-width for everything. During decoding, a small learned controller looks at four cheap signals for the current token (entropy of the next-token distribution, a token- rarity score from training frequency, how spread-out the final-layer attention is, and the model's confidence) and decides whether that token's keys and values should be stored at 2, 4, 8, or 16 bits. The idea borrows the Huffman-coding intuition that frequent or low- importance items deserve fewer bits, so most tokens are stored cheaply while the few that matter keep high precision. On small SmolLM models this keeps accuracy almost identical to FP16 while lowering both memory traffic and per-token latency relative to static fixed-bit KV quantization.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **CVPR 2026** |
| Paper | [2604.04722](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.04722) |
| Code | [SayedPedramHaeri/Dont-Waste-Bits](https://github.com/SayedPedramHaeri/Dont-Waste-Bits) |
| Precision | W16A16KV2/4/8 (mixed; per-token chosen from {2,4,8,FP16}) |
| Granularity | per-token (precision assigned per decoded token; K and V treated equally) |
| Calibration | controller MLP trained on token-level saliency features; no weight-distribution calibration set required for the quantizer itself |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | important/uncertain tokens are kept at higher precision (8-bit or FP16) by the controller; low-importance tokens dropped to 2/4-bit |
| Hardware target | on-device / edge (memory- and bandwidth-constrained); evaluated on small SmolLM models |
| Training needed? | yes |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | within 0.30 accuracy points of FP16 on SmolLM-360M / HellaSwag (41.20% vs 41.50%), while cutting decoding latency ~17.75% (2.41 vs 3.50 ms/token) and beating the static 4-bit baseline (33.60%) by ~7.6 points |
| Builds on | [rtn](#rtn) |
| Related | [kvquant](#kvquant) · [kivi](#kivi) · [skvq](#skvq) · [qaq](#qaq) · [zipcache](#zipcache) |

**How it works:**

A three-layer MLP controller (two hidden layers of width 128, ReLU, softmax over precision classes) maps a 4-dimensional per-token feature vector -- next-token entropy, smoothed self- information / rarity, final-layer attention variance, and prediction confidence -- to one of four precision levels {2-bit, 4-bit, 8-bit, FP16}. The selected level is applied with uniform symmetric per-token quantization to that token's K and V entries as they are written to the cache. By spending bits proportional to estimated token importance, the scheme minimizes expected KV-cache memory and bandwidth while keeping salient tokens near full precision, trading a tiny accuracy loss for lower decoding latency.

<a id="pmkvq"></a>
### PM-KVQ · KV2-KV8 mixed progressive · KV Quant · 2025-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/pmkvq.svg" width="640" alt="PM-KVQ diagram">
<p><em>PM-KVQ: active CoT reasoning tokens at high precision; concluded chains compressed progressively.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[K and V tensors\nfrom attention] --> B[Compression strategy:\nhigher precision for early rea]
    B --> C[Quantize or prune\nKV to KV2-KV8 mixed progressive]
    C --> D[Store compressed\nKV cache]
    D --> E[Decompress during\nattention computation]
    E --> F[Lower memory\nlonger context]
```

> Long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning models generate thousands of tokens before giving a final answer, causing the KV cache to grow to extreme sizes. PM-KVQ addresses this with a progressive mixed-precision strategy: tokens in active reasoning steps are kept at higher precision, while concluded reasoning chains (past tokens no longer being extended) are compressed aggressively. This temporal structure of CoT reasoning enables much higher compression than static KV quantization without sacrificing reasoning quality.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICLR 2026** |
| Paper | [2505.18610](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18610) |
| Precision | KV2-KV8 mixed progressive |
| Granularity | per-layer, per-step progressive precision assignment |
| Calibration | chain-of-thought reasoning traces as calibration |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | higher precision for early reasoning tokens; compress concluded tokens |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | < 1% on CoT reasoning tasks vs FP16 KV cache |
| Builds on | [kivi](#kivi) · [kvquant](#kvquant) · [snapkv](#snapkv) |
| Related | [kvquant](#kvquant) · [kivi](#kivi) · [qaq](#qaq) · [zipcache](#zipcache) |

**How it works:**

PM-KVQ tracks the "reasoning state" of the KV cache: tokens belonging to the current active reasoning step receive high-precision quantization (KV8 or FP16), while tokens from completed reasoning branches are progressively compressed to KV2-KV4. The precision assignment is updated as generation proceeds. For very long CoT traces (thousands of tokens), the majority of the cache eventually gets compressed, achieving overall 2-3 bit average with near-lossless reasoning quality.

<a id="turboqu"></a>
### TurboQuant · W16A16KV2.5/KV3.5 (vector quantization) · KV Quant · 2025-04

<img src="../assets/diagrams/turboqu.svg" width="640" alt="TurboQuant diagram">
<p><em>TurboQuant: random rotation + per-coordinate scalar quantization for near-optimal KV cache compression.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[K and V tensors\nfrom attention] --> B[Compression strategy:\nrandom rotation induces concen]
    B --> C[Quantize or prune\nKV to W16A16KV2.5/KV3.5 vector quantization]
    C --> D[Store compressed\nKV cache]
    D --> E[Decompress during\nattention computation]
    E --> F[Lower memory\nlonger context]
```

> TurboQuant is a KV-cache quantization method that compresses keys and values during inference using an online vector quantization algorithm provably near-optimal in distortion rate. By randomly rotating input vectors and applying per-coordinate scalar quantizers, it achieves near-lossless KV compression at 3.5 bits/channel with no calibration data and virtually zero indexing overhead.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICLR 2026** |
| Paper | [2504.19874](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.19874) |
| Precision | W16A16KV2.5/KV3.5 (vector quantization) |
| Granularity | per-channel, online |
| Calibration | none - data-oblivious, applied at runtime |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | random rotation induces concentrated distribution; near-optimal rate-distortion coding |
| Hardware target | GPU (vLLM integration) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | quality-neutral at 3.5 bits/channel; marginal degradation at 2.5 bits/channel |
| Builds on | [kivi](#kivi) · [kvquant](#kvquant) |
| Related | [kivi](#kivi) · [kvquant](#kvquant) · [gear](#gear) · [rotatekv](#rotatekv) |

**How it works:**

TurboQuant addresses both MSE and inner-product distortion for KV vectors. It randomly rotates input vectors - inducing a concentrated Beta distribution on coordinates - then applies optimal scalar quantizers per coordinate, exploiting near-independence of dimensions in high-dimensional space. For unbiased inner-product estimation, a two-stage approach is used: an MSE quantizer followed by a 1-bit Quantized Johnson-Lindenstrauss transform on the residual. The method proves information-theoretic lower bounds and shows TurboQuant differs from optimal by only a small constant (~2.7x). Integrated with vLLM for production KV cache compression.

---

<a id="rotatekv"></a>
### RotateKV · KV2 (2-bit keys and values) · KV Quant · 2025-02

<img src="../assets/diagrams/rotatekv.svg" width="640" alt="RotateKV diagram">
<p><em>RotateKV: Hadamard rotation of K and V before 2-bit quantization eliminates outliers.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[K and V tensors\nfrom attention] --> B[Compression strategy:\nrandom Hadamard rotation remov]
    B --> C[Quantize or prune\nKV to KV2 2-bit keys and values]
    C --> D[Store compressed\nKV cache]
    D --> E[Decompress during\nattention computation]
    E --> F[Lower memory\nlonger context]
```

> RotateKV applies the same Hadamard rotation technique used in QuaRot and SpinQuant for weight/activation quantization to the KV cache problem. By rotating key and value tensors before quantization, RotateKV eliminates the outlier problem that makes 2-bit KV quantization challenging. The rotation is applied online (cheaply) before caching, and inverse-rotated during attention computation, enabling clean 2-bit KV storage with near-lossless attention quality.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **IJCAI 2025** |
| Paper | [0690.pdf](https://www.ijcai.org/proceedings/2025/0690.pdf) |
| Precision | KV2 (2-bit keys and values) |
| Granularity | per-head rotation then per-token quantization |
| Calibration | none |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | random Hadamard rotation removes outliers from K and V tensors |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | near-lossless at KV2; significantly better than unrotated 2-bit KV |
| Builds on | [quarot](#quarot) · [kivi](#kivi) · [kvquant](#kvquant) |
| Related | [kivi](#kivi) · [kvquant](#kvquant) · [wkvquant](#wkvquant) · [skvq](#skvq) |

**How it works:**

Standard KV tensors have heavy-tailed distributions with outlier channels that degrade 2-bit quantization. RotateKV applies a random Hadamard matrix R to K and V before storing them: K_rot = K * R^T, V_rot = V * R^T. The rotation spreads outliers incoherently across all dimensions, enabling uniform 2-bit quantization of K_rot and V_rot. During attention, the stored rotated KV are dequantized and inverse-rotated. The Hadamard transform is O(d log d) and fast enough for online use.

<a id="sageattention"></a>
### SageAttention · Q8K8V16 (INT8 Q and K for QK^T, FP16 V) · KV Quant · 2024-10

<img src="../assets/diagrams/sageattention.svg" width="640" alt="SageAttention diagram">
<p><em>SageAttention: INT8 Q and K for QK^T matmul, FP16 V for the AV weighted sum.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Query Q and Key K\nFP16] --> B[Per-token smooth\nquantization to INT8]
    B --> C[INT8 GEMM:\nQK^T on tensor cores]
    C --> D[Dequantize result\nbefore softmax]
    D --> E[Softmax in FP32]
    E --> F[FP16 Value V\nweighted sum AV]
    F --> G[2x attention speedup\nnear-zero quality loss]
```

> SageAttention quantizes Q and K to INT8 to compute the attention score matrix (QK^T) using integer tensor cores, while keeping V in FP16. A plug-and-play drop-in for standard attention requiring no fine-tuning or calibration. Per-token smooth quantization handles outliers. Achieves ~2x speedup on the attention operation with near-zero quality loss across language, vision, and video tasks.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICLR 2025** |
| Paper | [2410.02367](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.02367) |
| Code | [thu-ml/SageAttention](https://github.com/thu-ml/SageAttention) |
| Precision | Q8K8V16 (INT8 Q and K for QK^T, FP16 V) |
| Granularity | per-token for Q and K quantization |
| Calibration | none (dynamic at runtime) |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | per-token smooth quantization of Q and K before INT8 cast |
| Hardware target | GPU (CUDA); H100 / A100 optimized |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | < 0.5% on most tasks; 2.1x attention speedup on A100 |
| Builds on | [smoothquant](#smoothquant) |
| Related | [kvquant](#kvquant) · [kivi](#kivi) · [atom](#atom) |

**How it works:**

The QK^T matmul dominates attention FLOPs at long context. SageAttention replaces it with INT8 GEMM: each Q and K token vector is scaled to INT8 range independently (per-token quantization), QK^T is computed in INT8 on tensor cores, then dequantized before softmax. The weighted sum AV uses FP16 V unchanged. SageAttention2 (Nov 2024) extends to mixed INT4/INT8 Q/K for further speedup.

<a id="think"></a>
### ThinK · W16A16KV4 (with head-dimension pruning) · KV Quant · 2024-07

<img src="../assets/diagrams/think.svg" width="640" alt="ThinK diagram">
<p><em>Query-guided key-dimension pruning removes low-QK-correlation dimensions before quantization.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Query Q and Key K\nfor head h] --> B[Q-K channel correlation:\nI_j = norm Q_j hadamard K_j]
    B --> C[Rank dimensions\nby importance I_j]
    C --> D[Prune low-importance\nK cache dimensions]
    D --> E[20-40% key cache\nreduction]
    E --> F[Orthogonal to\nquantization methods]
```

> ThinK takes a different approach to KV cache compression: instead of quantizing all elements of the K cache, it prunes specific dimensions (channels) of the key vectors that contribute least to the query-key dot product. The importance of each key dimension is estimated from the query vectors - dimensions where Q·K correlates poorly across queries are pruned. This "thinner K" approach is combined with quantization of the retained dimensions, compressing the K cache in two orthogonal ways: fewer dimensions (pruning) and fewer bits per dimension (quantization).

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2407.21018](https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.21018) |
| Precision | W16A16KV4 (with head-dimension pruning) |
| Granularity | per-head, structured (channel pruning within head dimension) |
| Calibration | none |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | query-driven pruning removes low-attention key dimensions |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | < 0.5 ppl at 25% key cache reduction + INT4 quantization |
| Builds on | [kivi](#kivi) · [rtn](#rtn) |
| Related | [kivi](#kivi) · [kvquant](#kvquant) · [palu](#palu) |

**How it works:**

For head h with queries Q ∈ ℝ^{T_q × d} and keys K ∈ ℝ^{T_k × d}, compute the per-dimension importance score: I_j = ‖ Q_j ⊙ K_j ‖ (element-wise product of the j-th query and key dimension, summed over all heads and tokens). Low-importance dimensions (low I_j) contribute little to the attention score and can be pruned. Pruning removes the bottom-p% of key dimensions. The corresponding query dimensions are also zeroed to maintain attention correctness. Combined with INT4 quantization of the retained (d × (1-p)) key dimensions, ThinK achieves up to 25% fewer key cache bytes without quality degradation.

---

<a id="palu"></a>
### Palu · W16A16KV reduced-rank · KV Quant · 2024-07

<img src="../assets/diagrams/palu.svg" width="640" alt="Palu diagram">
<p><em>Low-rank KV projection: cache r-dim k_low instead of d-dim k; reconstruct at attention.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[K proj weight W_K\nd to d] --> B[SVD: W_K approx U_r Sigma V_r^T]
    B --> C[W_K_low in R^d_times_r\nW_K_proj in R^r_times_d]
    C --> D[k_low = x W_K_low\nstore in R^r]
    D --> E[At attention:\ndecode k = k_low W_K_proj]
    E --> F[r over d ratio\n= compression factor]
    F --> G[Combinable with\nKV quantization]
```

> Palu compresses the KV cache not by quantizing its values but by projecting K and V into a low-rank subspace before caching, and projecting back at attention time. The low-rank projection is learned offline by truncated SVD of the K and V projection matrices (W_K and W_V). If W_K ≈ U Σ Vᵀ and we keep only the top-r singular values, keys can be stored as low-rank factors rather than full-rank vectors, reducing the cache size by factor d/r. This is a fundamentally different approach from bit-reduction quantization - it reduces the dimensionality of the cache rather than the precision.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2407.21118](https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.21118) |
| Precision | W16A16KV reduced-rank |
| Granularity | per-head, low-rank projection |
| Calibration | calibration set for SVD of K/V projection matrices |
| Symmetric? | n/a (low-rank) |
| Outlier handling | low-rank projection inherently compresses; less sensitive to individual outliers |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | 50% K/V cache size reduction at < 1 ppl on LLaMA-2 |
| Builds on | [rtn](#rtn) |
| Related | [gear](#gear) · [think](#think) · [kvquant](#kvquant) |

**How it works:**

Original: k = x W_K (d→d projection). Palu: factorize W_K ≈ W_K^low W_K^proj where W_K^low ∈ ℝ^{d×r} and W_K^proj ∈ ℝ^{r×d}. Cache only the low-rank representation: k_low = x W_K^low ∈ ℝ^r (store r floats instead of d floats). At attention time, reconstruct: k = k_low W_K^proj for the dot product. The SVD of W_K is computed once offline from calibration data that tells which directions of k are most important. The rank r is the compression knob: r = d/2 gives 50% cache reduction. Palu also applies to V. The method can be combined with quantization of the low-rank k_low vectors.

---

<a id="pqcache"></a>
### PQCache · W16A16KV2-4 (product quantization) · KV Quant · 2024-07

<img src="../assets/diagrams/pqcache.svg" width="640" alt="PQCache diagram">
<p><em>PQCache: product quantization of KV vectors - each sub-vector maps to a codebook index.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Key or Value vector v] --> B[Split into m sub-vectors\nof dimension d/m each]
    B --> C[Assign each sub-vec\nto nearest codebook centroid]
    C --> D[Store m indices\ninstead of full vector]
    D --> E[Asymmetric distance:\nscore without full decode]
    E --> F[Product Quant KV\neffective 2-4 bits]
    F --> G[Long-context inference\nlower memory footprint]
```

> PQCache applies Product Quantization (PQ) to the KV cache - a technique borrowed from approximate nearest-neighbor search (FAISS) that decomposes each high-dimensional KV vector into sub-vectors, each quantized independently with a small codebook. This allows very low effective bit-widths (sub-2-bit per dimension) while maintaining good approximation quality for the attention computation, as the approximate KV entries are sufficient for the attention softmax to produce accurate output probabilities.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2407.12820](https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.12820) |
| Precision | W16A16KV2-4 (product quantization) |
| Granularity | per-head, vector quantization (PQ codebook) |
| Calibration | calibration data for codebook learning |
| Symmetric? | n/a (codebook-based) |
| Outlier handling | product quantization codebook absorbs all magnitudes |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | < 1 ppl at PQ-equivalent 2 bits on LLaMA-2; enables very long context at low memory |
| Builds on | [kivi](#kivi) · [rtn](#rtn) |
| Related | [kivi](#kivi) · [kvquant](#kvquant) · [aqlm](#aqlm) |

**How it works:**

Each KV vector of dimension d_head is divided into M sub-vectors of dimension d_head/M. For each sub-vector position, a codebook of K centroids is learned (K=256 typical), so each sub-vector is stored as a log₂(K) = 8-bit index. The effective bits per dimension: M × 8 / d_head - for M=8 and d_head=128: 0.5 bits/dim. At attention time, sub-vectors are reconstructed from codebooks for the dot product. Codebooks are shared across all tokens and learned from a representative set of KV activations. This approach is especially useful at context lengths >32K where KV memory dominates inference cost.

---

<a id="zipcache"></a>
### ZipCache · W16A16KV4 (mixed: salient tokens higher) · KV Quant · 2024-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/zipcache.svg" width="640" alt="ZipCache diagram">
<p><em>ZipCache: dynamic attention-score tracking assigns higher bits to salient tokens.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[KV cache tokens] --> B[Accumulate attention\nscores online]
    B --> C{Salient token?}
    C -->|high attention| D[Keep in FP16 / INT8]
    C -->|low attention| E[Quantize to INT4\nor INT2]
    D --> F[Mixed-precision\nKV cache]
    E --> F
    F --> G[3x KV compression\nnear-lossless for salient]
```

> ZipCache quantizes the KV cache with mixed precision, assigning more bits to "salient" tokens - those with high cumulative attention scores that are frequently attended to. The attention score of each token is tracked dynamically during generation, and tokens whose cumulative attention score exceeds a threshold are assigned higher-precision quantization (or kept at FP16). The remaining low-attention tokens are compressed more aggressively. This adaptive allocation outperforms uniform KV quantization at the same average bit-width with no additional calibration.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **NeurIPS 2024** |
| Paper | [2405.14256](https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.14256) |
| Precision | W16A16KV4 (mixed: salient tokens higher) |
| Granularity | per-token, adaptive bit-width |
| Calibration | none |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | salient token identification: tokens with high attention scores kept at higher precision |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | < 0.5 ppl at 4-bit average on LLaMA-2 family; outperforms uniform KV4 |
| Builds on | [kivi](#kivi) · [qaq](#qaq) |
| Related | [kivi](#kivi) · [qaq](#qaq) · [kvquant](#kvquant) · [skvq](#skvq) |

**How it works:**

ZipCache maintains a running sum of attention scores for each KV position across all heads and layers. Positions whose cumulative importance score exceeds a dynamic threshold θ (tuned to achieve a target average bit-width) are quantized at high precision (INT8 or FP16); others are quantized at INT4 or lower. The threshold is updated online as new tokens arrive. The approach is motivated by the "attention sink" phenomenon: early tokens and locally-important tokens receive disproportionate attention and must be preserved at high quality. Unlike KIVI or SKVQ, ZipCache's mixed precision is content-adaptive, not position-based.

---

<a id="skvq"></a>
### SKVQ · W16A16KV2/KV4 · KV Quant · 2024-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/skvq.svg" width="640" alt="SKVQ diagram">
<p><em>Sliding window of recent KV tokens provides fresh scale statistics for online quantization.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[KV cache stream] --> B[Sliding window:\nrecent tokens full precision]
    B --> C[Older tokens:\nchannel reordering]
    C --> D[Similar-range channels\ngrouped together]
    D --> E[Group quantize\nKV2 to KV4]
    E --> F[Clipping migration\nat window boundary]
    F --> G[Efficient long-context\nwith sliding precision]
```

> SKVQ tackles the challenge that KV cache quantization must work online - quantization parameters cannot be computed from future tokens. Naive online quantization suffers from stale statistics: the quantization scale computed at token 1 may be wrong at token 1000 if the KV distribution drifts. SKVQ uses a sliding window of the most recent W tokens to maintain fresh, locally-relevant statistics for the quantization scale, balancing between always-fresh (expensive) and never-updated (stale) approaches.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2405.06484](https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.06484) |
| Precision | W16A16KV2/KV4 |
| Granularity | per-channel (with sliding window for online statistics) |
| Calibration | none (online) |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | clipped quantization; sliding window maintains fresh statistics for accurate scale computation |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | < 0.5 ppl at KV4; competitive with KIVI at KV2 without calibration |
| Builds on | [kivi](#kivi) · [rtn](#rtn) |
| Related | [kivi](#kivi) · [kvquant](#kvquant) · [wkvquant](#wkvquant) |

**How it works:**

For each KV head, maintain a sliding window of the W most recent raw (FP16) KV vectors. Compute the quantization scale from this window: s = max(|K_{t-W:t}|) / 127. Apply this scale to quantize the current K_t. As the window slides forward, the old quantized values are not re-quantized (too expensive), but new tokens use the fresh window scale. The window size W is a hyperparameter balancing freshness (smaller W) against scale stability (larger W). Additionally, SKVQ applies channel-aware clipping based on the long-run statistics of each K head channel to prevent outliers from inflating the window scale.

---

<a id="snapkv"></a>
### SnapKV · KV selective eviction (variable effective bits) · KV Quant · 2024-04

<img src="../assets/diagrams/snapkv.svg" width="640" alt="SnapKV diagram">
<p><em>SnapKV: observation-window attention selects which past KV entries to retain.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Input prompt tokens] --> B[Observation window:\nlast W tokens]
    B --> C[Accumulate attention\nscores over window]
    C --> D{High cumulative\nattention?}
    D -->|yes| E[Retain KV pair\nin cache]
    D -->|no| F[Evict KV pair]
    E --> G[Compressed KV cache\n+ full window KV]
    F --> G
    G --> H[3.6x reduction\nat 16K context]
```

> SnapKV identifies which past KV entries each attention head actually uses by examining attention patterns over a short observation window of recent tokens. Only high-attention tokens are kept; the rest are evicted. This is a tuning-free, plug-and-play approach compatible with FlashAttention, achieving 3.6x KV cache reduction at 16K context with minimal accuracy loss and no model changes required.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **NeurIPS 2024** |
| Paper | [2404.14469](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.14469) |
| Code | [FasterDecoding/SnapKV](https://github.com/FasterDecoding/SnapKV) |
| Precision | KV selective eviction (variable effective bits) |
| Granularity | per-head, observation-window based |
| Calibration | none |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | preserves high-attention tokens; evicts low-attention ones |
| Hardware target | GPU; compatible with FlashAttention |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | < 1% on long-context QA; 3.6x KV cache reduction at 16K context |
| Related | [kvquant](#kvquant) · [kivi](#kivi) · [think](#think) · [gear](#gear) · [zipcache](#zipcache) |

**How it works:**

For each head, SnapKV accumulates attention scores over the last W tokens (the observation window, default 32-64 tokens). Past tokens receiving high cumulative attention score are retained; others are evicted. The surviving KV cache is concatenated with the full observation-window KV cache (always preserved), ensuring recent context is never dropped. Unlike quantization methods, SnapKV reduces the number of stored tokens rather than their bit-width.

---

<a id="gear"></a>
### GEAR · W16A16KV4 (with low-rank residual correction) · KV Quant · 2024-03

<img src="../assets/diagrams/gear.svg" width="640" alt="GEAR diagram">
<p><em>GEAR = quantized KV + low-rank + sparse residual; error decomposition updated incrementally.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[KV cache] --> B[Quantize to INT4]
    B --> C[Quantization\nerror residual E]
    C --> D[SVD low-rank\napprox of E]
    D --> E[Store: INT4 KV\n+ low-rank E_hat]
    E --> F[Dequant + low-rank\ncorrection at runtime]
    F --> G[Near-lossless\nKV at 4-bit avg]
```

> GEAR combines KV-cache quantization with a learned low-rank residual correction to compensate for quantization error. The idea is to decompose the KV cache as: KV ≈ Q(KV) + low_rank_correction, where the low-rank correction captures the systematic (structured) part of the quantization error. The rank-r correction requires only 2 × r × d_head parameters per layer (instead of 2 × T × d_head for the full KV), providing significant compression with minimal accuracy loss.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2403.05527](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.05527) |
| Precision | W16A16KV4 (with low-rank residual correction) |
| Granularity | per-token; rank-r low-rank residual |
| Calibration | none during inference; low-rank approximation computed online |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | low-rank + sparse residual decomposition of quantization error |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | near-lossless at KV4 with low-rank residual; outperforms KIVI at same bit-width |
| Builds on | [kivi](#kivi) · [rtn](#rtn) |
| Related | [kivi](#kivi) · [kvquant](#kvquant) · [palu](#palu) |

**How it works:**

GEAR's decomposition: KV = Q(KV) + L R^T + Sparse, where Q(KV) is the INT4 quantized cache, L ∈ ℝ^{T×r} and R ∈ ℝ^{d×r} are the low-rank residual factors, and Sparse captures outlier entries. The low-rank factors are computed online from the quantization error matrix E = KV − Q(KV) via a fast streaming SVD that is maintained incrementally as new tokens arrive. The sparse component captures the top-k% largest errors after the low-rank correction. At attention, Q(KV) + L R^T is used, with the sparse correction added for outlier positions only.

---

<a id="qaq"></a>
### QAQ · W16A16KV2–KV4 (mixed) · KV Quant · 2024-03

<img src="../assets/diagrams/qaq.svg" width="640" alt="QAQ diagram">
<p><em>Attention-score-based adaptive bit allocation: high-attention tokens get more KV bits.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[KV cache entry] --> B[Compute importance:\naccumulated attn score]
    B --> C{High attention?}
    C -->|yes| D[Assign high bit-width\n8-bit or FP16]
    C -->|no| E[Assign low bit-width\n2-4 bit]
    D --> F[Quality-adaptive\nmixed KV cache]
    E --> F
    F --> G[Dynamic precision\nper generation step]
```

> QAQ adaptively allocates bit-widths across KV cache entries based on their predicted importance to the attention output. Tokens that receive high attention scores (the "attention sink" phenomenon - early tokens and recent tokens tend to receive disproportionate attention) are assigned higher-precision quantization, while tokens with low attention scores can be safely compressed to very low bits (even 1–2 bits). This adaptive allocation achieves better quality per average bit than uniform quantization across all tokens.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2403.04643](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.04643) |
| Precision | W16A16KV2–KV4 (mixed) |
| Granularity | per-token, adaptive bit-width |
| Calibration | none |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | importance-adaptive bit allocation: sink tokens and recent tokens get more bits |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | better than uniform KV4 at same average bit-width |
| Builds on | [kivi](#kivi) · [rtn](#rtn) |
| Related | [kivi](#kivi) · [kvquant](#kvquant) · [think](#think) |

**How it works:**

QAQ uses a proxy for KV importance: the accumulated attention scores that a token has received across all heads and layers so far. Tokens with high accumulated attention (estimated from running statistics during generation) are assigned more bits in the KV cache; tokens with low accumulated attention are compressed further. The allocation is updated dynamically as new tokens arrive: a token's assigned bit-width can increase if later layers find it important. The implementation uses a budget-constrained allocation that targets an average bit-width (e.g. 3.0 bpw) while minimizing peak allocation.

---

<a id="wkvquant"></a>
### WKVQuant · W4A16KV4 · KV Quant · 2024-02

<img src="../assets/diagrams/wkvquant.svg" width="640" alt="WKVQuant diagram">
<p><em>Joint weight + KV quantization optimization minimizes compound error in the generation loop.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Weights W] --> B[W4 past-only quant:\nonly prev-gen KV quantized]
    A2[KV Cache] --> C[Within-group clipping\nfor KV INT4]
    B --> D[Joint W4KV4\ncombined objective]
    C --> D
    D --> E[Better throughput\nthan W4A16 alone]
```

> WKVQuant jointly optimizes weight and KV-cache quantization, observing that weight and KV quantization errors interact during generation: a quantized weight produces quantization-corrupted hidden states, which then get quantized again in the KV cache, compounding errors. WKVQuant uses a calibration-based approach that optimizes both the weight quantization parameters and the KV scale/zero-point together to minimize the compound error. The key insight is that weight quantization errors are not independent of KV errors and should be co-optimized.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2402.12065](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.12065) |
| Precision | W4A16KV4 |
| Granularity | per-group (W); per-channel (K); per-token (V) |
| Calibration | calibration set |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | joint weight + KV quantization optimization; NormalFloat for K |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | < 0.5 ppl at W4A16KV4 on LLaMA-2 |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) · [kivi](#kivi) |
| Related | [kivi](#kivi) · [kvquant](#kvquant) · [think](#think) |

**How it works:**

WKVQuant frames the joint quantization problem as minimizing ‖ f(x; W, K, V) − f(x; Q(W), Q(K), Q(V)) ‖² over all quantization parameters simultaneously. In practice, it uses a block-wise alternating optimization: fix KV quantization, optimize W (as in GPTQ); fix W quantization, optimize KV. The KV quantization uses a NormalFloat-inspired grid for K (which it observes is more Gaussian than V) and per-token asymmetric for V. The joint optimization gives 1–2% quality improvement over optimizing weight and KV independently.

---

<a id="coupled-quant"></a>
### Coupled Quantization · W16A16KV1/KV2 · KV Quant · 2024-02

<img src="../assets/diagrams/coupled-quant.svg" width="640" alt="Coupled Quantization diagram">
<p><em>Jointly optimize K and V quantization parameters to minimize attention output error via error cancellation.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Key K and Value V\nfrom same token] --> B[Analyze correlation\nbetween K and V]
    B --> C[Joint codebook\nshared K-V pairs]
    C --> D[Quantize K and V\ncoupled, not independent]
    D --> E[Inter-vector correlation\nreduces joint error]
    E --> F[KV2 near-lossless\ncoupled representation]
```

> Coupled Quantization observes that the errors in the quantized K and V caches are not independent in their effect on the attention output: the attention score (from Q×K) and the value aggregation (softmax×V) interact in a way that allows the error from K quantization to partially cancel the error from V quantization. By jointly designing the K and V quantization to exploit this cancellation (selecting quantization parameters that minimize the total attention output error rather than minimizing K error and V error separately), Coupled Quantization achieves much better quality at 1-bit KV than either independent 1-bit K or 1-bit V quantization.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2402.11535](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.11535) |
| Precision | W16A16KV1/KV2 |
| Granularity | per-head |
| Calibration | none |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | coupled K-V error cancellation: K and V quantization errors are designed to cancel in the attention output |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | enables 1-bit KV cache with acceptable quality loss; significantly better than independent 1-bit quantization |
| Builds on | [kivi](#kivi) · [rtn](#rtn) |
| Related | [kivi](#kivi) · [kvquant](#kvquant) · [qaq](#qaq) |

**How it works:**

The attention output for position t is: o_t = Σ_s softmax(q_t k_s / √d) × v_s. With quantized k̃_s and ṽ_s, the error is: δo_t = Σ_s ∂softmax/∂(q_t k_s) × q_t δk_s × v_s + Σ_s softmax(q_t k̃_s / √d) × δv_s. These two error terms have different signs and can partially cancel if chosen carefully. Coupled Quantization jointly optimizes (scale_K, zero_K, scale_V, zero_V) per head to minimize ‖ δo ‖ rather than ‖ δK ‖ and ‖ δV ‖ independently. The joint optimization is tractable and runs online.

---

<a id="kivi"></a>
### KIVI · W16A16KV2 · KV Quant · 2024-02

<img src="../assets/diagrams/kivi.svg" width="640" alt="KIVI diagram">
<p><em>K per-group (g=16) asymmetric + V per-token asymmetric + FP16 residual for recent tokens.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Key cache K] --> B[Per-channel\nINT2 quantize]
    A2[Value cache V] --> C[Per-token\nINT2 quantize]
    B --> D[Sliding window:\nrecent tokens FP16]
    C --> D
    D --> E[2-bit KV cache\nno fine-tuning needed]
    E --> F[2x longer context\nsame GPU memory]
```

> KIVI proposes a simple but effective asymmetric quantization strategy for KV cache: keys are quantized per-channel (using groups of 16 consecutive elements in the channel dimension), and values are quantized per-token. This asymmetric choice is motivated by empirical observation: K vectors have consistent per-channel magnitude patterns across all tokens (stable per-channel statistics), while V vectors have consistent per-token patterns. The most recent r=32 tokens are kept in FP16 as a "residual cache" to avoid quantizing newly computed KV entries before they stabilize. Implemented as a FlashAttention plugin with no tuning needed.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICML 2024** |
| Paper | [2402.02750](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.02750) |
| Code | [jy-yuan/KIVI](https://github.com/jy-yuan/KIVI) |
| Precision | W16A16KV2 |
| Granularity | per-channel g=16 (K); per-token (V); residual cache at FP16 |
| Calibration | none |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | recent tokens kept at FP16 (residual); per-channel K grouping reduces outlier impact |
| Hardware target | GPU (custom FlashAttention-2 kernel) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | < 0.3 ppl at KV2 on LLaMA-2-7B (WikiText-2); 2.35× throughput |
| Builds on | [rtn](#rtn) |
| Related | [kvquant](#kvquant) · [gear](#gear) · [wkvquant](#wkvquant) · [think](#think) |

**How it works:**

For the key cache K ∈ ℝ^{T × d_head}: divide d_head into groups of g=16. Per each group of each head, compute an asymmetric per-group scale and zero-point from the actual values across all T tokens in that group. Quantize to INT2. For the value cache V ∈ ℝ^{T × d_head}: compute per-token (per-row) scale and zero-point. Quantize to INT2. At attention time, dequantize K and V before computing attention - the dequantization is fused into the FlashAttention kernel so it adds < 5% overhead. Recent r tokens' KV are kept as FP16 and concatenated before attention computation.

---

<a id="kvquant"></a>
### KVQuant · W16A16KV4 / KV3 · KV Quant · 2024-01

<img src="../assets/diagrams/kvquant.svg" width="640" alt="KVQuant diagram">
<p><em>KVQuant: non-uniform quantile grid for K/V + per-channel K + sparse FP16 residual.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[KV tensors] --> B{Token type}
    B -->|sink + outlier tokens| C[FP16 sparse]
    B -->|normal tokens ~95%| D[Per-channel\nnon-uniform quant]
    D --> E[NF4-style bins\nKV2/KV3/KV4]
    C --> F[Compressed KV cache]
    E --> F
    F --> G[10M-token context\non single GPU]
```

> KVQuant is a comprehensive KV-cache quantization framework that targets extreme context lengths (millions of tokens) where the KV cache is the dominant memory consumer. Three key innovations: non-uniform quantization (NUQ) fits the K/V value distributions (which are not Gaussian) with per-quantile codebooks; per-channel quantization for K and per-token for V to match their respective structure; and a sparse high-precision overlay for outlier KV entries. Together, KVQuant achieves 4-bit KV cache with < 0.1% perplexity increase and enables 10M+ context length inference.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **NeurIPS 2024** |
| Paper | [2401.18079](https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.18079) |
| Code | [SqueezeAILab/KVQuant](https://github.com/SqueezeAILab/KVQuant) |
| Precision | W16A16KV4 / KV3 |
| Granularity | per-channel (K); per-token (V); non-uniform quantization grid |
| Calibration | calibration set for K/V distribution |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | non-uniform quantile-based (NUQ) quantization adapts grid to K/V distribution; per-channel K |
| Hardware target | GPU (FlashAttention-compatible) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | < 0.5 ppl at KV4; enables 10M context on single A100 by reducing KV cache memory |
| Builds on | [rtn](#rtn) |
| Related | [kivi](#kivi) · [gear](#gear) · [think](#think) · [skvq](#skvq) |

**How it works:**

Three technical contributions: (1) Non-uniform KV quantization (NUQ) - calibrate quantization levels at the quantiles of the actual K/V distribution (from calibration data), which fits the often-skewed distributions better than uniform grids. (2) Asymmetric per-channel quantization for K: each head's K channel has a fixed distribution across tokens, so per-channel scales computed from calibration are stable and low-error. Per-token quantization for V. (3) Sparse residual: for the ~1% of KV entries with largest quantization error (the "sink" tokens plus early positions), store the FP16 value and add it to the dequantized KV at attention time.

---

---

## Low-Precision Training & Numerical Formats

Hardware-oriented floating-point formats and training recipes for using them. FP8 and MX (Microscaling) formats are now standard in H100/Blackwell training. This category is adjacent to inference quantization - the formats overlap, but the use case is accelerating training rather than compressing inference.

<a id="scalesweep"></a>
### ScaleSweep · W4A4KV4 (NVFP4 / FP4) · LP Training · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/scalesweep.svg" width="640" alt="ScaleSweep diagram">
<p><em>ScaleSweep: per-block NVFP4 scale chosen by a bounded sweep that minimizes MSE/WMSE, vs. fixed AbsMax initialization.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP32 / BF16 model] --> B[Cast to\nW4A4KV4 NVFP4 / FP4]
    B --> C[Low-precision GEMM\non hardware tensor cores]
    C --> D[Cast back for\naccumulation]
    D --> E[FP32 master weights\noptimizer state]
    E --> F[Higher training\nthroughput]
```

> ScaleSweep is a post-training method for quantizing LLMs into NVFP4, NVIDIA's FP4 format that pairs every block of 16 values with an FP8 scale. Instead of setting each block's scale to the block's maximum absolute value (AbsMax), which is what current NVFP4 pipelines do, ScaleSweep tries a range of candidate scales for each block and keeps the one that minimizes the reconstruction error against the original block. To keep this cheap, the authors derive lower and upper bounds on where the best scale can possibly lie under mean-squared-error and weighted-mean-squared-error objectives, so only a small set of candidates needs to be checked. The result is a near-drop-in replacement for the quantization operator that adds little overhead and works when weights, activations, KV cache, and query states are all pushed to 4-bit.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2606.07618](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07618) |
| Precision | W4A4KV4 (NVFP4 / FP4) |
| Granularity | per-block (NVFP4 blocks of 16 elements with an FP8 block scale) |
| Calibration | calibration data used to set per-block scales; per-block scale selected by sweeping candidates that minimize MSE/WMSE reconstruction error |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | fine-grained per-block FP8 scales whose values are searched (not just AbsMax) to better fit each block's range |
| Hardware target | NVIDIA Blackwell NVFP4 Tensor Cores |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | preserves >93% of full-precision performance under end-to-end NVFP4 quantization of weights, activations, KV cache, and query states on Llama (3.1/3.2) and Qwen3 models (eval suite incl. WikiText-2, MMLU-Pro, GSM8K, HellaSwag, Winogrande, IFEval) |
| Builds on | [nvfp4](#nvfp4) |
| Related | [nvfp4](#nvfp4) · [mx-formats](#mx-formats) · [smoothquant](#smoothquant) · [quarot](#quarot) · [spinquant](#spinquant) |

**How it works:**

For each NVFP4 block, the per-block FP8 scale determines how the 16 values are mapped onto the E2M1 grid; AbsMax fixes this scale at the block max, which is generally not the error- minimizing choice. ScaleSweep instead treats the scale as a free parameter and sweeps over feasible candidates, selecting the one that minimizes MSE or WMSE between the original block and its quantized-then-dequantized reconstruction. A theoretical analysis of NVFP4 quantization yields provable lower and upper bounds on the optimal scale, which shrink the candidate set enough that the sweep cost is negligible relative to the baseline quantization operator while still containing the optimum. The scheme is applied end-to-end across weights, activations, KV cache, and query states.

---

<a id="mixfp4"></a>
### MixFP4 · W4A8 · LP Training · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/mixfp4.svg" width="640" alt="MixFP4 diagram">
<p><em>MixFP4: per-16-block choice of E2M1 (FP4) vs E1M2 (INT4-like) micro-format, selector packed into the FP8 E4M3 scale sign bit; both decode to a shared E2M2 compute format on the NVFP4 Tensor Core path.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP32 / BF16 model] --> B[Cast to\nW4A8]
    B --> C[Low-precision GEMM\non hardware tensor cores]
    C --> D[Cast back for\naccumulation]
    D --> E[FP32 master weights\noptimizer state]
    E --> F[Higher training\nthroughput]
```

> MixFP4 extends NVFP4 4-bit inference by letting each 16-element block pick the 4-bit number format that fits its values best. Instead of forcing every block to use NVFP4's E2M1 floating-point layout, a block can instead use an E1M2 layout whose representable values are evenly spaced like INT4, which suits blocks whose statistics are more uniform. The choice for each block is made by comparing quantization error (MSE) between the two layouts during quantization, and the single bit recording that choice is tucked into the unused sign bit of the FP8 block scale, so no extra storage is needed. At inference both layouts are decoded into one shared internal representation, so the same matrix-multiply hardware path works for either, and the only cost is a small amount of extra tensor-core logic.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.31035](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.31035) |
| Precision | W4A8 |
| Granularity | block of 16 elements (g=16) along the reduction dimension, each block shares one FP8 E4M3 scale |
| Calibration | PTQ uses WikiText training split (512-1024 samples) for calibration-based baselines; per-block format choice decided by MSE at quantization time |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | per-block choice between E2M1 (wider dynamic range) and E1M2 (uniform spacing) plus FP8 E4M3 block scale capturing block dynamic range |
| Hardware target | NVIDIA Blackwell Tensor Cores (NVFP4-compatible MMA/GEMM path); overhead synthesized at TSMC 28nm 1GHz (3.1% area, 1.5% power) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | WikiText perplexity: Llama-3.1-8B 8.06 (vs NVFP4 8.26), Qwen3-8B 12.39 (vs 12.74), Llama-3.2-1B 13.48 (vs 13.90) |
| Builds on | [nvfp4](#nvfp4) · [mx-formats](#mx-formats) |
| Related | [nvfp4](#nvfp4) · [mx-formats](#mx-formats) · [fp8-training](#fp8-training) |

**How it works:**

Each block stores its 4-bit payloads in one of two micro-formats: E2M1 (the standard NVFP4 layout, with non-uniform floating-point spacing for wider dynamic range) or E1M2 (which yields uniform, INT4-like level spacing, mapping to a symmetric range under 2x scaling). The per-block format is selected by whichever minimizes MSE for that block. The 1-bit format selector is packed into the sign bit of the FP8 E4M3 first-level block scale, adding zero metadata overhead while reusing NVFP4's existing scale hierarchy. Both micro-formats are decoded into a unified internal E2M2 compute representation so the standard block-scaled MMA/GEMM execution path is unchanged and the datapath is not duplicated.

<a id="mxfp4-rl-error-decomposition"></a>
### MXFP4-RL Error Decomposition · MXFP4 (W4A4 microscaling, E2M1 + E8M0 block scale) · LP Training · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/mxfp4-rl-error-decomposition.svg" width="640" alt="MXFP4-RL Error Decomposition diagram">
<p><em>MXFP4 RL error split into scale bias (gradients), deadzone (rollouts), and grid noise (entropy), each with a targeted correction; a residual irreducible floor remains.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP32 / BF16 model] --> B[Cast to\nMXFP4 W4A4 microscaling, E2M1 + E8M0 block scale]
    B --> C[Low-precision GEMM\non hardware tensor cores]
    C --> D[Cast back for\naccumulation]
    D --> E[FP32 master weights\noptimizer state]
    E --> F[Higher training\nthroughput]
```

> This work studies why MXFP4 4-bit quantization hurts reinforcement-learning post-training of LLMs and splits the quantization error into three separate causes. The first is a scale bias from rounding each block's shared scale to a power of two, which compounds through the backward pass and corrupts gradients. The second is a deadzone effect where small values get zeroed out, which lowers the quality of generated rollouts. The third is grid noise from snapping values to the nearest 4-bit level, which inflates the policy's entropy. For each cause the authors apply a matching fix (a finer macro-block scale, a fallback path that keeps a few important entries at higher precision, and an adaptive injected-noise term), and report that the combined recipe brings a quantized RL run back to BF16-level results on Qwen2.5-3B and slightly past it on a 30B MoE model.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.20402](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20402) |
| Precision | MXFP4 (W4A4 microscaling, E2M1 + E8M0 block scale) |
| Granularity | microscaling block (MXFP4 block of 32 elements share one E8M0 power-of-two scale) |
| Calibration | none (correction terms computed online during RL post-training; no offline calibration set) |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | outlier fallback that keeps deadzone/large entries at higher precision, plus macro-block scaling to reduce power-of-two scale bias |
| Hardware target | GPUs with MXFP4 microscaling support (e.g. Blackwell-class FP4 Tensor Cores) |
| Training needed? | yes |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | recovers BF16 accuracy to within 0.7% on Qwen2.5-3B (dense) and exceeds BF16 by +1.0% on Qwen3-30B-A3B-Base (MoE) for RL post-training |
| Builds on | [mx-formats](#mx-formats) · [nvfp4](#nvfp4) |
| Related | [mx-formats](#mx-formats) · [nvfp4](#nvfp4) · [fp8-training](#fp8-training) · [ramp](#ramp) |

**How it works:**

The paper decomposes MXFP4 error during RL training into three components. (1) Scale bias from E8M0 power-of-two rounding of the per-block scale accumulates multiplicatively through backprop and degrades gradient accuracy; it is mitigated by macro-block scaling that uses a finer/higher-precision scaling factor across blocks. (2) Deadzone truncation zeros out small magnitudes and lowers rollout quality; it is recovered by an outlier-fallback path that retains the affected (and large) entries at higher precision, which also partially offsets the scale bias. (3) Grid noise from nearest-4-bit rounding raises policy entropy; it is controlled by adaptive quantization noise (AQN) that shapes the injected rounding noise. A residual 'irreducible floor' remains after all three corrections.
