<a id="mxfp4-pretraining"></a>
### MXFP4 Pretraining (Deterministic Hadamard) · MXFP4 (E2M1 elements, E8M0 block scale) for Fprop/Dgrad/Wgrad; FP32 master weights · LP Training · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/mxfp4-pretraining.svg" width="640" alt="MXFP4 Pretraining (Deterministic Hadamard) diagram">
<p><em>MXFP4 pretraining: FP4 enabled across Fprop/Dgrad/Wgrad; Wgrad drives instability, fixed by a deterministic Hadamard rotation before quantization.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP32 / BF16 model] --> B[Cast to\nMXFP4 E2M1 elements, E8M0 block scale for Fprop/Dgrad/Wgrad; FP32 master weights]
    B --> C[Low-precision GEMM\non hardware tensor cores]
    C --> D[Cast back for\naccumulation]
    D --> E[FP32 master weights\noptimizer state]
    E --> F[Higher training\nthroughput]
```

> This is a controlled study of what makes 4-bit floating-point (MXFP4) pretraining of large language models go unstable. The authors turn on FP4 one piece at a time across the three matrix multiplies in training (forward pass, activation gradients, and weight gradients) while holding everything else fixed, and find that quantizing the weight gradient is the part that actually breaks convergence; the other two only slightly increase the number of tokens needed to reach a given loss. They then test ways to fix the weight-gradient problem and find that stochastic rounding and randomized Hadamard rotations do not help once the weight gradient is in FP4, but applying a fixed (deterministic) Hadamard rotation before quantization brings training back to stable convergence. Their conclusion is that the instability comes from structured errors introduced by block-level micro-scaling along sensitive gradient paths, not from a lack of randomness. All experiments use native MXFP4 on AMD Instinct MI355X GPUs, avoiding software emulation.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.09825](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09825) |
| Precision | MXFP4 (E2M1 elements, E8M0 block scale) for Fprop/Dgrad/Wgrad; FP32 master weights |
| Granularity | block of 32 elements share one 8-bit E8M0 exponent scale (OCP MX) |
| Calibration | no calibration - block scale computed from block max during training |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | deterministic Hadamard rotations applied before quantization to redistribute structured micro-scaling errors along sensitive gradient paths |
| Hardware target | AMD Instinct MI355X (native MXFP4 Tensor Core support) |
| Training needed? | yes |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | Llama 3.1-8B pretrained on C4: full MXFP4 (incl. Wgrad) diverges with stochastic rounding or randomized Hadamard; deterministic Hadamard rotations restore stable convergence; FP4 in Fprop+Dgrad alone adds only modest extra token requirement vs BF16 |
| Builds on | [mx-formats](#mx-formats) · [fp8-training](#fp8-training) |
| Related | [mx-formats](#mx-formats) · [nvfp4](#nvfp4) · [fp8-training](#fp8-training) · [deepseek-fp8](#deepseek-fp8) · [quarot](#quarot) · [spinquant](#spinquant) |

**How it works:**

Train a transformer with MXFP4 (OCP microscaling FP4: E2M1 elements in 32-element blocks sharing an E8M0 scale) progressively enabled in Fprop, Dgrad, and Wgrad to isolate the source of divergence. The weight-gradient (Wgrad) GEMM is identified as the dominant driver of convergence degradation. Because the instability stems from structured micro-scaling quantization error rather than insufficient stochasticity, randomized interventions (stochastic rounding, randomized Hadamard) fail, while a deterministic Hadamard rotation applied before quantizing the gradient consistently decorrelates and spreads the error to restore stable optimization. Native MI355X FP4 hardware is used so the analysis reflects real hardware behavior rather than emulation.

<a id="agoq"></a>
### AGoQ · W16A4 (gradients 8-bit; weights BF16, activations ~4-bit, gradients INT8) · LP Training · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/agoq.svg" width="640" alt="AGoQ diagram">
<p><em>AGoQ: layer-aware ~4-bit activation storage plus 8-bit gradient storage and 8-bit All-Reduce for memory-efficient distributed LLM training.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP32 / BF16 model] --> B[Cast to\nW16A4 gradients 8-bit; weights BF16, activations approx 4-bit, gradients INT8]
    B --> C[Low-precision GEMM\non hardware tensor cores]
    C --> D[Cast back for\naccumulation]
    D --> E[FP32 master weights\noptimizer state]
    E --> F[Higher training\nthroughput]
```

> AGoQ is a training-time recipe that shrinks the GPU memory footprint of distributed LLM pretraining by storing the saved activations and the gradients in low precision rather than full BF16. Instead of using one fixed bit-width everywhere, it inspects each transformer block and pipeline stage and hands out activation bits according to how much each layer can tolerate, pushing average activation storage down toward 4 bits. Gradients are kept in 8 bits and exchanged with an 8-bit All-Reduce that preserves precision, cutting both memory and inter-GPU communication time. Across LLaMA models from 8B to 32B parameters on clusters up to 64 GPUs, it reports up to 52% memory savings and up to 1.34x faster training versus Megatron-LM, COAT, and DeepSpeed with little convergence loss.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.00539](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.00539) |
| Precision | W16A4 (gradients 8-bit; weights BF16, activations ~4-bit, gradients INT8) |
| Granularity | per-layer bit-width allocation across transformer blocks and pipeline stages |
| Calibration | no calibration - bit-widths assigned by layer type/sensitivity, scales computed dynamically during training |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | layer-aware bit allocation: sensitive layers/pipeline stages keep more activation bits while tolerant ones drop toward 4-bit |
| Hardware target | NVIDIA GPU clusters (tested up to 64 GPUs), distributed training with pipeline/data parallelism |
| Training needed? | yes |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | negligible convergence/loss degradation pretraining LLaMA models (8B-32B); comparable downstream task accuracy vs BF16 |
| Builds on | [fp8-training](#fp8-training) |
| Related | [fp8-training](#fp8-training) · [mx-formats](#mx-formats) · [deepseek-fp8](#deepseek-fp8) |

**How it works:**

AGoQ separates two memory consumers in distributed LLM training and quantizes each. (1) Activations saved for the backward pass are compressed with a layer-aware allocation algorithm that assigns different bit-widths to different transformer blocks based on layer type and pipeline-stage sensitivity, yielding an average near 4-bit activation store while keeping accuracy. (2) Gradients are quantized to 8 bits for both storage and communication, using a precision-preserving 8-bit All-Reduce so that the reduced gradients remain accurate enough for stable convergence. Weights and the optimizer master copy remain in BF16; the quantization touches only the stored activations and exchanged gradients, so memory and communication shrink without changing the model math materially.

<a id="deepseek-fp8"></a>
### DeepSeek FP8 Training · FP8 (E4M3 forward / E5M2 gradient) with fine-grained block scaling · LP Training · 2024-12

<img src="../assets/diagrams/deepseek-fp8.svg" width="640" alt="DeepSeek FP8 Training diagram">
<p><em>Fine-grained FP8: 1×128 weight tiles + 128×128 activation tiles + FP32 accumulation.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[DeepSeek-V3 training\nBF16 baseline] --> B[Fine-grained FP8:\n1x128 weight tiles]
    B --> C[128x128 activation\ntiles E4M3]
    C --> D[E5M2 gradient tiles]
    D --> E[FP32 accumulators\nprevents drift]
    E --> F[FP8 GEMM on H800\ntensor cores]
    F --> G[671B MoE trained\nfull FP8 pipeline]
```

> DeepSeek-V3 (671B MoE model) was trained with FP8 mixed-precision throughout, making it one of the largest models trained with FP8. The key engineering contribution is a fine-grained block scaling scheme where weight scales are per 1×128 tile and activation scales are per 128×128 tile, with higher-precision accumulation in FP32 and careful clipping to prevent overflow. The training recipe demonstrates that FP8 is stable for very large MoE models with proper scale management, and achieves comparable quality to BF16 training at lower cost.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2412.19437](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.19437) |
| Code | [deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V3](https://github.com/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V3) |
| Precision | FP8 (E4M3 forward / E5M2 gradient) with fine-grained block scaling |
| Granularity | tile-level (1×128 rows, 128×128 columns) scale factors |
| Calibration | dynamic during training |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | fine-grained tile-level scaling with high-precision accumulation and clipping |
| Hardware target | NVIDIA H800 (FP8 Tensor Cores) |
| Training needed? | yes |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | stable FP8 training for 671B MoE model with no performance degradation |
| Builds on | [fp8-training](#fp8-training) |
| Related | [fp8-training](#fp8-training) · [mx-formats](#mx-formats) · [moqe](#moqe) |

**How it works:**

DeepSeek's FP8 recipe uses "fine-grained quantization" - smaller tiles than the standard per-tensor approach. Weights: each 1×128 row segment has its own FP8 scale. Activations: each 128×128 block has its own FP8 scale. This finer granularity is critical for MoE models where different experts have very different activation magnitudes. Accumulation is done in FP32 before rescaling back to BF16/FP8. An "activation anomaly" issue (some activation channels becoming very large) is handled by clipping with a smooth clipping function rather than hard clipping, preventing scale explosion.

<a id="nvfp4"></a>
### NVFP4 · W4 A8/A16 (NVFP4 weights + FP8/FP16 activations) · LP Training · 2024-03

<img src="../assets/diagrams/nvfp4.svg" width="640" alt="NVFP4 diagram">
<p><em>NVFP4: E2M1 per-element + FP8 per-16-block scale; native Blackwell W4A8 Tensor Core support.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 weight tensor] --> B[Tile into 16-element\nblocks]
    B --> C[Shared E8 scale\nper block]
    C --> D[Each element:\nE2M1 FP4 value]
    D --> E[NVFP4 tensor\nBlackwell GB200 native]
    E --> F[approx 2x memory vs FP8\nhigher throughput density]
```

> NVFP4 is NVIDIA's 4-bit floating-point format introduced for Blackwell-architecture GPUs. It uses E2M1 encoding (2 exponent bits, 1 mantissa, 1 sign bit = 4 bits total) and pairs each block of 16 consecutive weights with an FP8 block scale. This differs from MXFP4 (blocks of 32, E8M0 scale) in having smaller blocks and a higher-precision (FP8) scale. NVFP4 with FP8 activation achieves very high throughput on Blackwell's native W4A8 Tensor Cores and is the primary quantization format for Blackwell inference.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2509.25149](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25149) |
| Code | [NVIDIA/TensorRT-LLM](https://github.com/NVIDIA/TensorRT-LLM) |
| Blog / post | [link](https://developer.nvidia.com/blog/nvidia-blackwell-gpu-architecture/) |
| Precision | W4 A8/A16 (NVFP4 weights + FP8/FP16 activations) |
| Granularity | block of 16 elements share one FP8 scale |
| Calibration | calibration data for static scale computation |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | block-level FP8 scale captures block dynamic range |
| Hardware target | NVIDIA Blackwell (B100/B200/GB200/RTX 50xx) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | < 1 ppl at W4A8 vs FP16 on LLaMA-3 family |
| Builds on | [mx-formats](#mx-formats) · [fp8-training](#fp8-training) |
| Related | [mx-formats](#mx-formats) · [fp8-training](#fp8-training) · [marlin](#marlin) |

**How it works:**

NVFP4 stores weights in 4 bits (E2M1 format: values from ±{0.5, 1.0, 1.5, 2.0, 3.0, 4.0, 6.0}). Every 16 consecutive weights share one FP8 block scale. At inference, weights are dequantized per block (multiply by FP8 scale) and the matmul proceeds with FP8/FP16 activations. The combination of native Blackwell NVFP4 Tensor Cores and the compact 4-bit format yields roughly 2× the effective throughput of FP8 inference at comparable quality, enabling LLaMA-3 70B and similar models to run at very high batch throughput.

<a id="mx-formats"></a>
### MX Formats (MXFP8/MXFP6/MXFP4) · MXFP8 / MXFP6 / MXFP4 / MXINT8 · LP Training · 2023-10

<img src="../assets/diagrams/mx-formats.svg" width="640" alt="MX Formats (MXFP8/MXFP6/MXFP4) diagram">
<p><em>MX block-float: 32 elements share one 8-bit exponent; elements store mantissa bits.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Tensor block\n32 elements] --> B[Shared scale\nexponent E8]
    B --> C{MX format}
    C -->|MXFP8| D[E4M3 or E5M2\nper element]
    C -->|MXFP4| E[E2M1\nper element]
    C -->|MXINT8| F[INT8\nper element]
    D --> G[OCP standard\nBlackwell / MI300X]
    E --> G
    F --> G
```

> Microscaling (MX) is an industry standard for block floating-point formats, standardized by the Open Compute Project (OCP) with participation from AMD, ARM, Intel, Meta, Microsoft, NVIDIA, Qualcomm, and Samsung. Each MX format defines blocks of 32 elements that share a single 8-bit exponent scale (E8M0, i.e. just an 8-bit integer exponent with no mantissa). Within a block, each element has its own mantissa bits: MXFP8 (3 mantissa), MXFP6 (2), MXFP4 (0), MXINT8 (7-bit signed int). This block-scale approach provides more dynamic range than per-tensor quantization with lower overhead than per-element scales.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **NeurIPS 2023** |
| Paper | [2310.10537](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.10537) |
| Precision | MXFP8 / MXFP6 / MXFP4 / MXINT8 |
| Granularity | block of 32 elements share one 8-bit E8M0 exponent scale |
| Calibration | no calibration - block scale computed from block max |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | block-level exponent covers the block's dynamic range; outliers within a block are captured by the block scale |
| Hardware target | NVIDIA Blackwell (B100/B200/GB200), AMD MI300X, Intel Gaudi, ARM |
| Training needed? | yes |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | MXFP8: comparable to FP8; MXFP4: slight quality reduction vs MXFP8 |
| Builds on | [fp8-training](#fp8-training) |
| Related | [fp8-training](#fp8-training) · [nvfp4](#nvfp4) · [deepseek-fp8](#deepseek-fp8) |

**How it works:**

An MX tensor is divided into contiguous blocks of 32 elements. For each block: (1) find the maximum absolute value, compute the E8M0 scale = floor(log₂(max)) as an 8-bit integer exponent; (2) scale each element by 2^{-scale} and round to the element format (e.g. FP4 E2M1 for MXFP4). Storage: 32 × b bits + 8 bits per block (where b is element bit-width). Compared to per-tensor FP8: block scaling captures local dynamic range variation (e.g. outlier channels) without per-element overhead. Compared to INT8: floating-point mantissa bits provide non-linear grid spacing. Supported natively in Blackwell GPU Tensor Cores.

---

<a id="fp8-training"></a>
### FP8 Training (Transformer Engine) · FP8 (E4M3 forward / E5M2 gradient) · LP Training · 2022-09

<img src="../assets/diagrams/fp8-training.svg" width="640" alt="FP8 Training (Transformer Engine) diagram">
<p><em>FP8 training: E4M3 for forward pass, E5M2 for gradients, with delayed per-tensor scaling.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[BF16 training] --> B[Cast forward pass\nto E4M3 FP8]
    B --> C[FP8 GEMM\nH100 tensor cores]
    C --> D[Cast gradients\nto E5M2 FP8]
    D --> E[FP32 master weights\n+ optimizer state]
    E --> F[Loss scaling\nprevents underflow]
    F --> G[approx 2x throughput\nvs BF16]
```

> FP8 is a family of 8-bit floating-point formats designed to accelerate deep learning on NVIDIA H100 and later GPUs. Two variants are used in training: E4M3 (4 exponent bits, 3 mantissa, range up to 448) for activations and weights in the forward pass, and E5M2 (5 exponent, 2 mantissa, range up to 57344) for gradients in the backward pass. The Transformer Engine library automates the casting and scale management, using "delayed scaling" (scale from previous step's tensor max) to avoid a synchronization barrier. FP8 training achieves ~2× throughput vs BF16 at equivalent quality on GPT-3-scale models.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2209.05433](https://arxiv.org/abs/2209.05433) |
| Code | [NVIDIA/TransformerEngine](https://github.com/NVIDIA/TransformerEngine) |
| Precision | FP8 (E4M3 forward / E5M2 gradient) |
| Granularity | per-tensor with delayed scaling |
| Calibration | no calibration - scales computed dynamically during training |
| Symmetric? | symmetric |
| Outlier handling | delayed scaling: scale is computed from the max of the previous iteration's tensor |
| Hardware target | NVIDIA H100 / H800 / H20 (FP8 Tensor Cores) |
| Training needed? | yes |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | < 0.5% training loss degradation vs BF16 on most transformer models |
| Related | [mx-formats](#mx-formats) · [deepseek-fp8](#deepseek-fp8) · [nvfp4](#nvfp4) |

**How it works:**

FP8 training uses mixed-precision: weights, activations, and gradient computation are cast to FP8 E4M3/E5M2 for tensor core operations, while weight updates and accumulation happen in FP32 or BF16. A per-tensor scale s is maintained alongside each FP8 tensor; values are stored as FP8 × s. "Delayed scaling" reuses the previous iteration's scale (updated each step), avoiding an expensive all-reduce to find the current max. The Transformer Engine (NVIDIA's implementation) wraps PyTorch modules to automatically handle casting, scale management, and the custom FP8 GEMM kernels.

---

---

## MoE-Specific Quantization

Mixture-of-Experts models (Mixtral, DeepSeek-MoE, etc.) pose unique quantization challenges: the total parameter count is large (many experts), but only a few experts activate per token, so expert weights are rarely observed in calibration. Methods in this category exploit MoE structure for better compression.

<a id="alphaq"></a>
### AlphaQ · W3.5A16 (per-expert mixed, ~3.5 avg weight bits) · MoE Quant · 2026-06

<img src="../assets/diagrams/alphaq.svg" width="640" alt="AlphaQ diagram">
<p><em>AlphaQ: per-expert power-law alpha (Hill estimator) on the weight spectrum drives a budget-constrained mixed-precision bit allocation, then GPTQ per expert.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[MoE model\nmany experts] --> B[Analyze per-expert\nactivation frequency]
    B --> C[Assign bit-width\nby importance]
    C --> D[High-freq experts:\nhigher precision]
    D --> E[Low-freq experts:\nlower precision]
    E --> F[Mixed-precision\nMoE inference]
```

> AlphaQ decides how many bits each expert in a Mixture-of-Experts model should get, without looking at any calibration data to make that decision. For every expert weight matrix it computes a single number from the matrix's eigenvalue spectrum that captures how heavy- tailed that spectrum is, drawing on Heavy-Tailed Self-Regularization theory: experts whose spectra are more heavy-tailed are treated as better-trained and more important, so they keep more bits, while experts with lighter tails are pushed to lower precision. These per-expert scores feed a budget-constrained optimization that picks the bit assignment minimizing total quantization error under a target average bit-width. Each expert is then quantized with group-wise asymmetric GPTQ. The result is roughly 3.5 average bits per expert weight with accuracy close to the full-precision model and over 4x memory reduction.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2606.04980](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04980) |
| Code | [Superone77/AlphaQ](https://github.com/Superone77/AlphaQ) |
| Blog / post | [link](https://www.liquid.ai/research/alphaq-calibration-free-bit-allocation-for-mixture-of-experts-quantization) |
| Precision | W3.5A16 (per-expert mixed, ~3.5 avg weight bits) |
| Granularity | per-expert bit-width; group-wise weight quantization (group size 128) |
| Calibration | calibration-free bit allocation (HT-SR spectral statistic from weights only); the base per-expert GPTQ quantizer still uses 128 WikiText-2 samples |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | experts with heavier-tailed weight spectra (lower power-law alpha) get higher bit-widths; GPTQ error compensation within each expert |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | near-BF16 accuracy at ~3.5 avg bits: Qwen1.5-MoE WikiText-2 PPL 7.38 / 68.04% avg acc; DeepSeek-V2-Lite PPL 6.44 / 70.07%; Mixtral-8x7B PPL 4.23 / 74.29% |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) · [moqe](#moqe) |
| Related | [moqe](#moqe) · [mc-moe](#mc-moe) · [gptq](#gptq) · [squeezellm](#squeezellm) |

**How it works:**

Each expert's weight matrix has its empirical spectral density tail fit to a power law p(lambda) proportional to lambda^(-alpha), with alpha estimated via the Hill estimator (PL_Alpha_Hill). Smaller alpha means a heavier tail, which HT-SR theory associates with a better-trained, higher-quality layer that should receive more bits; larger alpha (lighter tail) experts can be quantized more aggressively. Given a global average-bit budget, AlphaQ solves a budget-constrained allocation that assigns discrete bit-widths to experts to minimize total expected quantization error, and then applies group-wise asymmetric GPTQ at the chosen bit-width per expert. The allocation itself needs no forward passes or calibration data, only the weights, which lets it work when training data is unavailable.

---

<a id="gemq"></a>
### GEMQ · W1.5/W2/W2.5/W3 A16 (per-expert mixed, average bits-per-expert budget) · MoE Quant · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/gemq.svg" width="640" alt="GEMQ diagram">
<p><em>GEMQ: a global linear program allocates per-expert bit-widths from model-wide quantization-error importance, followed by router fine-tuning, iterated progressively down to ~1.5 bits/expert.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[MoE model\nmany experts] --> B[Analyze per-expert\nactivation frequency]
    B --> C[Assign bit-width\nby importance]
    C --> D[High-freq experts:\nhigher precision]
    D --> E[Low-freq experts:\nlower precision]
    E --> F[Mixed-precision\nMoE inference]
```

> GEMQ compresses Mixture-of-Experts LLMs by giving each expert a different bit-width, since experts are not equally important and dominate the model's memory. Instead of deciding bit- widths layer by layer, it scores every expert across the whole model using a quantization- error estimate and then solves a single linear program that picks the bit assignment for all experts at once under a target average-bits-per-expert budget. Because quantizing experts shifts which experts the router picks, it also lightly fine-tunes the routers so they route well to the now-quantized experts. The whole thing runs as a progressive loop that re- estimates importance and re-allocates bits as it pushes down to very low budgets like 1.5 bits per expert, and ships with Triton kernels so the low-bit experts actually run faster and use less memory.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICML 2026** |
| Paper | [2605.23078](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23078) |
| Code | [jndeng/GEMQ](https://github.com/jndeng/GEMQ) |
| Precision | W1.5/W2/W2.5/W3 A16 (per-expert mixed, average bits-per-expert budget) |
| Granularity | per-expert mixed bit-widths; group-wise (group size 128) within each expert |
| Calibration | 128 WikiText-2 sequences of length 2048 (optionally augmented with MATH/C4 for math tasks) |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | globally important experts receive higher bit-widths via the linear-programming allocation |
| Hardware target | GPU (NVIDIA H100); custom Triton kernels for real low-bit MoE inference |
| Training needed? | yes |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | Mixtral-8x7B at 2.5 bits/expert: WikiText-2 5.03 ppl (FP 3.84), 65.13% avg 7-task 0-shot (FP 70.97, -8.2%); DeepSeekV2-Lite at 2.5 bits/expert: WikiText-2 6.65 ppl (FP 6.31) |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) · [moqe](#moqe) |
| Related | [moqe](#moqe) · [mc-moe](#mc-moe) · [gptq](#gptq) · [awq](#awq) · [spqr](#spqr) |

**How it works:**

GEMQ casts expert bit-width selection as one global integer/linear program over all experts in the model: each expert's contribution to output error at each candidate bit-width is estimated (using a second-order/quantization-error proxy rather than per-layer activation- frequency heuristics), and the LP minimizes total error subject to a global average-bits- per-expert budget, capturing model-wide rather than layer-local importance. After allocation, each expert is quantized with group-wise asymmetric GPTQ (group size 128). To counter the routing distribution shift introduced by quantization, the gating routers are fine-tuned with AdamW (lr 1e-4, cross-entropy loss, one epoch) on the calibration set while all other weights stay fixed. These steps are wrapped in a progressive framework that steps the budget down (e.g. 2.5 to 2.0 to 1.5 bpe), re-estimating importance and re-solving the LP at each stage.

---

<a id="tileq"></a>
### TileQ · W2/W3 A16 · MoE Quant · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/tileq.svg" width="640" alt="TileQ diagram">
<p><em>TileQ: low-bit MoE experts plus 2D-tiled low-rank factors shared across input/output dims, with fused single-pass low-rank correction at inference.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[MoE model\nmany experts] --> B[Analyze per-expert\nactivation frequency]
    B --> C[Assign bit-width\nby importance]
    C --> D[High-freq experts:\nhigher precision]
    D --> E[Low-freq experts:\nlower precision]
    E --> F[Mixed-precision\nMoE inference]
```

> TileQ compresses Mixture-of-Experts models by combining low-bit weight quantization (2-bit and 3-bit) with a low-rank correction term that recovers the lost accuracy. The key wrinkle is that the low-rank factors are laid out in 2D tiles so the same factors are reused across both the input and output dimensions of the expert weight matrices, which keeps the extra low-rank memory small instead of growing with every expert. Because each expert otherwise needs its own low-rank pass, TileQ also fuses the low-rank computations of the activated experts into one batched operation so the correction adds little latency at inference. It is a post-training method that needs only a calibration set and no fine-tuning, and the low- rank factors are stored in FP8 with FP16 singular values to keep their footprint down.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.09281](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09281) |
| Code | [r/TileQ-BE20](https://anonymous.4open.science/r/TileQ-BE20) |
| Precision | W2/W3 A16 |
| Granularity | per-tile (2D tiling) low-rank factors + per-group residual quantization |
| Calibration | calibration set |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | LoPRo low-rank rotation (learned orthogonal transforms) before quantization |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | WikiText-2 PPL 4.10 at 3-bit and 4.78 at 2-bit on Mixtral-8x7B (vs 3.87 FP16) |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) · [aqlm](#aqlm) |
| Related | [moqe](#moqe) · [mc-moe](#mc-moe) · [gptq](#gptq) · [spqr](#spqr) |

**How it works:**

Each quantized expert weight matrix is paired with a low-rank residual that compensates for quantization error, but instead of storing an independent rank-r factor per expert, TileQ arranges the low-rank factors in a 2D-tiled structure so factors are shared across the input and output dimensions of the experts, cutting the added low-rank memory by up to ~10x. Before quantization it applies LoPRo's learned low-rank rotations to suppress outliers, and the residual weights are quantized with GPTQ/GPTVQ-style per-group quantization. At inference the multiple per-expert low-rank corrections of the activated experts are fused into a single-pass batched operation, so the extra latency over the plain quantized forward pass is held to roughly 5% rather than the 50%+ overhead of element-wise low-rank methods.

---

<a id="mobie"></a>
### MoBiE · W1A16 · MoE Quant · 2026-04

<img src="../assets/diagrams/mobie.svg" width="640" alt="MoBiE diagram">
<p><em>MoBiE: cross-expert joint SVD strips shared redundancy, gradient-aware Hessian saliency guides 1-bit binarization, and null-space error constraint suppresses router shift in MoE LLMs.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[MoE model\nmany experts] --> B[Analyze per-expert\nactivation frequency]
    B --> C[Assign bit-width\nby importance]
    C --> D[High-freq experts:\nhigher precision]
    D --> E[Low-freq experts:\nlower precision]
    E --> F[Mixed-precision\nMoE inference]
```

> MoBiE is a post-training method for squeezing Mixture-of-Experts language models down to 1-bit weights. Plain binary methods built for dense models stumble on MoE models for three reasons: experts overlap a lot so binarizing each one separately wastes bits, the usual per- layer importance score ignores how a weight affects the overall loss, and crushing weights to one bit shifts which expert the router picks. MoBiE tackles each: it factorizes weights jointly across experts with SVD to strip shared redundancy before binarizing, blends global loss gradients into the local Hessian so salient weights are scored more accurately, and adds an error constraint tied to the input null space so the binarized weights perturb the router as little as possible. It does this without extra storage cost and reports large quality recovery over earlier binary baselines on Qwen3-30B-A3B.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2604.06798v3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.06798v3) |
| Precision | W1A16 |
| Granularity | per-expert (binary weights), with joint SVD across experts |
| Calibration | calibration set used for Hessian/gradient-based importance and null-space estimation |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | salient-weight importance estimation (global loss gradients combined with local Hessian) keeps important weights better represented during binarization |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | On Qwen3-30B-A3B, reduces perplexity by 52.2% and improves average zero-shot accuracy by 43.4% versus prior binary methods, with >2x inference speedup (relative gains vs. SOTA binary baselines, not vs. FP16) |
| Builds on | [billm](#billm) · [gptq](#gptq) · [moqe](#moqe) |
| Related | [billm](#billm) · [pb-llm](#pb-llm) · [onebit](#onebit) · [moqe](#moqe) · [mc-moe](#mc-moe) · [gptq](#gptq) |

**How it works:**

Three components address MoE-specific binarization failures. (1) Joint SVD decomposition is applied across experts to remove cross-expert redundancy, so the shared low-rank structure is captured cheaply and only residual, expert-unique weights are binarized. (2) Importance estimation integrates global loss gradients into the local Hessian metric, giving a saliency score that reflects each weight's effect on the end task rather than just local reconstruction error, which guides which weights are preserved during binarization. (3) An error constraint guided by the input null space steers binarization error into directions that do not change router decisions, suppressing quantization-induced routing shifts. The design adds no extra storage overhead.

---

<a id="expert-router-norm-mpq"></a>
### Router-Norm MoE MPQ · W1/W2/W3 A16 (per-expert mixed, ~2.0-2.75 avg bits/expert) · MoE Quant · 2026-04

<img src="../assets/diagrams/expert-router-norm-mpq.svg" width="640" alt="Router-Norm MoE MPQ diagram">
<p><em>Router-norm MoE MPQ: rank experts by router L2-norm change (small change -> more bits), reorder high-variance experts up, then assign two/three-level per-expert bit-widths.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[MoE model\nmany experts] --> B[Analyze per-expert\nactivation frequency]
    B --> C[Assign bit-width\nby importance]
    C --> D[High-freq experts:\nhigher precision]
    D --> E[Low-freq experts:\nlower precision]
    E --> F[Mixed-precision\nMoE inference]
```

> This method assigns each expert in a Mixture-of-Experts model its own weight bit-width before deployment, rather than quantizing every expert to the same precision. The ranking signal is how much an expert's router vector changes (in L2 norm) between the initial and fine-tuned model: experts whose routers barely move learn rare-but-important features and produce weak activations, so the model's output is most sensitive to quantizing them, and they get more bits. Experts whose routers shift a lot can be pushed to very low precision. A second pass promotes any expert with unusually large per-neuron weight variance to higher precision so it does not inject too much quantization noise. Because the allocation only requires sorting router norms, it adds almost no compute, and for pretrained models the final router norm can stand in for the change in norm so no fine-tuning is needed.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **ICLR 2026** |
| Paper | [2604.06515](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.06515) |
| Code | [nowazrabbani/moe_quantization](https://github.com/nowazrabbani/moe_quantization) |
| Precision | W1/W2/W3 A16 (per-expert mixed, ~2.0-2.75 avg bits/expert) |
| Granularity | per-expert mixed bit-width (per-column bin size within an expert) |
| Calibration | none for bit allocation (router norms only); underlying PTQ (HQQ/GPTQ) may use calibration |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | experts with large max intra-neuron variance promoted to higher precision (ζ=3 threshold) |
| Hardware target | GPU (single A5000 48GB used for quantized inference) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | ~2.7 pt avg accuracy drop on Mixtral-8x7B at ~2.75 avg bits/expert (70.01% vs 72.72% FP, 8 zero-shot tasks); beats PMQ at equal bits |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) · [hqq](#hqq) · [moqe](#moqe) |
| Related | [mc-moe](#mc-moe) · [awq](#awq) · [moqe](#moqe) |

**How it works:**

For each expert s, define the change in router L2 norm Lambda_s = |w_s^(T)| - |w_s^(0)| and rank experts in ascending order, with smaller-change experts placed higher (more bits). The paper proves on a two-layer MoE classification model that experts learning less-prevalent tokens undergo smaller router-norm changes, generate weaker activations, and dominate generalization sensitivity, so quantizing them to b_h while dropping others to b_l preserves full-precision generalization while saving log2((1-alpha)/alpha) bits per low-precision expert. The ordering is then adjusted by max intra-neuron variance MaxVar_s: any lower- ranked expert with variance at least zeta=3 times that of a higher-ranked one is promoted, fixing rare high-noise experts (about 11/256 on Mixtral). Bits are assigned two-level (top kappa fraction to b_h, rest to b_l) or three-level to hit a target average, and the actual quantization is performed with an off-the-shelf PTQ backend (HQQ for Switch Transformer, GPTQ for Mixtral); non-expert weights stay at 8 bits (Switch) or 3 bits (Mixtral).

---

<a id="mc-moe"></a>
### MC-MoE · W2/W4 A16 (per-expert mixed) · MoE Quant · 2024-08

<img src="../assets/diagrams/mc-moe.svg" width="640" alt="MC-MoE diagram">
<p><em>MC-MoE: prune low-importance experts first, then mixed-precision quantize remaining experts.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[MoE experts] --> B[Compute per-expert\nquantization sensitivity]
    B --> C[Rank experts\nby sensitivity score]
    C --> D[High sensitivity:\nassign more bits]
    D --> E[Low sensitivity:\nassign fewer bits]
    E --> F[Mixed-precision\nbit allocation per expert]
    F --> G[Better accuracy\nthan uniform MoE quant]
```

> MC-MoE (Mixture Compressor for MoE) combines expert pruning with quantization to achieve higher compression of MoE models than quantization alone. Experts are first analyzed for their contribution to model outputs (using activation frequency and Hessian-based sensitivity). Low-importance experts are fully pruned (removed). The remaining experts are quantized with bit-widths proportional to their importance. This co-design of pruning and quantization gives a better quality-vs-size tradeoff than either technique alone for MoE models.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2408.11813](https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.11813) |
| Precision | W2/W4 A16 (per-expert mixed) |
| Granularity | per-expert |
| Calibration | calibration set |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | expert pruning + quantization co-design |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | better than MoQE on Mixtral-8x7B at same compression ratio |
| Builds on | [moqe](#moqe) · [gptq](#gptq) |
| Related | [moqe](#moqe) · [gptq](#gptq) · [awq](#awq) |

**How it works:**

Two-stage compression: (1) Expert pruning - rank experts by score = activation_frequency × reconstruction_sensitivity. Prune bottom-k% of experts entirely (replacing them with the average of nearby experts for output stability). (2) Mixed-precision quantization - apply GPTQ or AWQ to remaining experts with bit-width proportional to importance rank. Shared experts (if present, as in DeepSeek-MoE) are always kept at higher precision. The pruning step reduces the total parameter count before quantization, allowing the remaining budget to be concentrated on the most important experts.

---

<a id="moqe"></a>
### MoQE · W2/W4 A16 (expert-specific) · MoE Quant · 2023-10

<img src="../assets/diagrams/moqe.svg" width="640" alt="MoQE diagram">
<p><em>MoQE: frequency × sensitivity score assigns bit-widths per expert in MoE models.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[MoE model] --> B[Profile expert\nactivation frequency]
    B --> C{Expert usage\nfrequency}
    C -->|high frequency| D[Keep in INT8\nor FP16]
    C -->|low frequency| E[Quantize to INT4\nor INT2]
    D --> F[Mixed-precision\nMoE inference]
    E --> F
    F --> G[Better than uniform\nquantization of all experts]
```

> MoQE (Mixture of Quantization Experts) addresses the unique quantization challenge of Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models: the MoE architecture has many experts (sub-networks), but only a few activate per token. Calibration data therefore sees each expert only a small fraction of the time, making Hessian estimation unreliable. MoQE assigns different bit-widths to different experts based on their activation frequency and importance - frequently-activated experts get more bits, rarely-activated experts can tolerate lower precision. This expert-aware mixed-precision scheme beats uniform quantization of all experts at the same average bit-width.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2310.02410](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.02410) |
| Precision | W2/W4 A16 (expert-specific) |
| Granularity | per-expert, mixed bit-widths |
| Calibration | calibration set for expert importance |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | more important experts get higher precision |
| Hardware target | GPU |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | < 0.5 ppl vs uniform expert quantization at same average bits |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) |
| Related | [gptq](#gptq) · [awq](#awq) · [deepseek-fp8](#deepseek-fp8) |

**How it works:**

MoQE assigns bit-widths per expert based on two factors: (1) activation frequency - how often each expert is routed to across the calibration set (can be computed cheaply from the router logits); (2) quantization sensitivity - the reconstruction error of each expert when quantized (requires a calibration forward pass through each expert). A budget-constrained allocation then assigns higher bits to experts with high frequency × sensitivity and lower bits to others, maintaining an average bit-width target. The router weights and non-expert transformer components (attention, LayerNorm) remain at FP16.

---

---

## Systems, Kernels & Runtimes

Software that implements quantization methods in practice: inference engines, CUDA kernels, quantization toolkits. These are not novel algorithms but are the infrastructure through which the algorithms ship. One card per runtime, listing which methods it supports.

<a id="tilefuse"></a>
### TileFuse · W4A16, W8A16 · Systems · 2026-06

<img src="../assets/diagrams/tilefuse.svg" width="640" alt="TileFuse diagram">
<p><em>TileFuse microkernel: pre-tiled INT4/INT8 weights + packed scales/zero-points stream into a fused unpack -> dequantize(BF16) -> GEMM/GEMV pipeline on the XDNA2 4x8 AIE array.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Quantized model\nweights on disk] --> B[Load and parse\nquant metadata]
    B --> C[Select kernel\nfor target hardware]
    C --> D[Fused dequant +\nGEMM kernel]
    D --> E[Paged or continuous\nbatching]
    E --> F[Efficient serving\nW4A16, W8A16]
```

> TileFuse is a kernel library that lets already-quantized LLMs (in common AWQ-style W4A16 and W8A16 formats from Hugging Face) run efficiently on AMD's XDNA2 NPUs found in Ryzen AI laptop chips. Instead of unpacking the low-bit weights, dequantizing them, and then doing the matrix multiply as separate passes that bounce data through memory, TileFuse merges all three steps into a single on-chip kernel so the spatial AIE array stays busy. It also lays out the weight tiles ahead of time in an interleaved column-major order and packs the scales and zero-points alongside them, and it redesigns the GEMV dataflow to use the full 4x8 array for the memory-bound decode phase. The result is faster, lower-energy on-device inference without changing the quantized model itself.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2606.11357](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11357) |
| Code | [feature/update-mix-mm-int4-verification](https://github.com/glassescrab/mlir-aie/tree/feature/update-mix-mm-int4-verification) |
| Precision | W4A16, W8A16 |
| Granularity | W4A16 asymmetric per-group (g=128); W8A16 symmetric per-channel; dequantized to BF16 |
| Calibration | n/a (inference kernel library; consumes pre-quantized off-the-shelf models) |
| Symmetric? | mixed |
| Outlier handling | n/a (kernel/runtime; relies on the upstream AWQ-style quantization for accuracy) |
| Hardware target | AMD XDNA2 NPUs in Ryzen AI SoCs (Ryzen AI 7 350 Krackan Point, Ryzen AI 9 HX 370 Strix Point); uses the IRON/MLIR-AIE programming stack and the 4x8 AIE array |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | n/a - kernel/runtime, not a quantization algorithm; no accuracy change reported. Performance: up to 2.0x lower prefill latency and >64.6% lower energy vs the iGPU baseline; up to 121.6% (GEMM) and 281% (GEMV) kernel throughput gains over full-precision on AMD NPU, evaluated on Llama3-8B, Gemma-2B, and Qwen2.5-3B. |
| Builds on | [awq](#awq) · [marlin](#marlin) |
| Related | [awq](#awq) · [marlin](#marlin) · [fp6-llm](#fp6-llm) · [qserve](#qserve) |

**How it works:**

TileFuse fuses weight unpacking (bit-mask/shift to extract 4-bit fields), dequantization (integer-to-BF16 using per-group/per-channel scale and zero-point metadata), and the GEMM/GEMV matmul into one microkernel pipeline running on the XDNA2 spatial array, keeping dequantized weights in compute-core buffers for reuse. Weights are pre-tiled offline in an interleaved column-major layout (supporting dimensions up to 32K) with scales and zero- points pre-packed alongside the tiles, minimizing on-device data movement. A redesigned GEMV dataflow maps the memory-bound decode phase across the full 4x8 AIE array to maximize utilization. It targets standard AWQ-format models via AMD's open IRON/MLIR-AIE stack rather than requiring a proprietary NPU quantization scheme.

---

<a id="llamaweb"></a>
### LlamaWeb · W2/W4/W8 A16 (q2_k, q4_k_m, q8_0) and f16; supports 21 GGUF quantization formats · Systems · 2026-05

<img src="../assets/diagrams/llamaweb.svg" width="640" alt="LlamaWeb diagram">
<p><em>LlamaWeb: WebGPU backend for llama.cpp with static memory planning, format-templated WGSL kernels, and per-device tunable kernel parameters.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Quantized model\nweights on disk] --> B[Load and parse\nquant metadata]
    B --> C[Select kernel\nfor target hardware]
    C --> D[Fused dequant +\nGEMM kernel]
    D --> E[Paged or continuous\nbatching]
    E --> F[Efficient serving\nW2/W4/W8 A16 q2_k, q4_k_m, q8_0 and f16; supports 21 GGUF quantization formats]
```

> LlamaWeb is a WebGPU backend for llama.cpp that runs quantized language models entirely inside a web browser across GPUs from many different vendors. It cuts memory use by allocating all the GPU buffers it needs once at startup (instead of creating buffers on the fly) and by loading model weights efficiently, and it stays fast across very different hardware by generating its compute shaders from templates and auto-tuning parameters like workgroup and tile sizes per device. The same kernel templates are instantiated for each weight format, so the engine can read many GGUF quantization layouts (2-bit through 8-bit K-quants plus f16) and be extended to new ones without hand-writing a separate kernel each time. In browser benchmarks it decodes tokens faster and uses less memory than WebLLM and Transformers.js, though its prefill phase is slower than WebLLM's.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2605.20706](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20706) |
| Blog / post | [link](https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/publication/llamas-on-the-web-memory-efficient-performance-portable-and-multi-precision-llm-inference-with-webgpu/) |
| Precision | W2/W4/W8 A16 (q2_k, q4_k_m, q8_0) and f16; supports 21 GGUF quantization formats |
| Granularity | per-block (GGUF K-quant blocks); inherits llama.cpp/GGUF block layouts |
| Calibration | none (runtime; consumes pre-quantized GGUF weights) |
| Symmetric? | mixed (depends on GGUF format) |
| Outlier handling | n/a (runtime backend; relies on the chosen GGUF quantization format) |
| Hardware target | WebGPU in the browser across vendors (tested on 16 devices from 8 GPU vendors) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | n/a (inference runtime, no extra accuracy loss); 54% higher decode throughput than WebLLM and 69% higher than Transformers.js, and ~49% lower memory than WebLLM, measured on 10 models including Llama3.2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B, Gemma3-270M, and SmolLM3-3B |
| Builds on | [llama-cpp](#llama-cpp) · [gguf-kquants](#gguf-kquants) |
| Related | [mlc-llm](#mlc-llm) · [gguf-iquants](#gguf-iquants) |

**How it works:**

LlamaWeb adds a WebGPU device backend to llama.cpp built around three pieces: (1) static memory planning, where the full set of GPU buffers required to run a model is allocated up front into a single large buffer with fixed parameter slots, avoiding costly dynamic WebGPU buffer creation and reducing peak memory; (2) templated WGSL compute kernels parameterized over the weight format, so dequantization-plus-matmul shaders for the 23 supported formats are generated from shared templates rather than written individually; and (3) a tunable kernel library that exposes device-specific knobs (workgroup sizes, shared-memory tile sizes) whose best values are found by exhaustively sweeping thousands of configurations across many GPUs, giving performance portability across heterogeneous browser hardware.

---

<a id="sglang-quant"></a>
### SGLang (Quantization) · W4A16 (GPTQ/AWQ/Marlin), W8A8 (FP8), W4A8 (Marlin W4A8) · Systems · 2024-12

<img src="../assets/diagrams/sglang-quant.svg" width="640" alt="SGLang (Quantization) diagram">
<p><em>SGLang: RadixAttention prefix KV cache reuse + Marlin/FP8/AWQ quantization backends.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[vLLM engine] --> B{Quant method}
    B -->|awq| C[AutoAWQ W4A16]
    B -->|gptq| D[ExLlamaV2 W4A16]
    B -->|fp8| E[Native FP8\nH100 tensor core]
    B -->|marlin| F[Marlin INT4\nhigh throughput]
    C --> G[PagedAttention\ncontinuous batching]
    D --> G
    E --> G
    F --> G
```

> SGLang is a high-performance LLM serving framework built around RadixAttention for efficient prefix caching and structured generation. Its quantization support mirrors vLLM: native GPTQ, AWQ, Marlin, FP8, and INT8 backends, selected automatically by model format. SGLang focuses on structured generation efficiency (JSON, regex, code) and achieves throughput competitive with or exceeding vLLM at comparable quantization quality. It integrates Marlin for W4A16 decoding and CUTLASS-based FP8 kernels for H100 prefill-dominated workloads.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **OSDI 2024** |
| Paper | [2312.07104](https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.07104) |
| Code | [sgl-project/sglang](https://github.com/sgl-project/sglang) |
| Precision | W4A16 (GPTQ/AWQ/Marlin), W8A8 (FP8), W4A8 (Marlin W4A8) |
| Granularity | per-group (varies by backend) |
| Calibration | depends on method |
| Symmetric? | varies |
| Outlier handling | method-dependent |
| Hardware target | NVIDIA GPU (H100/A100/H200); AMD GPU (partial) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | n/a (serving runtime) |
| Builds on | [marlin](#marlin) · [gptq](#gptq) · [awq](#awq) |
| Related | [vllm-quant](#vllm-quant) · [autogptq](#autogptq) · [marlin](#marlin) |

**How it works:**

SGLang's quantization stack provides the same kernel backends as vLLM (Marlin, AWQ, GPTQ, FP8) within its RadixAttention serving architecture. The key differentiation is RadixAttention: a prefix tree structure that reuses KV cache across requests with shared prefixes, dramatically reducing prefill cost for applications with common system prompts. Quantization interacts with RadixAttention by compressing the shared KV cache, multiplying the memory savings. SGLang also includes overlap scheduling (GPU/CPU computation overlap) and DP attention for multi-node serving.

<a id="marlin"></a>
### Marlin · W4A16 (INT4), W4A8 (INT4 weight, FP8 activation) · Systems · 2024-08

<img src="../assets/diagrams/marlin.svg" width="640" alt="Marlin diagram">
<p><em>Marlin warp layout: prefetch → dequantize → tensor-core accumulate, all pipelined.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[INT4 weights\nGPTQ format] --> B[Permute for\nmemory coalescing]
    B --> C[Marlin kernel:\nfuse dequant + GEMM]
    C --> D[128-thread warps\npipeline async loads]
    D --> E[FP16 GEMM throughput\nnear theoretical peak]
    E --> F[2.4x faster than\ntorch W4A16 baseline]
```

> Marlin is a high-performance CUDA kernel library for W4A16 and W4A8 grouped-quantized matrix multiplication on NVIDIA GPUs. For a transformer layer serving a single token (decode phase), the bottleneck is memory bandwidth: loading the 4-bit weight matrix. Marlin achieves close to the theoretical memory bandwidth limit of A100/H100 by carefully designing warp layouts, prefetching patterns, and the FP16 dequantization pipeline to overlap computation with memory loads. It is integrated into vLLM, TRT-LLM, and SGLang.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2408.11743](https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.11743) |
| Code | [IST-DASLab/marlin](https://github.com/IST-DASLab/marlin) |
| Precision | W4A16 (INT4), W4A8 (INT4 weight, FP8 activation) |
| Granularity | per-group, g=128 |
| Calibration | n/a (inference kernel) |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | n/a |
| Hardware target | NVIDIA A100/H100 (Ampere/Hopper); achieves near-theoretical memory bandwidth |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | n/a - kernel, not algorithm |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) · [awq](#awq) |
| Related | [exl2](#exl2) · [awq](#awq) · [gptq](#gptq) |

**How it works:**

Marlin's kernel decomposes the matmul into tiles processed by persistent warps. Each warp prefetches the next tile of INT4 weights from global memory while the previous tile's FP16 dequantization and tensor-core accumulation runs in parallel. The dequantization (unpack INT4 → FP16 using the per-group scale) is fused into the memory pipeline so it adds no latency. A careful warp layout avoids shared-memory bank conflicts. The result is ~3.7× speedup over FP16 matmul for batch=1 decode on A100, matching the theoretical 4× reduction in memory traffic.

---

<a id="flute"></a>
### FLUTE · W3/W4 A16 · Systems · 2024-07

<img src="../assets/diagrams/flute.svg" width="640" alt="FLUTE diagram">
<p><em>FLUTE: learned per-group codebook with fused lookup-table decode inside the GEMM kernel.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Weight group\ng elements] --> B[k-means cluster\ninto 2^b centroids]
    B --> C[Store b-bit indices\nper element]
    C --> D[FLUTE CUDA kernel:\nlookup indices → FP16]
    D --> E[Fuse decode +\nGEMM accumulation]
    E --> F[Single kernel\nno separate dequant]
    F --> G[W3/W4 throughput\nnear FP16 GEMM]
```

> FLUTE represents quantized weights as small learned lookup tables (codebooks) per group, rather than uniform scalar quantization. A custom CUDA kernel fuses the lookup-table decode step with the GEMM operation, eliminating explicit dequantization overhead. Supports 2-to-4-bit weight-only quantization, achieves accuracy competitive with GPTQ, and delivers faster inference through the fused kernel design.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **NeurIPS 2024** |
| Paper | [2407.10960](https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.10960) |
| Code | [HanGuo97/flute](https://github.com/HanGuo97/flute) |
| Precision | W3/W4 A16 |
| Granularity | per-group, learned codebook |
| Calibration | calibration set for codebook learning |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | non-uniform codebook levels fit to weight distribution |
| Hardware target | GPU; custom CUDA LUT kernel fused with GEMM |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | comparable to GPTQ; faster inference due to fused kernel |
| Builds on | [gptq](#gptq) · [gguf-kquants](#gguf-kquants) |
| Related | [gptq](#gptq) · [awq](#awq) · [marlin](#marlin) · [exl2](#exl2) |

**How it works:**

For each weight group, FLUTE learns a small codebook (e.g. 16 entries for 4-bit) that minimizes reconstruction error. At inference, the FLUTE CUDA kernel reads 4-bit indices, performs table lookups to recover FP16 values, and immediately feeds them into GEMM accumulation in a single fused kernel. This eliminates the separate dequant pass. Non-uniform codebook levels (like k-means or NF4 bins) are naturally supported without extra cost, giving better accuracy than uniform quantization at the same bit-width.

---

<a id="exllamav2"></a>
### ExLlamaV2 · W2–W8 A16 (EXL2 mixed per-row format) · Systems · 2023-10

<img src="../assets/diagrams/exllamav2.svg" width="640" alt="ExLlamaV2 diagram">
<p><em>ExLlamaV2: custom CUDA kernels for mixed-precision EXL2 format + paged KV + speculative decode.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[EXL2 quantized\nmodel file] --> B[Mixed-precision\nper-layer bit assignment]
    B --> C[ExLlamaV2 kernel:\nfused W4A16 decode]
    C --> D[Paged attention\ncache management]
    D --> E[Speculative decoding\noptional]
    E --> F[Highest single-GPU\ngeneration throughput]
```

> ExLlamaV2 is the fastest open-source inference engine for GPTQ/EXL2 quantized models on NVIDIA GPUs. Its EXL2 format enables arbitrary target bit-widths (e.g. 3.0, 4.25, 5.1 bpw) by assigning different quantization precisions per row of each weight matrix based on Hessian importance. Custom CUDA kernels handle heterogeneous row precisions in a single kernel call. ExLlamaV2 also supports paged KV cache, speculative decoding, and continuous batching, making it competitive with production inference engines for single-user local deployment.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | n/a (no paper) |
| Code | [turboderp/exllamav2](https://github.com/turboderp/exllamav2) |
| Precision | W2–W8 A16 (EXL2 mixed per-row format) |
| Granularity | per-row mixed precision; per-group within rows |
| Calibration | calibration dataset for Hessian-based row importance |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | EXL2: per-row bit allocation proportional to Hessian importance |
| Hardware target | NVIDIA GPU (Ada Lovelace, Ampere, Hopper); Llama-family models |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | 3.0 bpw EXL2 outperforms GPTQ W4A16 on most benchmarks |
| Builds on | [exl2](#exl2) · [gptq](#gptq) |
| Related | [autogptq](#autogptq) · [llama-cpp](#llama-cpp) · [marlin](#marlin) |

**How it works:**

ExLlamaV2's core innovation is the EXL2 quantization format (detailed in the EXL2 entry) plus highly optimized CUDA kernels that process heterogeneous row precisions efficiently. The engine also implements: continuous batching for serving multiple users; paged attention for efficient KV cache management; speculative decoding with a small draft model for 2–3× throughput improvement; and a Python/C++ server mode. For GPTQ compatibility, ExLlamaV2 includes an ExLlama2 GPTQ backend that outperforms AutoGPTQ's default Triton backend by ~2× on A100 via better kernel design.

<a id="autoawq"></a>
### AutoAWQ · W4A16 (AWQ) · Systems · 2023-10

<img src="../assets/diagrams/autoawq.svg" width="640" alt="AutoAWQ diagram">
<p><em>AutoAWQ: calibrate → scale → quantize → save AWQ model; multi-backend inference.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 model] --> B[Collect activation\nmagnitude stats]
    B --> C[AWQ: per-channel\nscale optimization]
    C --> D[Apply scales, quantize\nweights to INT4]
    D --> E[Save AWQ weights\n+ metadata]
    E --> F[Fast inference:\ngemm_a16w4 or Marlin kernel]
```

> AutoAWQ is the standard Python package for applying AWQ quantization to Hugging Face models. Like AutoGPTQ for GPTQ, AutoAWQ provides a simple API for quantizing any supported LLM architecture to W4A16 AWQ format, which can then be uploaded to Hugging Face Hub or used directly for inference. AWQ-quantized models typically have slightly better quality than GPTQ at the same group size due to the activation-guided scaling. AutoAWQ is integrated with vLLM, TGI, and TRT-LLM for deployment.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | n/a (no paper) |
| Code | [casper-hansen/AutoAWQ](https://github.com/casper-hansen/AutoAWQ) |
| Precision | W4A16 (AWQ) |
| Granularity | per-group, g=128 |
| Calibration | small activation sample |
| Symmetric? | asymmetric |
| Outlier handling | AWQ activation-magnitude-guided scaling |
| Hardware target | NVIDIA GPU; Exllama/Marlin backends |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |
| Typical degradation | n/a (runtime wrapper for AWQ) |
| Builds on | [awq](#awq) |
| Related | [autogptq](#autogptq) · [llama-cpp](#llama-cpp) · [vllm-quant](#vllm-quant) |

**How it works:**

AutoAWQ wraps the AWQ quantization algorithm: (1) collect per-channel activation statistics from the calibration set; (2) compute optimal per-channel scales (the α parameter in AWQ); (3) apply the scale transformation to weights and absorb the inverse into preceding layers; (4) quantize scaled weights with RTN at group size 128. The resulting model is saved in AWQ format with per-group scale factors and zero points stored in FP16. At inference, AutoAWQ uses custom CUDA kernels (inherited from TinyChat) or the Marlin backend for maximum throughput.

<a id="vllm-quant"></a>
### vLLM (Quantization) · W4A16 (GPTQ/AWQ/Marlin), W8A8 (FP8), W4A8 (Marlin) · Systems · 2023-06

<img src="../assets/diagrams/vllm-quant.svg" width="640" alt="vLLM (Quantization) diagram">
<p><em>vLLM quantization stack: PagedAttention + pluggable W4/W8/FP8 kernels for production serving.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[vLLM engine] --> B{Quant method}
    B -->|awq| C[AutoAWQ W4A16]
    B -->|gptq| D[ExLlamaV2 W4A16]
    B -->|fp8| E[Native FP8\nH100 tensor core]
    B -->|marlin| F[Marlin INT4\nhigh throughput]
    C --> G[PagedAttention\ncontinuous batching]
    D --> G
    E --> G
    F --> G
```

> vLLM is the dominant open-source LLM serving framework for production deployment, built around PagedAttention for efficient KV cache management. Its quantization support includes native GPTQ (Triton kernel), AWQ (custom CUDA), Marlin (high-throughput W4A16), FP8 (H100), GGUF (partial), and INT8. vLLM's quantization handling is transparent to the user - load a pre-quantized model and vLLM selects the appropriate kernel. The Marlin backend (IST-DASLab) gives near-theoretical memory bandwidth for W4A16 decoding at batch=1, while FP8 on H100 gives compute-bound throughput for large batches.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | **SOSP 2023** |
| Paper | [2309.06180](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.06180) |
| Code | [vllm-project/vllm](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm) |
| Precision | W4A16 (GPTQ/AWQ/Marlin), W8A8 (FP8), W4A8 (Marlin) |
| Granularity | per-group (varies by backend) |
| Calibration | depends on method (none for AWQ loaded, needed for GPTQ) |
| Symmetric? | varies |
| Outlier handling | method-dependent (Marlin/AWQ/GPTQ backends) |
| Hardware target | NVIDIA GPU (A100/H100); AMD GPU (partial); CPU (partial) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | n/a (runtime - quality depends on quantization method used) |
| Builds on | [marlin](#marlin) · [gptq](#gptq) · [awq](#awq) · [smoothquant](#smoothquant) |
| Related | [autogptq](#autogptq) · [autoawq](#autoawq) · [marlin](#marlin) · [llama-cpp](#llama-cpp) |

**How it works:**

vLLM's quantization stack is a collection of backend kernels selected by model format: GPTQ → Triton kernel (or Exllama2 backend); AWQ → custom fused CUDA; W4A16 → Marlin; FP8 → H100 FP8 Tensor Core kernel; INT8 SmoothQuant → INT8 GEMM. PagedAttention manages KV cache in fixed-size 16-token blocks, enabling non-contiguous KV allocation and serving many concurrent requests without memory fragmentation. Continuous batching iterates over requests at the token level, maximizing GPU utilization. All quantization kernels plug into this serving infrastructure.

<a id="mlc-llm"></a>
### MLC-LLM · W4A16 (AWQ-style), W8A8, FP16 · Systems · 2023-06

<img src="../assets/diagrams/mlc-llm.svg" width="640" alt="MLC-LLM diagram">
<p><em>MLC-LLM: TVM compilation to CUDA/Metal/Vulkan/WebGPU with fused W4A16 dequantization kernels.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Model weights\nFP16 or quantized] --> B[TVM compilation\ntarget-specific tuning]
    B --> C{Target platform}
    C -->|CUDA| D[GPU GEMM kernel]
    C -->|Metal| E[Apple GPU kernel]
    C -->|WebGPU| F[Browser inference]
    C -->|x86| G[CPU SIMD kernel]
    D --> H[Universal LLM\nruntime MLC-LLM]
    E --> H
    F --> H
    G --> H
```

> MLC-LLM is a machine learning compilation framework that enables LLM inference on any hardware with near-native performance, including Apple Silicon (Metal), NVIDIA GPUs (CUDA), AMD GPUs (Vulkan), WebGPU (browser), iOS, and Android. It uses the Apache TVM compiler to generate optimized kernels for each target from a unified model specification. MLC-LLM supports AWQ-style W4A16 quantization, INT8, and FP16, with automatic kernel generation for each backend. This makes it the primary runtime for cross-platform quantized LLM deployment including mobile and browser environments.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | n/a (no paper) |
| Code | [mlc-ai/mlc-llm](https://github.com/mlc-ai/mlc-llm) |
| Precision | W4A16 (AWQ-style), W8A8, FP16 |
| Granularity | per-group |
| Calibration | varies by method |
| Symmetric? | varies |
| Outlier handling | method-dependent |
| Hardware target | CUDA, Metal (Apple Silicon), Vulkan, WebGPU, iOS, Android |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | n/a (cross-platform runtime) |
| Builds on | [awq](#awq) |
| Related | [llama-cpp](#llama-cpp) · [vllm-quant](#vllm-quant) · [autoawq](#autoawq) |

**How it works:**

MLC-LLM compiles each model to a target-specific shared library via TVM. The compilation pipeline: (1) model spec in Relax IR (TVM's high-level IR); (2) quantization applied at the IR level; (3) auto-tuning of kernel schedules per target hardware; (4) code generation to CUDA PTX / Metal Shading Language / GLSL (Vulkan) / JS (WebGPU). Quantized weights (W4A16 AWQ-compatible) are stored in a compact binary format and loaded at runtime. The dequantization is fused into the matmul kernel via TVM's operator fusion. The same model spec runs on all backends, with only the kernels differing per platform.

<a id="llama-cpp"></a>
### llama.cpp (GGUF) · W2/W3/W4/W5/W6/W8 A16 (GGUF K-quants and I-quants) · Systems · 2023-03

<img src="../assets/diagrams/llama-cpp.svg" width="640" alt="llama.cpp (GGUF) diagram">
<p><em>llama.cpp: portable C++ runtime with GGUF quantization for CPU/GPU/any-hardware inference.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[GGUF file] --> B[Load quantized\nweights + metadata]
    B --> C{Quant type}
    C -->|Q4_K_M| D[K-quant:\nImportance-weighted\ngroup quant]
    C -->|IQ4_XS| E[I-quant:\nLattice codebook\nactivation-aware]
    D --> F[CPU or GPU\nmetal / CUDA / Vulkan]
    E --> F
    F --> G[Cross-platform\nLLM inference]
```

> llama.cpp is the most widely used open-source LLM inference engine for consumer hardware. Written in C++, it supports CPU, GPU (CUDA/Metal/Vulkan), and heterogeneous inference. Its GGUF quantization format (K-quants: Q2_K through Q6_K; I-quants: IQ1_S through IQ4_NL) covers 2–8 bit precision with hierarchical or lattice-based quantization. GGUF models are the standard format for local LLM deployment: most model releases on Hugging Face include GGUF variants. The engine handles all major model architectures (LLaMA, Mistral, Phi, Gemma, Qwen, etc.) through a unified C/C++ implementation.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | n/a (no paper) |
| Code | [ggerganov/llama.cpp](https://github.com/ggerganov/llama.cpp) |
| Precision | W2/W3/W4/W5/W6/W8 A16 (GGUF K-quants and I-quants) |
| Granularity | hierarchical two-level (K-quants) or lattice (I-quants) |
| Calibration | none (K-quants); imatrix optional (I-quants) |
| Symmetric? | mixed |
| Outlier handling | K-quants: hierarchical scaling; I-quants: importance-weighted lattice VQ |
| Hardware target | CPU (x86, ARM), GPU (CUDA, Metal, Vulkan), any hardware |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | Q4_K_M: best CPU quality/speed at 4-bit; IQ4_NL slightly better |
| Builds on | [gguf-kquants](#gguf-kquants) · [gguf-iquants](#gguf-iquants) |
| Related | [autogptq](#autogptq) · [exllamav2](#exllamav2) · [mlc-llm](#mlc-llm) |

**How it works:**

llama.cpp implements transformer inference from scratch in C++ with portable backends. Key design choices: (1) row-major packed GGUF format for memory-efficient quantized storage; (2) architecture-specific SIMD optimizations (AVX2, NEON, AMX) for CPU performance; (3) custom Metal/CUDA/Vulkan compute kernels for GPU acceleration; (4) split inference across CPU and GPU for models that don't fit in VRAM; (5) speculative decoding support. The GGUF quantization formats (K-quants, I-quants) are designed specifically for CPU inference where memory bandwidth is the bottleneck.

<a id="autogptq"></a>
### AutoGPTQ · W4/W3/W2 A16 (GPTQ) · Systems · 2023-01

<img src="../assets/diagrams/autogptq.svg" width="640" alt="AutoGPTQ diagram">
<p><em>AutoGPTQ: one-API GPTQ quantization pipeline with multi-backend inference support.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[FP16 model] --> B[Load calibration\ndataset]

    F -->|CUDA| I[Standard W4A16]
```

> AutoGPTQ is the de-facto standard Python package for applying GPTQ quantization to any Hugging Face model and saving/loading the quantized result. It wraps the GPTQ algorithm in an easy-to-use API that handles model loading, layer-by-layer quantization, and saving in a standardized format compatible with Hugging Face Hub. AutoGPTQ supports multiple inference backends (Triton, CUDA, Exllama, Marlin) and handles the model architecture discovery automatically. The vast majority of GPTQ-quantized open models on Hugging Face Hub were created with AutoGPTQ.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|

| Symmetric? | asymmetric |

| Hardware target | NVIDIA GPU; ExLlamaV2 and Marlin kernels optional |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | yes |

**How it works:**

AutoGPTQ provides: (1) a `quantize()` API that iterates over transformer blocks, applies the GPTQ algorithm per linear layer, and saves quantized weights in a standardized format; (2) loading quantized models with `from_quantized()` which reconstructs the quantized model for inference; (3) inference backend selection (Triton for flexibility, ExLlama for speed, Marlin for maximum A100/H100 throughput); (4) compatibility with Hugging Face Transformers `AutoModelForCausalLM` API. Supported formats: W4, W3, W2 with variable group sizes. Supported architectures: LLaMA, Mistral, Falcon, GPT-J, and most major open-source LLM families.

<a id="bitsandbytes"></a>
### bitsandbytes · W8A8 (LLM.int8()); W4A16 (NF4/FP4) · Systems · 2022-08

<img src="../assets/diagrams/bitsandbytes.svg" width="640" alt="bitsandbytes diagram">
<p><em>bitsandbytes: LLM.int8() and NF4 CUDA kernels for PyTorch quantized inference and fine-tuning.</em></p>

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[Model load] --> B{dtype param}
    B -->|load_in_8bit| C[LLM.int8\nmixed-precision]

```

> bitsandbytes is the foundational Python library for efficient quantized inference and fine-tuning in PyTorch. It provides CUDA kernels for LLM.int8() (W8A8 with outlier decomposition), NF4/FP4 (W4A16, used in QLoRA), and supports both inference and quantization-aware fine-tuning. The library is installed by default with Hugging Face Transformers and enables loading any AutoModelForCausalLM in 4-bit or 8-bit with a single `load_in_4bit=True` flag. It is the most widely deployed quantization runtime in the open-source LLM ecosystem.

| Field | Value |
|-------|-------|
| Published at | arXiv (preprint) |
| Paper | [2208.07339](https://arxiv.org/abs/2208.07339) |
| Code | [bitsandbytes-foundation/bitsandbytes](https://github.com/bitsandbytes-foundation/bitsandbytes) |
| Precision | W8A8 (LLM.int8()); W4A16 (NF4/FP4) |
| Granularity | per-channel and per-group; NF4 double-quantization |
| Calibration | none |
| Symmetric? | mixed |
| Outlier handling | LLM.int8() decomposition for 8-bit; NF4 quantile grid for 4-bit |
| Hardware target | NVIDIA GPU (CUDA 11.1+) |
| Training needed? | no |
| Calibration data? | no |
| Typical degradation | n/a (library, quality depends on method used) |
| Builds on | [llm-int8](#llm-int8) · [bitsandbytes-nf4](#bitsandbytes-nf4) |
| Related | [autogptq](#autogptq) · [autoawq](#autoawq) · [llama-cpp](#llama-cpp) |

**How it works:**

bitsandbytes wraps standard PyTorch `nn.Linear` with custom CUDA kernels that handle the mixed-precision compute. For LLM.int8(): outlier column detection + decomposed INT8/FP16 GEMM. For NF4: 4-bit NF4 weight storage with dequantization to BF16 before multiplication (W4→BF16→GEMM, not INT4×FP16 Tensor Core). The library implements per-tensor and per-group quantization, double-quantization of scales, and paged optimizer support for memory-efficient training. Methods supported: LLM.int8(), NF4, FP4, and linear quantization.
